1.
Purity, Mixedness, and Realization

1.1.

Fix a finite field
\mathbf{F}
and an algebraic closure
\bar{\mathbf{F}}
.
Throughout the paper,
we assume that the characteristic of
\mathbf{F}
is large
.
Recall that the Galois group of
\bar{\mathbf{F}}
over
\mathbf{F}
is pro-generated by the automorphism
a\mapsto a^{q}
.
We write
F
for the inverse generator, also known as the geometric Frobenius element.

Fix a root datum
(\Phi,\mathbf{X},\Phi^{\vee},\mathbf{X}^{\vee})
.
Let
G
be the reductive group over
\bar{\mathbf{F}}
defined by the root datum, and let
G_{0}
be its split form over
\mathbf{F}
, so that
G=G_{0}\otimes_{\mathbf{F}}\bar{\mathbf{F}}
.
In some later sections, we will require
G
to be semisimple, not merely reductive.

1.2.

Let
X_{0}
be a
G_{0}
-variety over
\mathbf{F}
, equipped with a fixed
G_{0}
-stable stratification by locally closed subvarieties.
Let
X=X_{0}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{F}}
.
In general, we use a subscript
0
to denote varieties over
\mathbf{F}
, and omit the subscript to denote their pullback to
\bar{\mathbf{F}}
.

Fix a prime
\ell
invertible in
\mathbf{F}
.
We write

(39)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{G}^{b}(X)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{G}^{b}(X,\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}),

(40)

\displaystyle\emph{resp.}\qquad\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0},\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}),

for the bounded derived category of complexes,
resp.
mixed complexes, of
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-sheaves on the lisse-étale site of the stack
[X/G]
,
resp.

[X_{0}/G_{0}]
, with constructible cohomology with respect to the stratification.
Mixed complexes were introduced by Deligne in the non-stacky, étale setting
[
D80
]
; we refer to
[
BBD
, Ch. 5]
for an overview.
We fix a square root
q^{\frac{1}{2}}\in\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}
to define a half-Tate twist
(\frac{1}{2})
.
The
shift-twist

\langle 1\rangle=[1](\frac{1}{2})
is a weight-preserving endofunctor of
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
.
We write

(41)

\displaystyle\xi:\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})\to\mathsf{D}_{G}^{b}(X)

for the pullback functor.

In
[
LO08
]
, Laszlo–Olsson extended the Grothendieck–Verdier yoga of the six operations to lisse-étale sheaves on Artin stacks locally of finite type over a finite-dimensional affine excellent base.
In
[
LO09
]
, they did the same for the theory of
t
-structures and perverse sheaves developed in
[
BBD
, Ch. 1-2]
.
In
[
Su
]
, S. Sun extended the theory of mixed complexes to this setting, under the hypothesis that
the stabilizers of the stack are affine algebraic groups
.
For all choices of
X_{0}
that we will consider, this hypothesis will hold for
[X_{0}/G_{0}]
and
[X/G]
.
Sun’s work covers all of the results from
[
BBD
, Ch. 5]
that we need.

Throughout this paper, all maps between
G_{0}
-varieties or
G
-varieties will be equivariant and compatible with the relevant stratifications.
The six operations on
\mathsf{D}_{G}^{b}
and
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}
are always assumed to be derived:
That is, we will drop
\mathrm{R}
and
\mathrm{L}
from our notation for derived functors.

Remark 1.2.1
.

The categories
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
and
\mathsf{D}_{G}^{b}(X)
need not be closed under internal Hom.
But for any
K,L\in\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
, the object
\underline{\Hom}(K,L)
does exist in the
bounded-below
derived category of mixed complexes over
[X_{0}/G_{0}]
, and similarly with
\mathsf{D}_{G}^{b}(X)
in place of
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})

[
LO08
]
.
We will not comment further on this issue since it will not raise problems, except to note that the hypercohomology of
\underline{\Hom}(K,L)
will always be bounded below.

Let
\mathit{pt}_{0}=\Spec\mathbf{F}
, and let
a:X_{0}\to\mathit{pt}_{0}
be the structure map.
For all objects
K,L\in\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
, we write

(42)

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}^{n}(K,L)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}^{n}(\xi a_{\ast}\underline{\Hom}(K,L)).

Similarly, we write
\underline{\End}^{n}(K)=\underline{\Hom}^{n}(K,K)
.
We can view
a_{\ast}\underline{\Hom}(K,L)
as a sheaf over
\mathit{pt}_{0}
, so its pullback along
\xi
can be viewed as a sheaf over
\mathit{pt}
endowed with an
F
-equivariant structure.
In particular,
\underline{\Hom}^{n}(K,L)
is the vector space
\Hom^{n}(\xi K,\xi L)
endowed with an
F
-action.
The
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[F]
-module formed by
\underline{\Hom}^{\ast}(K,L)
is related to the vector space
\Hom^{\ast}(K,L)
by short exact sequences

(43)

\displaystyle 0\to\underline{\Hom}^{n-1}(K,L)_{F}\to\Hom^{n}(K,L)\to\underline{\Hom}^{n}(K,L)^{F}\to 0

for all
n

[
BBD
, (5.1.2.5)]
.
The morphism
\Hom^{n}(K,L)\to\Hom^{n}(\xi K,\xi L)
factors through the morphism
\Hom^{n}(K,L)\to\underline{\Hom}^{n}(K,L)^{F}
above.

Throughout this paper, all
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[F]
-modules will arise from mixed complexes, and all of their eigenvalues under the action of
F
will be pure of integer weight in the sense of
[
D80
, §1.2.1]
.
We write
\bm{w}_{\leq\ast}
to denote the weight filtration on such a module
M
.
Explicitly,
\bm{w}_{\leq n}M\subseteq M
is the sum of the generalized eigenspaces of
F
where the eigenvalues are pure of weight
\leq n
.

1.3.

We will introduce a mild generalization of the realization functors used by Rider in
[
R
]
.
Our formalism also resembles the ones used by Bezrukavnikov–Yun in
[
BY
, Appendix B]
and Lusztig–Yun in
[
LY20
, §9]
, though we do not need to develop ours as extensively.
All of these formalisms are ultimately based on the work of Beilinson in
[
B
]
.

Let
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})\subseteq\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
be a full, additive subcategory.
We say that
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
is
realizable
iff, for all
K,L\in\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
, we have

(44)

\displaystyle n\neq 0\implies\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{n}(K,L)=0,

where
\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}
denotes the
0
th graded piece of the weight filtration.
(Compare to Assumption
\mathrm{C}_{1}
in
[
BY
, §B.1]
.)
In practice, the objects of
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
will be direct sums of pure shifted perverse sheaves.

Lemma 1.3.1
.

If
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
is realizable, then

(45)

\displaystyle n\neq 0,1\implies\Hom^{n}(K,L)=0

for all
K,L\in\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
.

Proof.

If
n\neq 0,1
, then
\underline{\Hom}^{n-1}(K,L)_{F},\,\underline{\Hom}^{n}(K,L)^{F}=0
by the realizability of
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
, so
\Hom^{n}(K,L)=0
by the exactness of (
43
).
∎

We write
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})
for the category in which the objects are the same as in
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
, but the morphisms are given by

(46)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})}(K,L)=\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{0}(K,L).

By construction, it is a full, additive subcategory of
\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
.
There is a functor

(47)

\displaystyle\kappa:\mathsf{C}(X_{0})\to\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})

induced by the composition of morphisms

(48)

\displaystyle\Hom(K,L)\to\underline{\Hom}(K,L)^{F}\to\bm{w}_{\leq 0}\,\underline{\Hom}(L,K)\to\Hom_{\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})}(K,L).

If
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
is realizable, then (
48
) is injective by the exactness of (
43
), so in this case,
\kappa
is faithful.

1.4.

Given any additive category
\mathsf{A}
, we write
\mathsf{C}^{b}(\mathsf{A})
for the category of bounded complexes of objects of
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{A})
for the homotopy category of
\mathsf{C}^{b}(\mathsf{A})
.
We write
[1]_{\triangle}
for the endofunctors of these categories that shift each complex down by one degree.

Throughout the rest of this subsection, we assume that
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
is realizable
.
We will construct
realization functors

(49)

\displaystyle\rho:\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(X_{0}))

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0}),

(50)

\displaystyle\emph{resp.}\qquad\rho:\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))

\displaystyle\to\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0}),

that are triangulated in the sense that
\rho([1]_{\triangle})=[1]
and restrict to the identity functor on
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
,
resp.

\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})
, viewed as the full subcategory of
\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(X_{0}))
,
resp.

\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))
, of complexes concentrated in degree zero.
The explicit construction of these functors will show that the diagram

(51)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(X_{0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})}
\mathsf{K}^{b}\kappa
\mathsf{K}^{b}\kappa
\rho
\rho

commutes.

Let
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{C}
be defined by one of the following situations:

(1)

\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
and
\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
.

(2)

\mathsf{D}=\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
and
\mathsf{C}=\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})
.

Let
\tilde{\mathsf{D}}
be a filtered version of
\mathsf{D}
in the sense of Beilinson
[
B
]
, and let
\omega:\tilde{\mathsf{D}}\to\mathsf{D}
be the forgetful functor.
We will construct a full and faithful embedding
\tilde{\rho}:\mathsf{C}^{b}(\mathsf{C})\to\tilde{\mathsf{D}}
, fitting into a commutative diagram:

(52)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}^{b}(\mathsf{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
\tilde{\rho}
\omega
\rho

More precisely, we will show that
\omega\circ\tilde{\rho}
factors through the quotient functor
\mathsf{C}^{b}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C})
, allowing us to define
\rho
.

The filtered structure of
\tilde{\mathsf{D}}
is given by full triangulated subcategories
\tilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\leq n},\tilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\geq n}
for each integer
n
, subject to certain axioms
[
R
, Def. 3.1]
.
Let

(53)

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{\leq n}:\tilde{\mathsf{D}}

\displaystyle\to\tilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\leq n},

(54)

\displaystyle\emph{resp.}\qquad\bm{w}_{\geq n}:\tilde{\mathsf{D}}

\displaystyle\to\tilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\geq n},

be right adjoint to the inclusion
\tilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\leq n}\to\tilde{\mathsf{D}}
,
resp.
left adjoint to the inclusion
\tilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\geq n}\to\tilde{\mathsf{D}}
.
We set

(55)

\displaystyle\gr_{n}=\bm{w}_{\leq n}\,\bm{w}_{\geq n}=\bm{w}_{\geq n}\,\bm{w}_{\leq n}:\tilde{\mathsf{D}}\to\tilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\leq n}\cap\tilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\geq n}\subseteq\tilde{\mathsf{D}}.

Let
s:\tilde{\mathsf{D}}\to\tilde{\mathsf{D}}
be the shift-of-filtration functor, so that
s(\tilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\leq n})=\tilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\leq n+1}
and
s(\tilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\geq n})=\tilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\geq n+1}
.

Let
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}\subseteq\tilde{\mathsf{D}}
be the full, additive subcategory of objects
K
such that
\gr_{n}(K)\in s^{n}\mathsf{C}[n]
for all
n
.
We construct an additive functor
\alpha:\tilde{\mathsf{A}}\to\mathsf{C}^{b}(\mathsf{C})
as follows.
Let

(56)

\displaystyle\alpha(K)^{n}=\omega\gr_{-n}(K)[n],

and let
\delta^{n}:\alpha(K)^{n}\to\alpha(K)^{n+1}
be the morphism we obtain by applying
\omega(-)[n]
to the boundary morphism in the exact triangle

(57)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gr_{-(n+1)}(K)\to\bm{w}_{\leq-n}\,\bm{w}_{\geq-(n+1)}(K)&\to\gr_{-n}(K)\xrightarrow{\delta^{n}}\gr_{-(n+1)}(K)[1].\end{split}

The map
K\mapsto\alpha(K)
is functorial and additive, so it remains to show that:

Lemma 1.4.1
.

For all
K\in\tilde{\mathsf{A}}
, the sequence
\alpha(K)
is a well-defined object of
\mathsf{C}^{b}(\mathsf{C})
,
i.e.
,
\delta^{n+1}\circ\delta^{n}=0
for all
n
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of
[
R
, Lem. 3.6]
, except that we use (
44
) or (
45
) in place of
[
R
, Lem. 2.4]
to ensure that
\Hom(\gr_{-n-2}(K),\gr_{-n}(K))
vanishes for all
n
.
∎

We want to show that
\alpha
is an equivalence.
That
\alpha
is faithful amounts to the following statement:

Lemma 1.4.2
.

If
\phi:K\to L
is a morphism in
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}
, then
\phi=0
if and only if
\gr_{n}(\phi)=0
for all
n
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of
[
R
, Lem. 3.5]
, except that we use (
44
) or (
45
) in place of
[
R
, Lem. 2.4]
to ensure that
\Hom(\gr_{n}(K),\bm{w}_{\leq n-1}(L))
vanishes for all
K,L\in\tilde{\mathsf{A}}
and
n
.
∎

Lemma 1.4.3
.

The functor
\alpha:\tilde{\mathsf{A}}\to\mathsf{C}^{b}(\mathsf{C})
is an equivalence.
Moreover,
\tilde{\rho}\vcentcolon=\alpha^{-1}
factors through the quotient functor
\mathsf{C}^{b}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C})
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of
[
R
, Prop. 3.7]
, with Lemma
1.4.2
in place of
[
R
, Lem. 3.5]
.
∎

Remark 1.4.4
.

Realization functors are compatible with other functors in the following sense.
We keep the notation above, but assume that we have two copies of each category and functor,
e.g.
,
\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}
in place of
\mathsf{D}
.
Let
\Phi:\mathsf{D}_{1}\to\mathsf{D}_{2}
be an exact functor that admits a canonical lift
\tilde{\Phi}:\tilde{\mathsf{D}}_{1}\to\tilde{\mathsf{D}}_{2}
compatible with the filtered structures on these categories in the sense of
[
B
]
.
In particular, this condition means
\tilde{\Phi}
is compatible with the functors
\gr_{n}
.
If
\Phi
restricts to a functor
\mathsf{C}_{1}\to\mathsf{C}_{2}
, then there is a commutative diagram:

(58)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathsf{A}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathsf{A}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}^{b}(\mathsf{C}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}^{b}(\mathsf{C}_{2})}
\tilde{\Phi}
\Phi
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}

It in turn induces a commutative diagram:

(59)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{2}}
\mathsf{K}^{b}\Phi
\Phi
\rho
\rho

So in this situation,
\Phi
is compatible with realization.

In practice, it will be easier to check the conditions on
\Phi
when the categories
\mathsf{D}_{i}
take the form
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
than when they take the form
\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
.

1.5.

We say that a full, additive subcategory
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})\subseteq\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
is
\underline{\Hom}
-pure
iff:

(1)

\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
is stable under
\langle n\rangle
for all
n\in\mathbf{Z}
.

(2)

\underline{\Hom}^{0}(K,L)
is concentrated in weight zero for all
K,L\in\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
.

These hypotheses imply that

(60)

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}^{n}(K,L)\simeq\underline{\Hom}^{0}(K,L\langle n\rangle)(-\tfrac{n}{2})

is concentrated in weight
n
for all
K,L\in\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
and
n\in\mathbf{Z}
, so
\underline{\Hom}
-purity implies realizability.
The following result is inspired by
[
R
, Thm. 4.3]
:

Proposition 1.5.1
.

If
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
is
\underline{\Hom}
-pure, then
\rho:\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))\to\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
induces an isomorphism of vector spaces

(61)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L}\langle n\rangle[-n]_{\triangle})\xrightarrow{\sim}\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{0}(\rho(\mathcal{K}),\rho(\mathcal{L}))

for all
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L}\in\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))
and
n\in\mathbf{Z}
.

Proof.

First, suppose that
\mathcal{K}=K
and
\mathcal{L}=L[i]_{\triangle}
, where
K,L\in\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})
, viewed as objects of
\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))
represented by complexes concentrated in degree zero.
In this case, the right-hand side of (
61
) becomes
\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i}(K,L)
.
If
i\neq n
, then

(62)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L}\langle n\rangle[-n]_{\triangle})\simeq 0\simeq\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i}(K,L),

where the second isomorphism uses the
\underline{\Hom}
-purity of
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
.
If
i=n
, then

(63)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Hom_{\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L}\langle n\rangle[-n]_{\triangle})&\simeq\Hom_{\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})}(K,L\langle n\rangle)\\
&\simeq\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{0}(K,L\langle n\rangle)\\
&\simeq\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{n}(K,L).\end{split}

Now, we allow
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{L}
to be arbitrary, and proceed by double induction on their amplitudes.
As in
[
R
]
, we only illustrate the induction on the amplitude of
\mathcal{K}
.

By taking (brutal) truncations of
\mathcal{K}
, we can build an exact triangle
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{K}^{\prime}[1]
in which
\mathcal{K}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))
are of strictly smaller amplitude than
\mathcal{K}
.
We get the following commutative diagram in which the rows are exact:

(64)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(\mathcal{K}^{\prime},\mathcal{L}\langle n\rangle[-n-1]_{\triangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{L}\langle n\rangle[-n]_{\triangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L}\langle n\rangle[-n]_{\triangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{-1}(\rho(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}),\rho(\mathcal{L}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{0}(\rho(\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}),\rho(\mathcal{L}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{0}(\rho(\mathcal{K}),\rho(\mathcal{L}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdots}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(\mathcal{K}^{\prime},\mathcal{L}\langle n\rangle[-n]_{\triangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{L}\langle n\rangle[n+1]_{\triangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdots}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{0}(\rho(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}),\rho(\mathcal{L}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{1}(\rho(\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}),\rho(\mathcal{L}))}

By the inductive hypothesis, the first, second, fourth, and fifth vertical arrows are isomorphisms, so by the five lemma, the third vertical arrow is an isomorphism as well, completing the induction.
∎

Generalizing
[
R
, Def. 2.3]
, we say that
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
is
Frobenius invariant
iff it is
\underline{\Hom}
-pure and, strengthening hypothesis (2),
\underline{\Hom}^{0}(K,L)
is
F
-invariant for all
K,L
.
In this case,
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})\simeq\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
.
In particular, by the commutativity of (
51
), Proposition
1.5.1
is actually a generalization of
[
R
, Thm. 4.3]
.

1.6.

It is not obvious when functors between categories of the form
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
can be extended to functors between categories of the form
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})
, because
\Hom_{\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})}
is defined in terms of the global sections of a sheaf.
However, we will only need this property in the case of pullback functors, where we can make use of the well-known adjunctions.
The arguments below are essentially the same as the arguments showing that the morphisms on homology/cohomology induced by pullbacks and pushforwards between
G_{0}
-varieties are weight-preserving,
cf.
Section
4
.

Namely, if
f:Y_{0}\to X_{0}
is an (equivariant, stratification-preserving) map of
G_{0}
-varieties over
\mathbf{F}
, then we have a composition of natural transformations of sheaf-valued bifunctors on
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
:

(65)

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}(-,-)\to\underline{\Hom}(-,f_{\ast}f^{\ast}(-))\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{\ast}\underline{\Hom}(f^{\ast}(-),f^{\ast}(-)).

Let
a:X_{0}\to\mathit{pt}_{0}
and
b:Y_{0}\to\mathit{pt}_{0}
be the structure maps, so that
b=a\circ f
.
Then we have
b_{\ast}=a_{\ast}f_{\ast}
, so applying
a_{\ast}
gives:

(66)

\displaystyle a_{\ast}\underline{\Hom}(-,-)\to b_{\ast}\underline{\Hom}(f^{\ast}(-),f^{\ast}(-)).

Applying
\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}\,\mathrm{H}^{0}\circ\xi
gives:

(67)

\displaystyle f^{\ast}:\Hom_{\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})}(-,-)\to\Hom_{\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(Y_{0})}(f^{\ast}(-),f^{\ast}(-)).

We can check that these natural transformations together define a functor

(68)

\displaystyle f^{\ast}:\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})\to\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(Y_{0})

that sends
K\mapsto f^{\ast}K
at the level of objects.

Lemma 1.6.1
.

For
f:Y_{0}\to X_{0}
as above, we have a commutative diagram:

(69)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(Y_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(Y_{0})}
f^{\ast}
\kappa
\kappa
f^{\ast}

Proof.

After we apply
\mathrm{H}^{0}
to (
66
), the resulting natural transformation is
F
-equivariant and commutes with shifts.
So the result follows from the exactness of (
43
).
∎

2.
Mixed Categories in Representation Theory

2.1.

In this section, we introduce the various choices of
X_{0}
and
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
that we need in the rest of the paper.
They, and the functors between them, will be summarized by the following commutative diagram:

(70)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(G_{0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(\mathcal{U}_{0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(G_{0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{U}_{0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(G_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{U}_{0})}
\mathsf{K}^{b}i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle
\mathsf{K}^{b}i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle
i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle
\oplus
\oplus
\mathsf{K}^{b}\mathsf{CH}
\mathsf{CH}
\rho
\rho
\mathsf{K}^{b}\mathsf{PR}
\mathsf{K}^{b}\mathsf{PR}

Note that we will not discuss the “missing” functor
\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(G_{0}))\to\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(G_{0})
.

2.2.

Let
W
be the Weyl group of
G
.
Henceforth, we fix a Coxeter presentation of
W
(see §
A.1
), so that
W
is endowed with a Bruhat order.
As in §
A.5
, we write
\hat{W}
for the set of irreducible characters of
W
.
In particular,
1\in\hat{W}
is the trivial character.

It will also be convenient to set

(71)

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\rk_{\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{X},

(72)

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}|\Phi|.

We have
\dim G=r+2N
.

2.3.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{0}
be the flag variety of
G_{0}
,
i.e.
, the variety that parametrizes its Borel subgroups.
Let
G_{0}
act on
\mathcal{B}_{0}
by right conjugation.

We stratify
\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}
by its diagonal
G_{0}
-orbits.
The poset formed by the orbits under the closure relation is isomorphic to the poset formed by
W
under the Bruhat order.
For each
w\in W
, let

(73)

\displaystyle j_{w}:O_{w,0}\to\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}

denote the inclusion of the corresponding orbit.
We have
\dim O_{w}=|w|+N
, where
|w|
is the Bruhat length of
w
.
Thus the (shift-twisted) intersection complex

(74)

\displaystyle\mathit{IC}_{w}=j_{w,!\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\langle|w|-r-N\rangle\in\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})

is a simple perverse sheaf of weight zero on
[(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]
.

Definition 2.3.1
.

Let
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})\subseteq\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})
be the full, replete, additive subcategory generated by the objects
\mathit{IC}_{w}\langle n\rangle
with
w\in W
and
n\in\mathbf{Z}
.

Lemma 2.3.2

(Achar–Riche)
.

\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})
is Frobenius-invariant, hence
\underline{\Hom}
-pure.

Proof.

[
AR
, Lem. 7.8]
proves a Frobenius-invariance statement for any variety equipped with an affine even stratification.
Their argument generalizes to the stacks in
[
Su
]
as long as the same condition holds on the stratification.

Fix a Borel
B_{0}\subseteq G_{0}
.
The stratification of the coset space
B_{0}\backslash G_{0}
into right
B_{0}
-orbits,
i.e.
, into double cosets, is affine even.
We have an isomorphism

(75)

\displaystyle[B_{0}\backslash G_{0}/B_{0}]\simeq[(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}],

so the stacky version of
[
AR
, Lem. 7.8]
implies the desired result.
∎

2.4.

The
convolution
on
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})
is the monoidal product

(76)

\displaystyle(-)\star(-):\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})\times\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})\to\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})

defined by

(77)

\displaystyle K\star L=\mathit{pr}_{1,3,\ast}(\mathit{pr}_{1,2}\times\mathit{pr}_{2,3})^{\ast}(K\boxtimes L)\langle r+N\rangle,

where
\mathit{pr}_{1,2},\mathit{pr}_{1,3},\mathit{pr}_{2,3}:\mathcal{B}_{0}^{3}\to\mathcal{B}_{0}^{2}
are the three projection maps.

We claim that
\star
is weight-preserving by the results of
[
D80
]
.
Indeed,
\mathit{pr}_{1,3}
is proper, and it can be checked that the map of stacks
[\mathcal{B}_{0}^{3}/G_{0}]\to[\mathcal{B}_{0}^{2}/G_{0}]\times[\mathcal{B}_{0}^{2}/G_{0}]
induced by
\mathit{pr}_{1,2}\times\mathit{pr}_{2,3}
is smooth of relative dimension
\dim G-\dim\mathcal{B}=r+N
.
More strongly, we have
[
BY
, Prop. 3.2.5]
:

Lemma 2.4.1

(Bezrukavnikov–Yun)
.

The convolution
\star
restricts to a monoidal product on
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})
.

Remark 2.4.2
.

Let
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B})
be the the essential image of
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})
in
\mathsf{D}_{G}^{b}(\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B})
.
Prior to
[
BY
]
, it was known that the convolution on
\mathsf{D}_{G}^{b}(\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B})
preserves the class of objects in
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B})
,
cf.

[
S82
, Prop. 2.13]
. As explained in
[
BY
, Rem. 3.2.6]
, the force of the lemma is to assert that for all
K,L\in\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})
, the object
K\star L
is semisimple
before
its pullback to
\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}
.
The proof involves developing a Frobenius-enriched version of some of the results of
[
S82
]
.

The convolution on
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})
induces one on
\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}))
.
In the case where
G
is semisimple
, we define the
Hecke category
of
W
to be the resulting monoidal triangulated category, and abbreviate

(78)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{H}_{W}&=\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})).\end{split}

By Remark
1.4.4
, the convolution on
\mathsf{H}_{W}
is compatible with realization functors in the sense that the diagram

(79)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{H}_{W}\times\mathsf{H}_{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{H}_{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})\times\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})}
\star
\star
\rho\times\rho
\rho

commutes.

Remark 2.4.3
.

This definition of the Hecke category originates in work of Beilinson, Ginzburg, Soergel, and others on Koszul duality patterns in the geometry of mixed complexes.
See
[
BGS
, §4]
and the references therein.

2.5.

Let
H_{W}
be the Iwahori–Hecke algebra of
W
(see §
A.3
).
Below, we review how
\mathsf{H}_{W}
decategorifies to
H_{W}
.

If
\mathsf{A}
is an additive category, then we write
[\mathsf{A}]_{\oplus}
for its
split
Grothendieck group.
The elements of
[\mathsf{A}]_{\oplus}
are the isomorphism classes
[K]
of the objects
K
, and the relations are
[K\oplus L]=[K]+[L]
for all objects
K,L
.
If
\mathsf{A}
is given a monoidal product
\star
, then
[\mathsf{A}]_{\oplus}
forms a ring under the multiplication
[K]\cdot[L]=[K\star L]
.

We endow
[\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})]_{\oplus}
with the action of
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
in which
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}
acts by
\langle-1\rangle
.
For each
K\in\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})
, let

(80)

\displaystyle\chi_{w}(K)=\sum_{i,j}{(-1)^{i+j}}\bm{q}^{\frac{j}{2}}\dim\gr_{j}^{\bm{w}}\mathrm{H}^{i}(j_{w}^{\ast}K),

the virtual weight polynomial of the stalk of
K
over
[O_{w,0}/G]
.
Thm. 2.8 of
[
S82
]
implies if
G
is
semisimple
, then the map

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}{[\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})]_{\oplus}}&\to&H_{W}\\
{[K]}&\mapsto&\displaystyle\sum_{w\in W}\bm{q}^{\frac{|w|}{2}}\sigma_{w}\chi_{w}(K)\end{array}

is a morphism of
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-algebras.
In fact, it is an isomorphism, and if
\gamma_{w}
is the image of
[\mathit{IC}_{w}]
, then the set
\{\gamma_{w}\}_{w\in W}
forms a free
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-linear basis of
H_{W}
,
cf.

[
WW11
, 407]
.
The element
\gamma_{w}
is called the
Kazhdan–Lusztig element
attached to
w
.

If
\mathsf{T}
is a triangulated (additive) category, then we write
[\mathsf{T}]_{\triangle}
for its
triangulated
Grothendieck group.
The elements of
[\mathsf{T}]_{\triangle}
are the isomorphism classes of the objects of
\mathsf{T}
, and the relations are
[\mathcal{K}]=[\mathcal{K}^{\prime}]+[\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}]
for each exact triangle
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{K}^{\prime}[1]
in
\mathsf{T}
.

If
\mathsf{A}
is any additive category, then there is an isomorphism
[\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{A})]_{\triangle}\xrightarrow{\sim}[\mathsf{A}]_{\oplus}
that sends any complex to the alternating sum of its terms
[
Ro
]
.
In particular,

(83)

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\mathsf{H}_{W}]_{\triangle}&\simeq[\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})]_{\oplus}.\end{split}

Since the convolutions on these categories are compatible with each other, this is an isomorphism of rings, not just groups.
We endow
[\mathsf{H}_{W}]_{\triangle}
with the action of
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
in which
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}
acts by
\langle-1\rangle
.
Then we obtain a
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-algebra isomorphism
[\mathsf{H}_{W}]_{\triangle}\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{W}
that again sends
[\mathit{IC}_{w}]\mapsto\gamma_{w}
.

Remark 2.5.1
.

Recall that
q^{\frac{1}{2}}\in\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}
is a fixed square root of
q=|\mathbf{F}|
.
In
[
I
]
, Iwahori gave an isomorphism of
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-algebras:

(84)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\otimes H_{W}|_{\bm{q}^{1/2}=q^{1/2}}\simeq\End_{G^{F}}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[\mathcal{B}^{F}]).

The isomorphism
H_{W}\simeq[\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})]_{\oplus}
is an integral version in the following sense.
We have a commutative diagram

(85)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})]_{\oplus}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Z}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}][\mathcal{B}^{F}\times\mathcal{B}^{F}]^{G^{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{W}|_{\bm{q}^{1/2}=q^{1/2}}}
\sim
\sim

where the left-hand arrow is induced by the function-sheaf dictionary.
Iwahori’s isomorphism can be factored as

(86)

\displaystyle\End_{G^{F}}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[\mathcal{B}^{F}])\xrightarrow{\sim}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[\mathcal{B}^{F}\times\mathcal{B}^{F}]^{G^{F}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\otimes H_{W}|_{\bm{q}^{1/2}=q^{1/2}},

where the second arrow is induced by the bottom arrow of the diagram.

2.6.

Let
G_{0}
act on itself by right conjugation.
There is a
G_{0}
-stable stratification of
G_{0}
by locally-closed subvarieties
[
L84-2
, §3.1]

[
L85-I
, §3.11]
, now known as Jordan classes.
Let

(87)

\displaystyle\mathit{act}:G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0},

(88)

\displaystyle\mathit{pr}:G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}

\displaystyle\to G_{0}

be defined by

(89)

\displaystyle\mathit{act}(g,B)

\displaystyle=(g^{-1}Bg,B),

(90)

\displaystyle\mathit{pr}(g,B)

\displaystyle=g.

The maps above are
G_{0}
-equivariant with respect to the diagonal
G_{0}
-actions on
G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}
.
Moreover, we can stratify
G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}
so that
\mathit{act}
,
resp.

\mathit{pr}
, is stratification-preserving with respect to the Bruhat stratification of
\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}
,
resp.
the Jordan stratification of
G_{0}
.

Following
[
L85-I
]
, we define the
character functor

(91)

\displaystyle\mathsf{CH}:\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})\to\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(G_{0})

to be the composition

(92)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{CH}=\bigoplus_{i}{}^{p}\mathcal{H}^{i}[-i]\circ\mathit{pr}_{\ast}\,\mathit{act}^{!}\langle r+N\rangle.\end{split}

Since
\mathit{act}
is smooth of relative dimension
r+N
and
\mathit{pr}
is proper,
\mathsf{CH}
preserves the class of pure complexes of weight zero
[
D80
]
.
We define a
mixed unipotent character sheaf
to be a simple perverse sheaf
E\in\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(G_{0})
such that
E\langle-i\rangle
is a subquotient of
\mathsf{CH}(\mathit{IC}_{w})
,
resp.

\xi\mathsf{CH}(\mathit{IC}_{w})
, for some
w\in W
and
i\in\mathbf{Z}
.
This means
E
is also pure of weight zero.

Recall that by
[
BBD
, Prop. 5.3.9]
, every indecomposable pure perverse sheaf takes the form
E\otimes M
, where
E
is a simple perverse sheaf and
M
is the pullback of a sheaf on
\mathit{pt}_{0}
that corresponds to a
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[F]
-module on which
F
acts by a single unipotent Jordan block.
Thus, a simple perverse sheaf
E\in\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(G_{0})
is a mixed unipotent character sheaf if and only if
E\langle-i\rangle\otimes M
occurs as a direct summand of
\mathsf{CH}(\mathit{IC}_{w})
for some
w,i,M
as above.

Definition 2.6.1
.

Let
\mathsf{C}(G_{0})\subseteq\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(G_{0})
be the full, replete, additive subcategory generated by the objects
E\otimes M
, where
E
is a mixed unipotent character sheaf and
M
is the pullback of a pure object of
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathit{pt}_{0})
of weight zero.

Remark 2.6.2
.

The objects
\xi\mathsf{CH}(\mathit{IC}_{w})\in\mathsf{D}_{G}^{b}(G)
coincide with the objects
\bar{K}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
studied by Lusztig in
[
L85-III
]
when
\mathcal{L}
is the trivial local system on a fixed maximal torus of
G
.
They also coincide with the objects
\mathbf{K}_{w}
studied by Webster–Williamson in
[
WW11
]
up to a shift-twist.

2.7.

The character functor is a monoidal trace on
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})
in the following sense.
Abbreviate

(93)

\displaystyle\mathsf{CH}(-\star-)\vcentcolon=\mathsf{CH}\circ((-)\star(-)):\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})^{2}\to\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(G_{0}).

The result below can be deduced from a paper of Lusztig
[
L15
]
, identifying the monoidal center of
\mathsf{D}_{G}^{b}(\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B})
with a certain category of unmixed unipotent character sheaves, but we give a self-contained proof.

Lemma 2.7.1
.

We have
{\mathsf{CH}(-\star-)\circ(\mathit{pr}_{2}\times\mathit{pr}_{1})^{\ast}}\,\simeq\,{\mathsf{CH}(-\star-)}
, where
\mathit{pr}_{2}\times\mathit{pr}_{1}
is the involution of
[(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]^{2}
that swaps the factors.

Proof.

Observe that
\mathsf{CH}(-\star-)
is induced by pullback and pushforward along the solid arrows in the diagram below:

(94)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[G_{0}/G_{0}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]^{2}}}
\mathit{pr}
\mathit{act}
\mathit{pr}_{1,3}
\mathit{pr}_{1,2}\times\mathit{pr}_{2,3}

Let the square in the diagram be cartesian.
By proper base change across that square, we see that

(95)

\displaystyle\mathsf{CH}(-\star-)=\bigoplus_{i}{}^{p}\mathcal{H}^{i}[-i]\circ\Psi_{\ast}\circ\Phi^{\ast}\langle 2(r+N)\rangle,

where the functors

(96)

\displaystyle\Phi:[(G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]

\displaystyle\to[(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]^{2},

(97)

\displaystyle\Psi:[(G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]

\displaystyle\to[G_{0}/G_{0}]

are defined by

(98)

\displaystyle\Phi([g,B,B^{\prime}])

\displaystyle=([g^{-1}B^{\prime}g,B],[B,B^{\prime}]),

(99)

\displaystyle\Psi([g,B,B^{\prime}])

\displaystyle=[g].

Now consider the diagram

(100)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[G_{0}/G_{0}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})/G_{0}]}}
\Phi
\Psi
\Phi
\Psi
\mathit{pr}_{2}\times\mathit{pr}_{1}
\Xi

in which
\Xi([g,B,B^{\prime}])=[g,g^{-1}B^{\prime}g,B]
.
We can check that the square on the left and the triangle on the right commute.
(The former is where it is essential to use orbits, not orbit representatives.)
Thus the whole diagram commutes, giving us the desired equivalence of functors.
∎

Remark 2.7.2
.

The cocenter of an associative algebra
A
is the largest commutative quotient,
i.e.
, the algebra
A/[A,A]
.
We expect that the functor
\mathsf{CH}
categorifies the map from the Iwahori–Hecke algebra to its cocenter, by way of the function-sheaf dictionary (see Remark
2.5.1
).

2.8.

Let
\tilde{G}_{0}
be defined by the cartesian diagram:

(101)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{G}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{1,0}=\mathcal{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}}
\mathit{act}
j_{1}

The composition
p^{\prime}:\tilde{G}_{0}\to G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}\to G_{0}
is a small, hence proper, map called the
Grothendieck–Springer map
.
The
Grothendieck–Springer sheaf
is

(102)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}=p^{\prime}_{\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}.

By base change and smallness,
\mathcal{G}\simeq\mathsf{CH}(\mathit{IC}_{1})
.

The Grothendieck–Springer map restricts to a Galois
W
-cover over the regular semisimple locus of
G
.
Therefore, the decomposition of
\mathcal{G}
into indecomposable perverse sheaves takes the form

(103)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\simeq\bigoplus_{\phi\in\hat{W}}\mathcal{G}_{\phi}\otimes V_{\phi},

where each
\mathcal{G}_{\phi}
is a simple perverse sheaf and
V_{\phi}
is the irreducible representation of
W
over
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
of character
\phi
.
We pin down
\mathcal{G}_{\phi}
up to unique isomorphism by declaring that Frobenius acts trivially on
V_{\phi}
.

Definition 2.8.1
.

Let
\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(G_{0})\subseteq\mathsf{C}(G_{0})
be the full, replete, additive subcategory generated by the objects
\mathcal{G}_{\phi}\otimes M
, where
\phi\in\hat{W}
and
M
is the pullback of a pure object of
\mathsf{D}_{G}^{b}(\mathit{pt}_{0})
of weight zero.

Remark 2.8.2
.

The work in Section
4
will show that
\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(G_{0})
and thus
\mathsf{C}(G_{0})
are not realizable, let alone
\underline{\Hom}
-pure.

Remark 2.8.3
.

If
G
is of type
A
, meaning
W=S_{n}
for some
n
, then every (unmixed) unipotent character sheaf on
G
is isomorphic to a summand of
\xi\mathcal{G}
(
e.g.
, by Lusztig’s classification of character sheaves).
So in this case, we have
\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(G_{0})\simeq\mathsf{C}(G_{0})
.
In other types, a unipotent character sheaf that does not occur as a summand of
\xi\mathcal{G}
is said to be
cuspidal
.

2.9.

Let
i:\mathcal{U}_{0}\to G_{0}
be the inclusion of the unipotent locus.
The action of
G_{0}
on itself by conjugation restricts to an action on
\mathcal{U}_{0}
.
We stratify
\mathcal{U}_{0}
by its
G_{0}
-orbits.
Every orbit is a Jordan class of
G_{0}
, so the inclusion
i:\mathcal{U}_{0}\to G_{0}
is stratification-preserving.
We have
\dim\mathcal{U}=2N
, from which
\dim\mathcal{U}-\dim G=-r
.

Definition 2.9.1
.

Let
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{U}_{0})\subseteq\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{U}_{0})
be the full, replete, additive subcategory generated by the objects
i^{\ast}E\otimes M
, where
E
is a mixed unipotent character sheaf and
M
is the pullback of a pure object of
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathit{pt}_{0})
of weight zero.

Since the map
i
is merely a closed immersion, there is a priori no reason that
i^{\ast}
sends unipotent character sheaves to well-behaved objects.
Nevertheless:

Theorem 2.9.2

(Lusztig)
.

If
E
is a mixed unipotent character sheaf, then there exist a
G_{0}
-orbit
j_{C}:C_{0}\to\mathcal{U}_{0}
and a pure
\ell
-adic local system
\mathcal{L}\in\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(C_{0})
of weight zero such that
i^{\ast}E\simeq j_{C,!\ast}\mathcal{L}\langle n_{C}\rangle
for some
n_{C}\in\mathbf{Z}
that only depends on
C
.

Proof.

The unmixed version of this statement is
[
L84-2
, Thm. 6.5(c)]
.
Below, we explain how to deduce our mixed version.
The notation
\mathit{IC}(-,-)
will mean the intersection complex of a local system (
not
necessarily shifted to be perverse).

First,
\xi E
is an object of
\mathsf{D}_{G}^{b}(G)
that, in Lusztig’s notation, takes the form
\phi_{!}K[\dim\bar{Y}]
, where
\phi
is a map
X\to\bar{Y}\subseteq G
and
K=\mathit{IC}(X,\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{1})
for some local system
\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
over
X

[
L84-2
, §4]
.
As long as we start with
\mathbf{F}
-structures on
X,Y,\phi,\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
such that
\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
is pure of weight zero, the construction descends to
G_{0}
to yield
E=\phi_{!}K\langle\dim\bar{Y}\rangle
.
In the notation of
[
L84-2
, §6.6]
, we have
i^{\ast}K\simeq K^{a}
, where
K^{a}=\mathit{IC}(X_{0}^{a},\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{a})
and
\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{a}=i^{\ast}\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
(and the subscript
0
means the
\mathbf{F}
-structure on
X^{a}
coming from the
\mathbf{F}
-structure we chose on
X
).
Therefore,
i^{\ast}E\simeq\phi_{!}^{a}K^{a}\langle\dim\bar{Y}\rangle
.
Since
\phi^{a}
is proper,
\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{a}
being pure implies
\phi_{!}^{a}K^{a}
being pure of the same weight
[
D80
]
,
cf.
(6.6.1) of
[
L84-2
]
.

In our situation,
E
is a unipotent character sheaf, which means that
\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
is a constant sheaf
[
L85-I
, §3.1, §4.4]
.
Therefore,
\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{a}
is the constant sheaf on
X_{0}^{a}
, hence pure of weight zero.
Therefore
i^{\ast}E
is pure of weight zero.
Now, the rest of
[
L84-2
, Thm. 6.5(c)]
implies that at the unmixed level,
i^{\ast}E
is a shift of some intersection complex
j_{C,!\ast}\mathcal{E}
.
Since
\mathcal{E}
is the shifted restriction of
i^{\ast}E
to
C
, it must be pure.
So up to twisting
\mathcal{E}
, we can find a pure local system
\mathcal{L}
of weight zero such that
i^{\ast}E\simeq j_{C,!\ast}\mathcal{L}\langle n\rangle
for some
n
.
Finally,
[
L84-2
, Thm. 6.5(c)]
also implies that
n
only depends on
\dim\bar{Y}^{a}
and
\dim C
, and the former is determined by a variety
C_{1}
that only depends on
C
.
∎

2.10.

Let
p:\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\to\mathcal{U}_{0}
be defined by the cartesian diagram:

(104)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{G}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
p
p^{\prime}
i

Then
p
(or rather,
p^{\mathrm{red}}:\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0}^{\mathrm{red}}\to\mathcal{U}_{0}
) is a semismall resolution of singularities called the
Springer resolution
.
The
Springer sheaf
is

(105)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=p_{\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\langle-r\rangle.

By proper base change,
\mathcal{S}=i^{\ast}\mathcal{G}\langle-r\rangle
.
Setting
\mathcal{S}_{\phi}=i^{\ast}\mathcal{G}_{\phi}\langle-r\rangle
, we have that

(106)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\simeq\bigoplus_{\phi\in\hat{W}}\mathcal{S}_{\phi}\otimes V_{\phi}

is the decomposition of
\mathcal{S}
into indecomposable perverse sheaves.

Definition 2.10.1
.

Let
\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(\mathcal{U}_{0})\subseteq\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{U}_{0})
be the full, replete, additive subcategory generated by the objects
\mathcal{S}_{\phi}\otimes M
, where
\phi\in\hat{W}
and
M
is the pullback of a pure object of
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathit{pt}_{0})
of weight zero.

Lemma 2.10.2
.

\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{U}_{0})
and thus
\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(\mathcal{U}_{0})
are
\underline{\Hom}
-pure.

Proof.

\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{U}_{0})
is closed under
\langle n\rangle
for all
n
because the same is true of the class of pure objects of weight zero in
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathit{pt}_{0})
.
It remains to check that
\underline{\Hom}^{0}(i^{\ast}E,i^{\ast}E^{\prime})
is concentrated in weight zero for all mixed unipotent character sheaves
E,E^{\prime}
.

In the notation of Theorem
2.9.2
, we can write
i^{\ast}E\simeq j_{C,!\ast}\mathcal{L}\langle n_{C}\rangle
and
i^{\ast}E^{\prime}\simeq j_{C^{\prime},!\ast}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\langle n_{C^{\prime}}\rangle
for some
C,C^{\prime},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\prime},n_{C},n_{C^{\prime}}
.
It remains to show that

(107)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\underline{\Hom}^{0}(j_{C,!\ast}\mathcal{L}\langle n_{C}\rangle,j_{C^{\prime},!\ast}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\langle n_{C^{\prime}}\rangle)\\
&=\underline{\Hom}^{d}(j_{C,!\ast}\mathcal{L}\langle\dim C\rangle,j_{C^{\prime},!\ast}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\langle\dim C^{\prime}\rangle)(\tfrac{d}{2})\end{split}

is concentrated in weight zero, where
d=(n_{C^{\prime}}-\dim C^{\prime})-(n_{C}-\dim C)
.
Note that
j_{C,!\ast}\mathcal{L}\langle\dim C\rangle
and
j_{C^{\prime},!\ast}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\langle\dim C^{\prime}\rangle
are both pure perverse of weight zero.
So the desired claim follows from
[
RR
, Cor. 4.8]
.
∎

2.11.

To conclude this section, we discuss the relationships between the categories
\mathsf{C}(G_{0})
,
\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(G_{0})
,
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{U}_{0})
, and
\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(\mathcal{U}_{0})
.

Theorem 2.11.1

(Lusztig, Rider–Russell)
.

The horizontal arrows in the commutative diagrams below are inclusions of direct summands:

(108)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(G_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}(G_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathsf{C}}_{\hat{W}}(G_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathsf{C}}(G_{0})}
\kappa
\kappa

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(\mathcal{U}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{U}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathsf{C}}_{\hat{W}}(\mathcal{U}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathsf{C}}(\mathcal{U}_{0})}
\kappa
\kappa

Proof.

In the diagram for
G_{0}
, the statement for the bottom arrow is essentially a theorem of Lusztig
[
L85-II
, Prop. 7.2]
.
The only modification needed is
G
-equivariance, which is done in
[
WW08
, Prop. 8]
.
In the diagram for
\mathcal{U}_{0}
, the statement for the bottom arrow follows from
[
RR
, Thm. 3.5]
.
Finally, the statements for the top arrows follow from the exactness of (
43
).
∎

We define the functors

(109)

\displaystyle\mathsf{PR}:\mathsf{C}(G_{0})

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(G_{0}),

(110)

\displaystyle\mathsf{PR}:\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{U}_{0})

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(\mathcal{U}_{0}),

as well as their analogues with
\underline{\mathsf{C}}
in place of
\mathsf{C}
, to be the projections that correspond to these summand inclusions.
By Lemma
1.6.1
, we have:

Lemma 2.11.2
.

We have a commutative diagram:

(111)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}(G_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{U}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathsf{C}}(G_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathsf{C}}(\mathcal{U}_{0})}
i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle
\kappa
\kappa
i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle

Here, the bottom arrow is defined by (
68
).

3.
The
\mathbf{A}_{W}
Trace

3.1.

If
A
is a
\mathbf{Z}
-graded
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-algebra, then we write:

(1)

\mathsf{Mod}_{1}(A)
for the category of graded left
A
-modules in which the grading is bounded below and each graded piece is finite-dimensional.

(2)

\mathsf{Mod}_{2}^{b}(A)
for the category of left
A
-modules
M
equipped with a
\mathbf{Z}^{2}
-grading such that
M\in\mathsf{Mod}_{1}(A)
with respect to both gradings and the value of
i-j
is uniformly bounded among the nonzero components
M^{i,j}
.

If
R
is an (ungraded)
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-algebra and
A
forms an
R
-module, then we write
R\ltimes A
for the vector space
R\otimes A
endowed with the ring structure where:

•

R
and
A
form subrings.

•

The only relations between
R
and
A
are those of the form
ra=(r\cdot a)r
for all
r\in R
and
a\in A
.

If the
R
-action on
A
preserves the grading, then we extend the grading on
A
to a grading on
R\ltimes A
in which
R
is placed in degree zero, so that
R\ltimes A
forms a graded
A
-algebra.

Recall that
\mathbf{X}
is the character lattice in the root datum of
G
.
We set

(112)

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{X}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}.

Let the symmetric algebra
\Sym(\mathbf{V})
be endowed with the grading where
\Sym^{i}(\mathbf{V})
is placed in degree
2i
, and let

(113)

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{W}=\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W]\ltimes\Sym(\mathbf{V}).

Below, we construct a contravariant, monoidal trace from the Hecke category to
\mathsf{Mod}_{2}^{b}(\mathbf{A}_{W})
.

3.2.

Suppose that
X_{0}
is a
G_{0}
-variety over
\mathbf{F}
and that
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
is a full, additive subcategory of
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})
stable under
\langle n\rangle
for all
n\in\mathbf{Z}
.
Any object
K\in\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})
gives rise to a graded
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-algebra:

(114)

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{K}=\bigoplus_{n}{\Hom_{\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})}(K,K\langle n\rangle)}.

We write the multiplication in
\mathbf{A}_{K}
as right-to-left composition:
g\cdot f=g\circ f
.
Then we have additive functors

(115)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{n}{\Hom_{\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})}(-,K\langle n\rangle)}:\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})^{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Mod}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{K}),

(116)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{n}{\Hom_{\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})}(K,(-)\langle n\rangle)}:\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Mod}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{K}^{\mathrm{op}}).

Above, the gradings are bounded below by Remark
1.2.1
.
These functors can be respectively extended to

(117)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i,j}{\Hom_{\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))}(-,K\langle j\rangle[i-j]_{\triangle})}:\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))^{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Mod}_{2}^{b}(\mathbf{A}_{K}),

(118)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i,j}{\Hom_{\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))}(K,(-)\langle j\rangle[i-j]_{\triangle})}:\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Mod}_{2}^{b}(\mathbf{A}_{K}^{\mathrm{op}}),

once we embed
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0})
into
\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))
as the full subcategory of complexes concentrated in degree zero.
Above, in either direct sum, the value of
i-j
is uniformly bounded among the nonzero terms precisely because the objects of
\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))
can be represented by bounded complexes.

In the rest of this section, we only use the contravariant functors; the covariant ones will appear again in Section
6
.
The following lemma is a consequence of Proposition
1.5.1
.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

If
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})
is
\underline{\Hom}
-pure, then we have a commutative diagram:

(119)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(X_{0}))^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(X_{0}))^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mod}_{2}^{b}(\mathbf{A}_{K})}
\mathsf{K}^{b}\kappa
\rho
\bigoplus_{i,j}\Hom(-,K\langle j\rangle[i-j]_{\triangle})
\bigoplus_{i,j}\gr_{j}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i}(-,K)

3.3.

We now set
X_{0}=\mathcal{U}_{0}
and
\mathsf{C}(X_{0})=\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{U}_{0})
as in Definition
2.9.1
.
Recall that
\mathcal{S}\in\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{U}_{0})
denotes the (mixed) Springer sheaf.

Theorem 3.3.1

(Lusztig)
.

We have
\mathbf{A}_{W}\simeq\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{S}}
as graded
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-algebras.

Proof.

By
[
L88
, Cor. 6.4]
,
\mathbf{A}_{W}\simeq\End^{\ast}(\xi\mathcal{S})
.
By Lemma
2.10.2
(or
[
R
, Lem. 5.4]
), we have
\End^{i}(\xi\mathcal{S})\simeq\underline{\Hom}^{0}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}\langle i\rangle)
for all
i
after we forget Frobenius.
∎

Remark 3.3.2
.

Although we will not use it, the proof of
[
R
, Prop. 6.5]
implies that the functor
\bigoplus_{i}\Hom(\mathcal{S},(-)\langle i\rangle)
induces a triangulated equivalence

(120)

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(\mathcal{U}_{0}))\simeq\mathsf{D}^{b}(\mathsf{Mod}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{W})),

where the right-hand side is the bounded derived category of
\mathsf{Mod}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{W})
.

Definition 3.3.3
.

The
\mathbf{A}_{W}
trace
on the Hecke category is the contravariant functor

(121)

\displaystyle\mathsf{AH}:\mathsf{H}_{W}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Mod}_{2}^{b}(\mathbf{A}_{W})

defined by the commutative diagram

(122)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}))^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{U}_{0}))^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(\mathcal{U}_{0}))^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0})^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{U}_{0})^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mod}_{2}^{b}(\mathbf{A}_{W})}
\mathsf{K}^{b}(i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle\circ\mathsf{CH})
i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle\circ\mathsf{CH}
\mathsf{K}^{b}\kappa
\rho
\rho

that we obtain by combining Remark
1.4.4
, Lemma
2.10.2
, Lemma
3.2.1
, and Theorem
3.3.1
.
Explicitly,

(123)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{AH}^{i,j}&=\Hom_{\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(\mathcal{U}_{0}))}(\mathsf{K}^{b}(\kappa\circ i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle\circ\mathsf{CH})(-),\mathcal{S}\langle j\rangle[i-j]_{\triangle})\\
&\simeq\gr_{j}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i}((\rho\circ\mathsf{K}^{b}(i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle\circ\mathsf{CH}))(-),\mathcal{S})\\
&\simeq\gr_{j}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i}((i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle\circ\mathsf{CH}\circ\rho)(-),\mathcal{S})\end{split}

for all
i,j
.
It is a monoidal trace in the sense of Lemma
2.7.1
because it factors through
\mathsf{CH}
.

4.
Steinberg Schemes

4.1.

In Section
2
, we reviewed the (mixed) Springer sheaf
\mathcal{S}\in\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathcal{U}_{0})
and the (mixed) Grothendieck–Springer sheaf
\mathcal{G}\in\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(G_{0})
.
In Section
3
, we gave a construction of a graded algebra
\mathbf{A}_{K}
from a mixed complex
K
: explicitly,

(124)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{A}_{K}&=\bigoplus_{n}{\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}}\underline{\Hom}^{0}(K,K\langle n\rangle)\\
&\simeq\bigoplus_{n}{\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}}\underline{\End}^{n}(K).\end{split}

In this section, we use topological methods to prove Theorem
1
:
The map

(125)

\displaystyle i^{\ast}:\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{G}}\to\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{S}}

induced by
i:\mathcal{U}_{0}\to\mathcal{G}_{0}
via Lemma
1.6.1
is an isomorphism of graded
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-algebras.

Remark 4.1.1
.

By Lemma
2.10.2
,
\underline{\End}^{n}(\mathcal{S})
is concentrated in weight
n
(see also
[
R
, Lem. 5.4]
.)
However, if
n>0
, then
\underline{\End}^{n}(\mathcal{G})
is not concentrated in weight
n
, essentially because the cohomology of the Grothendieck–Springer resolution of
G
contains the cohomology of a torus.
See Example
6.2.2
.

Remark 4.1.2
.

Our proof of Theorem
1
is inspired by an unpublished note of Riche about the analogous statement at the level of Lie algebras, which is well-known.
Riche states that his note was inspired by
[
CG
, §3.4]
.

4.2.

If
X_{0}
is a
G_{0}
-variety over
\mathbf{F}
, with structure map
a:X_{0}\to\mathit{pt}_{0}
, then its mixed equivariant dualizing complex is

(126)

\displaystyle\omega=\omega_{X_{0}/G_{0}}\vcentcolon=a^{!}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\in\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0}).

Its equivariant Borel–Moore homology (with coefficients in
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
) is

(127)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(X)\vcentcolon=\underline{\Hom}^{\ast}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},\omega),

viewed as a graded
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[F]
-module.
Borel–Moore homology is functorial in the following senses:

(1)

If
f:Y\to X
is proper, then it induces a pushforward

(128)

\displaystyle f_{\ast}:\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(Y)\to\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(X)

via the composition
f_{\ast}\omega_{Y/G}\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{!}f^{!}\omega_{X/G}\to\omega_{X/G}
.

(2)

If
f:Y\to X
is smooth of relative dimension
d
, then it induces a pullback

(129)

\displaystyle f^{\ast}:\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(X)\to\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(Y)\langle-2d\rangle

via the composition
\omega_{X/G}\to f_{\ast}f^{\ast}\omega_{X/G}\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{\ast}\omega_{Y/G}\langle-2d\rangle
.
If
f
is an affine-space bundle, then
f^{\ast}
is a shifted isomorphism.

(3)

If
i:Z\to X
is a closed immersion with complement
j:U\to X
, then it induces a long exact sequence

(130)

\displaystyle\cdots\to\mathrm{H}_{i}^{!,G}(Z)\to\mathrm{H}_{i}^{!,G}(X)\to\mathrm{H}_{i}^{!,G}(U)\to\mathrm{H}_{i-1}^{!,G}(Z)\to\cdots

via the exact triangle
i_{!}i^{!}\to\id\to j_{\ast}j^{\ast}\to i_{!}i^{!}[1]
.

(4)

If we have a cartesian square

(131)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
f_{Y}
g
g^{\prime}
f

in which
[X/G],[X^{\prime}/G]
are smooth of respective dimensions
d,d^{\prime}
, then
f
induces a
pullback with restricted support

(132)

\displaystyle f_{Y}^{\ast}:\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(Y^{\prime})\to\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(Y)\langle 2(d^{\prime}-d)\rangle

via the composition

(133)

\displaystyle\omega_{Y^{\prime}/G}\langle-2d^{\prime}\rangle\xrightarrow{\sim}{g^{\prime}}^{!}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\to f_{Y,\ast}g^{!}f^{\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{Y,\ast}\omega_{Y/G}\langle-2d\rangle.

Here, we use proper base change
[
LO08
, §5]
to identify
{g^{\prime}}^{!}f_{\ast}\simeq f_{Y,\ast}g^{!}
.

4.3.

We can express
\underline{\Hom}
-spaces between pushforward sheaves, like
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{G}}
, in terms of the Borel–Moore homology of associated schemes.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

If we have a cartesian square of
G
-varieties

(134)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\tilde{f}
p_{Y}
p
f

in which
[\tilde{X}/G]
is smooth of dimension
\tilde{d}
, then

(135)

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}^{\ast}(f_{!}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},p_{\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})\simeq\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\tilde{Y})\langle-2\tilde{d}\rangle.

In particular, if
p
is proper and
K=p_{\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
, then

(136)

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{K}^{n}\simeq\gr_{n-2\tilde{d}}^{\bm{w}}\mathrm{H}_{-(n-2\tilde{d})}^{!,G}(\tilde{X}\times_{X}\tilde{X})

for all
n
, and
\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\tilde{Y})
forms a left graded
\mathbf{A}_{K}
-module.

Proof.

By proper base change,

(137)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f^{!}p_{\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}&\simeq p_{Y,\ast}\tilde{f}^{!}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\simeq p_{Y,\ast}\tilde{f}^{!}\omega_{\tilde{X}/G}\langle-2\tilde{d}\rangle\simeq p_{Y,\ast}\omega_{\tilde{Y}/G}\langle-2\tilde{d}\rangle,\end{split}

giving
\underline{\Hom}(f_{!}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},p_{\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})\simeq f_{\ast}\underline{\Hom}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},f^{!}p_{\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})\simeq f_{\ast}p_{Y,\ast}\underline{\Hom}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},\omega_{\tilde{Y}/G})\langle-2\tilde{d}\rangle
.
∎

4.4.

In the notation of Section
2
, let

(138)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\times_{\mathcal{U}_{0}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0},

(139)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle=\tilde{G}_{0}\times_{G_{0}}\tilde{G}_{0}.

In the literature, the underlying reduced scheme of
\mathcal{Z}
is called the
Steinberg variety
, since it was introduced in
[
St76
]
.
Lemma
4.3.1
implies that

(140)

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{S}}^{n}

\displaystyle\simeq\gr_{n+2r}^{\bm{w}}\mathrm{H}_{-(n+2r)}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z})\left(\simeq\mathrm{H}_{-(n+2r)}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z})\right),

(141)

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{G}}^{n}

\displaystyle\simeq\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}\mathrm{H}_{-n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}).

It remains to compare the Borel–Moore homologies of
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
.

Let us outline the strategy.
We will lift the Bruhat strata of
\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}
to strata
\mathcal{Z}_{w}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
.
To compare the Borel–Moore homologies of
\mathcal{Z}_{w}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w}
, we will further replace these strata with affine-space bundles
\mathcal{Y}_{w}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{w}
such that there is an explicit map
\mathcal{Y}_{w}\times T\to\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{w}
, where
T
is a maximal torus of
G
.
Finally, we will use the long exact sequence (
130
) to patch the stratum-wise comparisons together.

4.5.

Let
\tilde{i}:\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\to\tilde{G}_{0}
be the lift of the inclusion
i:\mathcal{U}_{0}\to G_{0}
.
Let
\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2}:\mathcal{Z}_{0}\to\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0}
be the two projection maps, and let
\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{1},\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{2}:\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{0}\to\tilde{G}_{0}
be defined similarly.
Below, we will continually refer to the commutative diagram:

(142)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{G}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
i_{\mathcal{Z}}
\tilde{p}_{j}
\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{j}
\tilde{i}
p
p^{\prime}
i

Note that both squares in the diagram are cartesian.

Lemma 4.5.1
.

We have a commutative diagram

(143)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{G}}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{S}}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}\mathrm{H}_{-n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{-(n+2r)}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z})}
i^{\ast}
\sim
\sim
i_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\ast}

where
i^{\ast}
is defined by (
68
), the vertical isomorphisms are (
140
) and (
141
), and
i_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\ast}
is the pullback with restricted support induced by
\tilde{i}
via the top square of (
142
).

Proof.

The morphism
i_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\ast}
is induced by the morphism

(144)

\displaystyle(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{1}^{!}\to(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{1}^{!}\tilde{i}_{\ast}\tilde{i}^{\ast}\xrightarrow{\sim}i_{\mathcal{Z},\ast}{\tilde{p}}_{1}^{!}\tilde{i}^{\ast},

while the morphism
i^{\ast}
is induced by the composition

(145)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{2,\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{1}^{!}&\xrightarrow{\sim}{p^{\prime}}^{!}p^{\prime}_{\ast}\\
&\to{p^{\prime}}^{!}i_{\ast}i^{\ast}p^{\prime}_{\ast}\\
&\xrightarrow{\sim}\tilde{i}_{\ast}p^{!}p_{!}\tilde{i}^{\ast}\\
&\xrightarrow{\sim}\tilde{i}_{\ast}\tilde{p}_{2,!}\tilde{p}_{1}^{!}i^{\ast}\\
&\xrightarrow{\sim}\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{2,\ast}i_{\mathcal{Z},\ast}\tilde{p}_{1}^{!}i^{\ast}.\end{split}

By further diagram-chasing, the latter is obtained by applying
\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{2,\ast}
to the former.
∎

To analyze
i_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\ast}
, we decompose
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{0}
into strata.
For each
w\in W
, we define subschemes
\mathcal{Z}_{w,0}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w,0}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{0}
by stipulating that both squares in the commutative diagram below are cartesian:

(146)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}}
i_{\mathcal{Z},w}
i_{\mathcal{Z}}
j_{w}

Here, the map
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{0}\to\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}
is induced by the map
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{0}\xrightarrow{\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{1}\times\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{2}}\tilde{G}_{0}\times\tilde{G}_{0}
.

To analyze
i_{\mathcal{Z},w}:\mathcal{Z}_{w,0}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w,0}
, we rewrite these strata in a way that expresses their structure as fiber bundles over
O_{w,0}
.
Choose a split maximal torus
T_{0}\subseteq G_{0}
.
Choose a Borel
B_{0}\supseteq T_{0}
with unipotent radical
U_{0}\subseteq B_{0}
, so that
B_{0}\simeq T_{0}\ltimes U_{0}
.
Let
N_{0}
be the normalizer of
T_{0}
in
G_{0}
.
For each
w\in W
, fix a lift
\dot{w}\in N_{0}
.
Then
\dot{w}B_{0}\dot{w}^{-1}
contains
\dot{w}T_{0}\dot{w}^{-1}=T_{0}
and only depends on
w
.
If we write

(147)

\displaystyle B_{w,0}

\displaystyle=B_{0}\cap\dot{w}B_{0}\dot{w}^{-1},

(148)

\displaystyle U_{w,0}

\displaystyle=U_{0}\cap\dot{w}U_{0}\dot{w}^{-1},

then
B_{w,0}\simeq T_{0}\ltimes U_{w,0}
.
In what follows, let
B_{w,0}
act on
G_{0}
by right multiplication and on
U_{w,0}
and itself by right conjugation.

Lemma 4.5.2
.

We have a commutative diagram

(149)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G_{0}\times U_{w,0})/B_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G_{0}\times B_{w,0})/B_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}/B_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{w,0}}
\sim
\sim
\sim
i_{\mathcal{Z},w}

where the middle vertical map sends
[g,b]\mapsto[gbg^{-1},gBg^{-1},g\dot{w}B\dot{w}^{-1}g^{-1}]
.

Remark 4.5.3
.

The vertical isomorphisms respectively transport the
G
-actions on
\mathcal{Z}_{w}
,
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w}
,
O_{w}
to the
G
-actions on
(G\times U_{w})/B_{w}
,
(G\times B_{w})/B_{w}
,
G/B_{w}
given by left multiplication on the factor of
G
.

Proof.

The
w=1
case, entailing isomorphisms
(G\times U)/B\simeq\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
and
(G\times B)/B\simeq\tilde{G}
, is explained in
[
Sl
, Ch. 4]
, and the general case is similar.
∎

Again, let
T_{0}
act on
G_{0}
by right multiplication and on
U_{w,0}
and
B_{w,0}
by right conjugation.
Let
\mathcal{Y}_{w,0}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{w,0}
be defined by the cartesian squares:

(150)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G_{0}\times U_{w,0})/T_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G_{0}\times U_{w,0})/B_{w,0}}
\sim

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G_{0}\times B_{w,0})/T_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G_{0}\times B_{w,0})/B_{w,0}}
\sim
\sim

Let
i_{\mathcal{Y},w}:\mathcal{Y}_{w,0}\to\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{w,0}
be the pullback of
i_{\mathcal{Z},w}:\mathcal{Z}_{w,0}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w,0}
.
The advantage of
i_{\mathcal{Y},w}
over
i_{\mathcal{Z},w}
is that the former can be factored as

(151)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{w,0}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{Y}_{w,0}\times\{1\}\to\mathcal{Y}_{w,0}\times T_{0}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{w,0}.

If we let
G_{0}
act trivially on
T_{0}
, then these maps are all
G_{0}
-equivariant.

4.6.

In what follows, if
M=\bigoplus_{n}M^{n}
is a
\mathbf{Z}
-graded
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[F]
-module, then we write

(152)

\displaystyle\begin{split}M^{\dagger}&\simeq\bigoplus_{n}{\gr_{n}^{\bm{w}}M^{n}}.\end{split}

Let
i_{\mathcal{Z},w}^{\ast}:\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w})\to\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}_{w})\langle 2r\rangle
be the pullback with restricted support induced by
\tilde{i}
via the top square of (
142
) and the left square of (
146
), and let

(153)

\displaystyle i_{\mathcal{Z},w}^{\dagger}:\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w})^{\dagger}\to\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}_{w})\langle 2r\rangle

be its restriction to
\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w})^{\dagger}
.
Let
i_{\mathcal{Y},w}^{\dagger}
,
i_{\mathcal{Y},w}^{\dagger}
be defined similarly.

Lemma 4.6.1
.

The map
i_{\mathcal{Y},w}^{\dagger}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Writing
N_{w}=\dim U_{w}
, we have

(154)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Y}_{w})\simeq\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(G/T)\langle 2N_{w}\rangle\simeq\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,T}(\mathit{pt})\langle 2N_{w}\rangle.

By Poincaré duality,
\mathrm{H}_{-i}^{!,T}(\mathit{pt})\simeq\mathrm{H}_{!,T}^{-i}(\mathit{pt})\simeq\mathrm{H}_{T}^{i-2r}(\mathit{pt})
, where
\mathrm{H}_{!,T}^{\ast}
denotes
T
-equivariant, compactly-supported cohomology.
So Frobenius acts on
\mathrm{H}_{-i}^{!,T}(\mathit{pt})
by
q^{\frac{i}{2}-r}
, which means it is concentrated in weight
i-2r
.

Again by Poincaré duality,
\mathrm{H}_{-j}^{!}(T)\simeq\mathrm{H}_{!}^{-j}(T)^{\vee}\simeq\mathrm{H}^{j+2r}(T)
, where
\mathrm{H}_{\ast}^{!}
,
resp.

\mathrm{H}_{!}^{\ast}
, denotes non-equivariant Borel–Moore homology,
resp.
compactly-supported cohomology.
So Frobenius acts on
\mathrm{H}_{-j}^{!}(T)
by
q^{j+2r}
, which means it is concentrated in weight
2j+4r
.

Altogether, the Künneth isomorphism

(155)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{-n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{w})\xrightarrow{\sim}\bigoplus_{i+j=n}\mathrm{H}_{-i}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Y}_{w})\otimes\mathrm{H}_{-j}^{!}(T)

restricts to an isomorphism

(156)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{-n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{w})^{\dagger}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{H}_{-(n+2r)}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Y}_{w})\otimes H_{2r}^{!}(T).

But
i_{\mathcal{Y},w}^{\ast}
is the composition of the Künneth isomorphism with

(157)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Y}_{w})\otimes\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!}(T)&\to\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Y}_{w})\otimes\mathrm{H}_{2r}^{!}(T)\\
&\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Y}_{w})\otimes\mathrm{H}_{0}^{!}(\mathit{pt})\langle 2r\rangle\\
&\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Y}_{w})\langle 2r\rangle,\end{split}

so we win.
∎

Lemma 4.6.2
.

The map
i_{\mathcal{Z},w}^{\dagger}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The maps
\mathcal{Y}_{w,0}\to\mathcal{Z}_{w,0}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{w,0}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w,0}
define affine-space bundles of rank
N_{w}
, so they induce pullback isomorphisms

(158)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}_{w})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Y}_{w})\langle-2N_{w}\rangle,

(159)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{H}_{-\ast}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{w})\langle-2N_{w}\rangle

that preserve weights.
Now we are done by the previous lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Fix a total order
\preceq
on
W
in which
w_{1}\preceq w_{2}
implies
|w_{1}|\leq|w_{2}|
, where
|{-}|
denotes Bruhat length.
Such a total order is necessarily a refinement of the Bruhat partial order.
For any
w\in W
, let

(160)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\preceq w,0}

\displaystyle=\coprod_{x\preceq w}\mathcal{Z}_{x,0}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{0},

(161)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\prec w,0}

\displaystyle=\coprod_{x\prec w}\mathcal{Z}_{x,0}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{0}.

Then
\mathcal{Z}_{w,0}\to\mathcal{Z}_{\preceq w,0}
is a closed immersion with complement
\mathcal{Z}_{\prec w,0}\to\mathcal{Z}_{\preceq w,0}
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{\preceq w,0},\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{\prec w,0}
be defined similarly.
The map
i_{\mathcal{Z}}:\mathcal{Z}_{0}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{0}
gives rise to the following commutative diagram whose rows are long exact sequences and whose vertical arrows are pullbacks with restricted support:

(162)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{\preceq w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{\prec w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}_{w})\langle 2r\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}_{\preceq w})\langle 2r\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}_{\prec w})\langle 2r\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
i_{\mathcal{Z},w}^{\ast}
i_{\mathcal{Z},\preceq w}^{\ast}
i_{\mathcal{Z},\prec w}^{\ast}

Since
(-)^{\dagger}
is exact, we obtain another commutative diagram

(163)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{w})^{\dagger}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{\preceq w})^{\dagger}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{\prec w})^{\dagger}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}_{w})\langle 2r\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}_{\preceq w})\langle 2r\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{n}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}_{\prec w})\langle 2r\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
i_{\mathcal{Z},w}^{\dagger}
i_{\mathcal{Z},\preceq w}^{\dagger}
i_{\mathcal{Z},\prec w}^{\dagger}

in which the top row remains exact.

We induct along
\preceq
.
If
w=1
, then
i_{\mathcal{Z},\prec w}^{\dagger}=i_{\mathcal{Z},w}^{\dagger}
.
By Lemma
4.6.2
, the latter is an isomorphism in every homological degree
n
.
Now let
w
be arbitrary.
If
x
is the immediate predecessor of
w
in the total order
\preceq
, then
\mathcal{Z}_{\prec w}=\mathcal{Z}_{\preceq x}
and
i_{\mathcal{Z},\prec w}^{\dagger}=i_{\mathcal{Z},\preceq x}^{\dagger}
.
Lemma
4.6.2
and the inductive hypothesis respectively imply that
i_{\mathcal{Z},w}^{\dagger}
and
i_{\mathcal{Z},\prec w}^{\dagger}
are isomorphisms in every degree.
So, by the five lemma,
i_{\mathcal{Z},\preceq w}^{\dagger}
is an isomorphism in every degree as well, completing the induction.
∎

5.
Steinberg Schemes of Braids

5.1.

Let
S\subseteq W
be the set of simple reflections in the chosen Coxeter presentation of
W
.
Let
\mathit{Br}_{W}
be the Artin braid group of
W
, and let
\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}\subseteq\mathit{Br}_{W}
be the monoid of positive braids determined by
S
.
Each element
s\in S
lifts to an element
\sigma_{s}\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
, such that
\{\sigma_{s}\}_{s\in S}
is a generating set for
\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
.
The following result is
[
D97
, §1.11]
.

Theorem 5.1.1

(Deligne)
.

There is a map from elements
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
to
G_{0}
-varieties
O(\beta)_{0}
over
\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that:

(1)

O(\sigma_{s})_{0}=O_{s,0}
for all
s\in S
.

(2)

If
\beta=\beta^{\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}
, then there is a fixed isomorphism

(164)

\displaystyle O(\beta)_{0}\xrightarrow{\sim}O(\beta^{\prime})_{0}\times_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}O(\beta^{\prime\prime})_{0},

where the fiber product is formed with respect to the right projection
O(\beta^{\prime})_{0}\to\mathcal{B}_{0}
and the left projection
O(\beta^{\prime\prime})_{0}\to\mathcal{B}_{0}
.
These isomorphisms are associative in the sense that the diagram

(165)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O(\beta^{\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime\prime})_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O(\beta^{\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime})_{0}\times_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}O(\beta^{\prime\prime\prime})_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O(\beta^{\prime})_{0}\times_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}O(\beta^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime\prime})_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O(\beta^{\prime})_{0}\times_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}O(\beta^{\prime\prime})_{0}\times_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}O(\beta^{\prime\prime\prime})_{0}}
\sim
\sim
\sim
\sim

is commutative.

In particular, if
w=s_{1}\cdots s_{\ell}
is a reduced expression, then

(166)

\displaystyle O(\sigma_{w})_{0}\simeq O(\sigma_{s_{1}})_{0}\times_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}\cdots\times_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}O(\sigma_{s_{\ell}})_{0}\simeq O_{w,0}

by way of these fixed isomorphisms.

Deligne attributes the varieties
O(\beta)_{0}
to Broué–Michel
[
BM97
]
, though they are implicit in Lusztig’s earlier papers on character sheaves.
Note that
\dim O(\beta)=|\beta|+N
, where
|\beta|
is the writhe of
\beta
(see §
A.2
).

5.2.

Rouquier introduced an extension of Deligne’s result in which the full group
\mathit{Br}_{W}
replaces the monoid
\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
, and in which objects of
\mathsf{H}_{W}
replace varieties over
\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}
.
The fiber product over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
is replaced by the convolution on
\mathsf{H}_{W}
.

Each element
s\in S
gives rise to objects
\mathcal{R}_{s}^{+},\mathcal{R}_{s}^{-}\in\mathsf{H}_{W}
that can be represented by complexes of the following form:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcllllll}\mathcal{R}_{s}^{+}&=&0&\to&\underline{{\mathit{IC}_{s}}}&\to&\mathit{IC}_{1}\langle 1\rangle,\\[4.30554pt]
\mathcal{R}_{s}^{-}&=&\mathit{IC}_{1}\langle-1\rangle&\to&\underline{{\mathit{IC}_{s}}}&\to&0.\end{array}

Above, the underlined terms are the terms in degree zero.
To describe the morphisms, factor
j_{1}:O_{1,0}\to\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}
as the composition

(169)

\displaystyle O_{1,0}\xrightarrow{i_{s}}\bar{O}_{s,0}\xrightarrow{\bar{j}_{s}}\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}.

Since
O_{1,0}
and
\bar{O}_{s,0}
are smooth and closed in
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{2}
, we have

(170)

\displaystyle\mathit{IC}_{1}

\displaystyle=j_{1,\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\langle-r-N\rangle,

(171)

\displaystyle\mathit{IC}_{s}

\displaystyle=\bar{j}_{s,\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\langle 1-r-N\rangle.

Then the nontrivial morphism in
\mathcal{R}_{s}^{+}
,
resp.

\mathcal{R}_{s}^{-}
, is induced by pushing forward

(172)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\to i_{s,\ast}i_{s}^{\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\sim}i_{s,\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},

(173)

\displaystyle\emph{resp.}\qquad i_{s,\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\langle-2\rangle

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}i_{s,!}i_{s}^{!}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\to\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},

along
\bar{j}_{s}
.

In
[
So
]
, Soergel gave a module-theoretic description of
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B})
(see Remark
2.4.2
), and hence, of
\mathsf{H}_{W}
.
Under it, the complexes
\mathcal{R}_{s}^{+}
and
\mathcal{R}_{s}^{-}
correspond to the complexes of bimodules over
\Sym(\mathbf{V})
defined by Rouquier in §9.2.1 and §9.2.4 of
[
R06
]
.
Thus, Thm. 10.5 of
ibid.
amounts to the following result:

Theorem 5.2.1

(Rouquier)
.

There is a map from elements
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
to objects
\mathcal{R}(\beta)\in\mathsf{H}_{W}
such that:

(1)

\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{s})=\mathcal{R}_{s}^{+}
and
\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{s}^{-1})=\mathcal{R}_{s}^{-}
for all
s\in S
.

(2)

If
\beta=\beta^{\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}
, then there is a fixed isomorphism

(174)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\beta)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{R}(\beta^{\prime})\star\mathcal{R}(\beta^{\prime\prime}).

These isomorphisms are associative in the sense that the diagram

(175)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}(\beta^{\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}(\beta^{\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime})_{0}\star\mathcal{R}(\beta^{\prime\prime\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}(\beta^{\prime})_{0}\star\mathcal{R}(\beta^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}(\beta^{\prime})\star\mathcal{R}(\beta^{\prime\prime})\star\mathcal{R}(\beta^{\prime\prime\prime})}
\sim
\sim
\sim
\sim

commutes.

5.3.

The results of Deligne and Rouquier are related as follows.
For all
w\in W
, let
j_{\beta}
be the map
O(\beta)_{0}\to\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}
.
Let

(176)

\displaystyle\Delta(\beta)

\displaystyle=j_{\beta,!}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\langle|\beta|-r-N\rangle,

(177)

\displaystyle\nabla(\beta)

\displaystyle=j_{\beta,\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\langle|\beta|-r-N\rangle.

In the case where
\beta=\sigma_{s}
, we abbreviate by writing
\Delta_{s}=\Delta(\beta)
and
\nabla_{s}=\nabla(\beta)
.
The following lemma is strongly suggested by the statements in
[
R06
, §11]
, though it does not seem to appear there.

Lemma 5.3.1
.

We have

(178)

\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{R}(\beta))

\displaystyle\simeq\Delta(\beta),

(179)

\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{R}(\beta^{-1}))

\displaystyle\simeq\nabla(\beta)

for all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
.

Proof.

The explicit definitions of
\mathcal{R}_{s}^{+}
and
