\mathcal{R}_{s}^{-}
show that

(180)

\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{R}_{s}^{+})

\displaystyle\simeq\Delta_{s},

(181)

\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{R}_{s}^{-})

\displaystyle\simeq\nabla_{s}

for all
s\in S
.
We now apply the commutativity of (
79
).
∎

5.4.

We define
G_{0}
-schemes
G(\beta)_{0}
,
\mathcal{U}(\beta)_{0}
,
\mathcal{Z}(\beta)_{0}
by stipulating that all squares in the commutative diagram below are cartesian.

(182)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O(\beta)_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\beta)_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}(\beta)_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}(\beta)_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}}
\mathit{pr}_{0}\times\mathit{pr}_{\ell}
\mathit{act}
i\times\id
p\times\id

Let
\mathbf{1}\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
denote the identity braid.
Then
\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{1})_{0}=\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0}
, the Springer resolution, and
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{1})_{0}=\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, the Steinberg scheme from the previous section.

Definition 5.4.1
.

For all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
, we say that
\mathcal{Z}(\beta)_{0}
is the
Steinberg scheme
attached to
\beta
.

In what follows, let
p_{\beta}
be the composition
\mathcal{U}(\beta)_{0}\to\mathcal{U}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}\to\mathcal{U}_{0}
.
Note that
p_{\mathbf{1},!}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\simeq p_{\mathbf{1},\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\simeq\mathcal{S}
.
We can now prove Theorem
2
, relating the
\mathbf{A}_{W}
trace to the generalized Steinberg schemes
\mathcal{Z}(\beta)_{0}
.

Theorem 5.4.2
.

For all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
and
i,j
, we have:

(183)

\displaystyle\mathsf{AH}^{i,j}(\mathcal{R}(\beta))

\displaystyle\simeq\gr_{j+r}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i+r}(p_{\beta,!}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}),

(184)

\displaystyle\mathsf{AH}^{i,j}(\mathcal{R}(\beta^{-1}))

\displaystyle\simeq\gr_{j+r}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i+r}(p_{\beta,\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}).

In particular, since
\mathcal{S}=p_{\ast}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\langle-r\rangle
, we have

(185)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{AH}^{i,j}(\mathcal{R}(\beta))&\simeq\gr_{j+2r}^{\bm{w}}\mathrm{H}_{-(i+2r)}^{!,G}(\mathcal{Z}(\beta))\end{split}

by Lemma
4.3.1
.

Proof.

By the commutativity of (
122
), Lemma
5.3.1
, and both smooth and proper base change,

(186)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{AH}^{i,j}(\mathcal{R}(\beta))&\simeq\gr_{j}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i}(\mathsf{K}^{b}(i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle\circ\mathsf{CH}\circ\rho)(R(\beta)),\mathcal{S})\\
&\simeq\gr_{j}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i}((i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle\circ\mathsf{CH})(\Delta(\beta)),\mathcal{S})\\
&\simeq\gr_{j+r}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i+r}(p_{\beta,!}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}).\end{split}

The argument for
\mathcal{R}(\beta^{-1})
is completely analogous.
The remaining statement follows from Lemma
4.3.1
.
∎

6.
Khovanov–Rozansky Homology

6.1.

A link in a
3
-manifold is the image of a disjoint union of finitely many circles under a smooth embedding.
The HOMFLY series of a link in
S^{3}
is a bivariate Laurent series that only depends on its isotopy class
[
HOMFLY
]
.

Dunfield–Gukov–Rasmussen predicted
[
DGR
]
, and Khovanov–Rozansky constructed
[
KR
]
, a categorification of the HOMFLY series, sending a link in
3
-space to a triply-graded vector space over
\mathbf{Q}
.
This isotopy invariant is now known as
HOMFLY
or
Khovanov–Rozansky homology
.
The original construction used the homotopy theory of matrix factorizations.
In
[
Kh
]
, Khovanov gave an alternative approach via the Hochschild homology of Soergel bimodules for the groups
W=S_{n}
.
Via Soergel’s Erweiterungssatz
[
So
, Thms. 15-17]
, the output of his construction (after base change from
\mathbf{Q}
to
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
) is equivalent to a functor

(187)

\displaystyle\mathsf{HHH}:\mathsf{H}_{W}\to\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{Vect}_{2})\subseteq\mathsf{Vect}_{3},

where
\mathsf{Vect}_{d}
denotes the category of
\mathbf{Z}^{d}
-graded
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-vector spaces.
Khovanov’s construction extends to Soergel bimodules for any Coxeter group, so in particular,
\mathsf{HHH}
can be defined on the Hecke category of any such group.

Remark 6.1.1
.

To dispel any ambiguity from our conventions, we state explicitly:
If
\beta
is a topological braid on
n=r+1
strands, and
\hat{\beta}
is the link closure of
\beta
, then

(188)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\beta)={(at)^{|\beta|}}a^{-r}\sum_{i,j,k}{(a^{2}\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}t)^{r-i}}\bm{q}^{\frac{j}{2}}t^{k}\dim\mathsf{HHH}^{i,i+j,k}(\mathcal{R}(\beta))

is an isotopy invariant of
\hat{\beta}
that equals
1
when
\hat{\beta}
is the unknot.
It matches the invariant in
[
DGR
]
once we substitute
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}=q
.
In particular, for
\beta=\mathbf{1}
, we have

(189)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{1})=\left(\frac{\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{a}\left(\frac{1+a^{2}t}{1-\bm{q}}\right)\right)^{r}.

Compare to Example
6.2.2
, below.

6.2.

In
[
WW08
]
, Webster–Williamson interpreted the Hochschild homology of a Soergel bimodule as the hypercohomology of a sum of shifted pure perverse sheaves over
G
.
In subsequent papers
[
WW11
,
WW17
]
, they showed how to interpret
\mathsf{HHH}^{\ast,\ast,\ast}
in a similar way, except in terms of a
complex
of such objects.
We will rewrite their work in our notation.

For any
G_{0}
-variety
X_{0}
over
\mathbf{F}
, we write

(190)

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{G}^{\ast}(X,-)\vcentcolon=\underline{\Hom}^{\ast}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},-):\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G,m}^{b}(X_{0})\to\mathsf{Mod}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[F]),

where
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[F]
is concentrated in degree zero.
In other words,
\mathbf{H}_{G}^{\ast}(X,K)
is the hypercohomology of
\xi K\in\underline{\mathsf{D}}_{G}^{b}(G)
, implicitly endowed with the Frobenius action coming from
K
.
With this notation, the theorem below summarizes the main results of
[
WW08
]
and
[
WW17
]
.

Theorem 6.2.1

(Webster–Williamson)
.

We have

(191)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{HHH}^{i,i+j,k}\simeq\mathrm{H}^{k}\circ\mathsf{K}^{b}(\gr_{i+j}^{\bm{w}}\mathbf{H}_{G}^{j}(G,(\kappa\circ\mathsf{CH})(-)))\end{split}

for all
i,j,k\in\mathbf{Z}
.
In particular, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{HHH}^{i,i+j,k}(\mathit{IC}_{w,0}[-n]_{\triangle})

\displaystyle\simeq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\gr_{i+j}^{\bm{w}}\mathbf{H}_{G}^{j}(G,\mathsf{CH}(\mathit{IC}_{w}))&k=n\\
0&k\neq n\end{array}\right.

for all
w\in W
and
n\in\mathbf{Z}
.

Example 6.2.2
.

Below, we review the calculation of
\mathsf{HHH}(\mathit{IC}_{1})\simeq\gr_{\ast}^{\bm{w}}\mathbf{H}_{G}^{\ast}(G,\mathcal{G})
.
Since
\mathcal{G}
is the pushforward of the constant sheaf on the Grothendieck–Springer resolution, it suffices to calculate the
G
-equivariant cohomology of
\tilde{G}
together with its weight filtration.

Fix a maximal torus
T_{0}\subseteq G_{0}
and a Borel
B_{0}\supseteq T_{0}
.
By Lemma
4.5.2
,
[\tilde{G}_{0}/G_{0}]\simeq[B_{0}/B_{0}]
, where
B_{0}
acts on itself by conjugation.
Therefore, there are weight-preserving isomorphisms

(194)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{H}_{G}^{j}(G,\mathcal{G})&\simeq\mathrm{H}_{B}^{j}(B,\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})\\
&\simeq\mathrm{H}_{T}^{j}(T,\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})\\
&\simeq\bigoplus_{i}{\mathrm{H}_{T}^{j-i}(\mathit{pt},\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})\otimes\mathrm{H}^{i}(T,\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})}.\end{split}

Above, Frobenius acts by
q^{\frac{j-i}{2}}
on
\mathrm{H}_{T}^{j-i}(\mathit{pt})
and by
q^{i}
on
\mathrm{H}^{i}(T)
, so it acts by
q^{\frac{i+j}{2}}
on the
i
th summand of the last expression.
We deduce that

(195)

\displaystyle\gr_{i+j}^{\bm{w}}\mathbf{H}_{G}^{j}(G,\mathcal{G})\simeq\mathrm{H}_{T}^{j-i}(\mathit{pt},\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})\otimes\mathrm{H}^{i}(T,\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}).

The proof of
[
WW11
, Prop. 11]
shows that this isomorphism is
W
-equivariant with respect to:

(1)

The
W
-action on the left-hand side induced by
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W]\subseteq\mathbf{A}_{W}\simeq\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{G}}
,
i.e.
, the Springer action arising from the monodromy of
\mathcal{G}
over the regular semisimple locus of
G
.

(2)

The
W
-action on the right-hand side induced by
\mathrm{H}_{T}^{j-i}(\mathit{pt},\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})\simeq\Sym^{\frac{j-i}{2}}(\mathbf{V})
and
\mathrm{H}^{i}(T,\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})\simeq\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V})
.

6.3.

In the rest of this section, we prove Theorem
3
, stating that Khovanov–Rozansky homology is a summand of the
\mathbf{A}_{W}
trace.

In what follows, recall from Section
2
that for all
\phi\in\hat{W}
, we write
V_{\phi}
for an irreducible representation of
W
that affords the character
\phi
, endowed with the trivial action of Frobenius.

Lemma 6.3.1
.

Let
K\in\mathsf{C}_{\hat{W}}(G_{0})
be an indecomposable shifted perverse sheaf, and write
K=\mathcal{G}_{\phi}\otimes M
, where
\phi\in\hat{W}
and
M
is the pullback of a pure weight-zero object of
\mathsf{D}_{G,m}^{b}(\mathit{pt}_{0})
.
Then we have
W
-equivariant isomorphisms:

(196)

\displaystyle\gr_{i+j}^{\bm{w}}\mathbf{H}_{G}^{j}(G,K)

\displaystyle\simeq\Hom_{W}(V_{\phi},{(\Sym^{\frac{j-i}{2}}\otimes{\Alt^{i}})}(\mathbf{V}))\otimes\xi M,

(197)

\displaystyle\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{0}(K,\mathcal{G}\langle i\rangle)

\displaystyle\simeq\Hom_{W}(V_{\phi}^{\vee},\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W]\otimes\Sym^{\frac{j}{2}}(\mathbf{V}))\otimes\xi M^{\vee}

\displaystyle\simeq V_{\phi}\otimes\Sym^{\frac{i}{2}}(\mathbf{V})\otimes\xi M^{\vee}.

Moreover, these isomorphisms are functorial in
K
.

Proof.

By Example
6.2.2
, there is a
W
-equivariant isomorphism

(198)

\displaystyle\gr_{i+j}^{\bm{w}}\mathbf{H}_{G}^{j}(G,\mathcal{G})\simeq{(\Sym^{\frac{j-i}{2}}\otimes\Alt^{i})}(\mathbf{V}),

which yields (
196
).
Next, Theorems
3.3.1
and
1
give a
W
-equivariant isomorphism

(199)

\displaystyle\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{0}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}\langle i\rangle)\simeq\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W]\otimes\Sym^{\frac{i}{2}}(\mathbf{V}),

which yields (
197
).
Functoriality is a consequence of the fact that
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{G}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{S}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{A}_{W}
is an isomorphism of algebras, not just of vector spaces.
∎

Lemma 6.3.2
.

We have

(200)

\displaystyle(\gr_{i+j}^{\bm{w}}\mathbf{H}_{G}^{j}(G,-))^{\vee}\simeq\Hom_{W}(\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V}),\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}{\underline{\Hom}^{0}(-,\mathcal{G}\langle j\rangle)})

as contravariant functors
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(G_{0})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Vect}_{0}
.

Proof.

By the preceding lemma and the self-duality of
\Sym^{\ast}(\mathbf{V})
as a representation of
W
, the statement holds with
\underline{\mathsf{C}}_{\hat{W}}(G_{0})
in place of
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(G_{0})
.
Now apply Theorem
2.11.1
, observing that the constant sheaf is a summand of
\mathcal{G}
.
∎

Lemma 6.3.3
.

We have

(201)

\displaystyle\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}{\underline{\Hom}^{0}(-,\mathcal{G}\langle j\rangle)}

\displaystyle\simeq\underline{\Hom}^{0}(i^{\ast}(-)\langle-r\rangle,\mathcal{S}\langle j\rangle)

as contravariant functors
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(G_{0})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Mod}_{0}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W])
.

Proof.

By Theorem
1
, the statement holds with
\underline{\mathsf{C}}_{\hat{W}}(G_{0})
in place of
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(G_{0})
.
Now apply Theorem
2.11.1
, observing that
i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle
is compatible with the functors
\mathsf{PR}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3
.

The theorem claims that

(202)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{HHH}^{i,i+j,k})^{\vee}

\displaystyle\simeq\Hom_{W}(\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V}),\mathsf{AH}^{j+k,j}(-))

as contravariant functors on
\mathsf{H}_{W}=\mathsf{K}^{b}(\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}))
.
For ease of notation, let

(203)

\displaystyle\Phi^{j}

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\underline{\mathsf{C}}(G_{0})}(i^{\ast}(-)\langle-r\rangle,\mathcal{S}\langle j\rangle),

(204)

\displaystyle\emph{resp.}\qquad\Psi^{i,j}

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(G_{0}))}(i^{\ast}(-)\langle-r\rangle,\mathcal{S}\langle j\rangle[i-j]_{\triangle}),

as a functor
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(G_{0})\to\mathsf{Mod}_{0}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W])
,
resp.

\mathsf{K}^{b}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}(G_{0}))\to\mathsf{Mod}_{0}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W])
.
By Lemma
2.11.2
, we have
\kappa\circ i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle\simeq i^{\ast}\langle-r\rangle\circ\kappa
as functors on
\mathsf{C}(G_{0})
, and therefore,

(205)

\displaystyle\mathsf{AH}^{j+k,j}

\displaystyle\simeq\Psi^{j+k,j}\circ\mathsf{K}^{b}(\kappa\circ\mathsf{CH}).

At the same time, we have

(206)

\displaystyle\Psi^{j+k,j}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{H}^{k}\circ\mathsf{K}^{b}\Phi^{j}.

Furthermore, by Lemmas
2.10.2
and
6.3.3
,

(207)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi^{j}&\simeq\underline{\Hom}^{0}(i^{\ast}(-)\langle-r\rangle,\mathcal{S}\langle j\rangle)\simeq\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{0}(-,\mathcal{G}\langle j\rangle).\end{split}

Combining these equivalences with Lemma
6.3.2
, we compute

(208)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Hom_{W}(\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V}),\mathsf{AH}^{j+k,j}(-))\\
&\simeq\Hom_{W}(\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V}),\mathrm{H}^{k}\circ\mathsf{K}^{b}(\Phi^{j}\circ\kappa\circ\mathsf{CH}))\\
&\simeq\Hom_{W}(\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V}),\mathrm{H}^{k}\circ\mathsf{K}^{b}(\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{0}((\kappa\circ\mathsf{CH})(-),\mathcal{G}\langle j\rangle)))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{H}^{k}\circ\mathsf{K}^{b}(\Hom_{W}(\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V}),\gr_{0}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{0}((\kappa\circ\mathsf{CH})(-),\mathcal{G}\langle j\rangle)))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{H}^{k}\circ\mathsf{K}^{b}((\gr_{i+j}^{\bm{w}}\mathbf{H}_{G}^{j}(G,(\kappa\circ\mathsf{CH})(-)))^{\vee}).\end{split}

The last expression is equivalent to
(\mathsf{HHH}^{i,i+j,k})^{\vee}
by Theorem
6.2.1
.
∎

6.4.

Let
\mathcal{X}(\beta)
be defined by the cartesian square:

(209)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
p_{\beta}

Proof of Corollary
4
.

We claim that for all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
, we have

(210)

\displaystyle\mathsf{HHH}^{0,j,k}(\mathcal{R}(\beta))^{\vee}

\displaystyle\simeq\gr_{j+2r}^{\bm{w}}\mathrm{H}_{-(j+k+2r)}^{!,G}(\mathcal{U}(\beta)),

(211)

\displaystyle\mathsf{HHH}^{r,r+j,k}(\mathcal{R}(\beta))^{\vee}

\displaystyle\simeq\gr_{j+2(r-N)}^{\bm{w}}\mathrm{H}_{-(j+k+2(r-N))}^{!,G}(\mathcal{X}(\beta)).

Let
1
and
\varepsilon
be the trivial and sign characters of
W
, respectively.
Then
1
,
resp.

\varepsilon
, is the character of
\Alt^{0}(\mathbf{V})
,
resp.

\Alt^{r}(\mathbf{V})
, so Theorems
3
and
5.4.2
give us

(212)

\displaystyle\mathsf{HHH}^{0,j,k}(\mathcal{R}(\beta))^{\vee}

\displaystyle\simeq\gr_{j+r}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{j+k+r}(p_{\beta,!}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{1})

(213)

\displaystyle\mathsf{HHH}^{r,r+j,k}(\mathcal{R}(\beta))^{\vee}

\displaystyle\simeq\gr_{j+r}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{j+k+r}(p_{\beta,!}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}).

By Springer theory, we know that:

•

\mathcal{S}_{1}
is the sheaf
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\langle-r\rangle
over
\mathcal{U}
.

•

\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}
is the skyscraper sheaf at
1\in\mathcal{U}
with stalk
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\langle-r-2N\rangle
.

Now, both of the desired isomorphisms follow from Lemma
4.3.1
.
∎

7.
The Decategorified Trace

7.1.

To state the main definition of this section, let
W
denote an
arbitrary
finite Coxeter group.

As before,
H_{W}
is the Iwahori–Hecke algebra of
W
.
Moreover, we will draw freely upon the notation introduced in Appendix
A
:

•

R(W)
is the representation ring of
W
.

•

\mathbf{Q}_{W}\subseteq\bar{\mathbf{Q}}
is the splitting field of
W
.

•

(-,-)_{W}:R(W)\times R(W)\to\mathbf{Z}
be the multiplicity pairing.

•

We write

(214)

\displaystyle{[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}}=\sum_{i}{\bm{q}^{i}}\Sym^{i}(\mathbf{V}).

•

If
\phi:W\to\mathbf{Q}_{W}
is an irreducible character, then
\phi_{\bm{q}}:H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})\to\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
is the corresponding
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-linear trace.

•

\{-,-\}:\hat{W}\times\hat{W}\to\mathbf{Q}_{W}
is the symmetric pairing (derived from) Lusztig’s exotic Fourier transform.

Definition 7.1.1
.

For any finite Coxeter group
W
, the
R(W)
trace
on its Iwahori–Hecke algebra is the
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-linear trace

(215)

\displaystyle\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}:H_{W}\to R(W)\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})

defined by

(216)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}=\sum_{\phi,\psi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}\phi_{\bm{q}}(\beta)\psi

for all
\beta\in H_{W}
.

In the next two subsections, we assume that
W
is still a Weyl group and prove Theorem
5
, which relates the
R(W)
trace to the decategorification of the
\mathbf{A}_{W}
trace.
In the remainder of the section, we allow
W
to be an arbitrary finite Coxeter group and relate the
R(W)
trace to the Markov trace studied by Ocneanu and Gomi.
We conclude by proving Propositions
7.5.1
and
7.5.5
, which roughly generalize rationality and symmetry properties of the Markov trace.

7.2.

Recall that an object of
\mathsf{Mod}_{2}^{b}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W])
is a
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W]
-module equipped with a
W
-invariant bigrading where the first grading is bounded below and the second is bounded.
We have an additive map

(217)

\displaystyle\mathsf{AH}:H_{W}\xrightarrow{\sim}[\mathsf{H}_{W}]_{\triangle}\to[\mathsf{Mod}_{2}^{b}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W])]_{\oplus}

induced by the composition

(218)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{W}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{AH}}\mathsf{Mod}_{2}^{b}(\mathbf{A}_{W})\to\mathsf{Mod}_{2}^{b}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W]),

where the second arrow is the forgetful functor.
Note that
\mathsf{Mod}_{2}^{b}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W])
is semisimple, since
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W]
is.

For any ring
R
, such as
R=R(W)
, we write
R(\!(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})\!)=R[\![\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}]\!][\frac{1}{\bm{q}}]
.
Let

(219)

\displaystyle[-]_{\bm{q}}:[\mathsf{Mod}_{1,1}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[W])]_{\oplus}\to R(W)(\!(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})\!)

be the additive map defined by

(220)

\displaystyle[M]_{\bm{q}}=\sum_{i,j}{(-1)^{i-j}}\bm{q}^{\frac{j}{2}}M^{i,j}.

We say that
[M]_{\bm{q}}
is the
\bm{q}
-graded character
of
M
.
For all
i,j
, let

(221)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{AH}^{\vee})^{i,j}(-)=\Hom_{\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}}(\mathsf{AH}^{i,j}(-),\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}).

Then the composition

(222)

\displaystyle[\mathsf{AH}^{\vee}]_{\bm{q}}\vcentcolon=[-]_{\bm{q}}\circ\mathsf{AH}^{\vee}:H_{W}\to R(W)(\!(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})\!)

is
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-linear.

7.3.

Recall from Section
2
that
\{\gamma_{w}\}_{w}
denotes the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis of the Iwahori–Hecke algebra.
Then Theorem
5
reduces to the claim that:

(223)

\displaystyle[\mathsf{AH}^{\vee}(\mathit{IC}_{w})]_{\bm{q}}=\langle{\gamma_{w}}\rangle_{\bm{q}}\cdot[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}

for all
w\in W
.
To prove it, we use a multiplicity formula for unipotent character sheaves, derived from
[
L85-III
, Cor. 14.11]
and
[
L85-V
, Thm. 23.1]
.

As in §
2.5
, let
[\mathsf{C}(G_{0})]_{\oplus}
be endowed with the action of
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}]
in which
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}
acts by
\langle-1\rangle
.
The following version of the formula is the one quoted in
[
WW08
, 413]
, fixed to incorporate semisimplification.

Theorem 7.3.1

(Lusztig)
.

For all
w\in W
, we have

(224)

\displaystyle{[\mathsf{CH}(\mathit{IC}_{w})^{ss}]}=\sum_{\phi,\psi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}\phi_{\bm{q}}(\gamma_{w})[\mathcal{G}_{\psi}]+\text{cuspidal terms}

in
[\mathsf{C}(G_{0})]_{\oplus}
, where “cuspidal terms” refers to the contribution of simple objects not of the form
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}
for some
\psi\in\hat{W}
, and “
ss
” refers to the semisimplification of (shifted) perverse summands.

Proof of Theorem
5
.

By an argument similar to the proof of the second equivalence in Lemma
6.3.1
, Theorem
3.3.1
implies that for all
i,j\in\mathbf{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\gr_{j}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i}(\mathcal{S}_{\psi},\mathcal{S})\simeq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}V_{\psi}\otimes\Sym^{\frac{i}{2}}(\mathbf{V})&i=j\\
0&i\neq j\end{array}\right.

as representations of
W
.
So Theorem
7.3.1
yields

(227)

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\mathsf{AH}^{\vee}(\mathit{IC}_{w})]_{\bm{q}}&=\sum_{i,j}{(-1)^{i-j}}\bm{q}^{\frac{j}{2}}\gr_{j}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i}(i^{\ast}\mathsf{CH}(\mathit{IC}_{w})\langle-r\rangle,\mathcal{S})^{\vee}\\
&=\sum_{i,j}{(-1)^{i-j}}\bm{q}^{\frac{j}{2}}\sum_{\phi,\psi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}\phi_{\bm{q}}(\gamma_{w})\gr_{j}^{\bm{w}}\underline{\Hom}^{i}(\mathcal{S}_{\psi},\mathcal{S})^{\vee}\\
&=\sum_{\phi,\psi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}\phi_{\bm{q}}(\gamma_{w})\sum_{i}{\bm{q}^{\frac{i}{2}}}\,(\psi\otimes\Sym^{\frac{i}{2}}(\mathbf{V}))^{\vee}.\end{split}

Every representation of
W
is self-dual because
\mathbf{Q}_{W}=\mathbf{Q}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
, so the inner sum in the last expression simplifies to
\psi\cdot[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}
.
∎

7.4.

We now relate
\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}
to Markov traces.

The
HOMFLY series
of a link
\lambda\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{3}
is one of the predecessors of the isotopy invariant mentioned in Remark
6.1.1
.
We will denote it by

(228)

\displaystyle[\lambda]_{a,\bm{q}}\in\mathbf{Z}[(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{\pm 1},a^{\pm 1}].

We have
[\lambda]_{a,\bm{q}}=\mathbf{P}(\lambda)|_{t=-1}
, where
\mathbf{P}
is the invariant in the remark.
With this normalization, the HOMFLY series of the unknot equals
1
.

There are many different constructions of the HOMFLY invariant
[
HOMFLY
]
.
Ocneanu’s approach relies on Alexander’s theorem, stating that every link in
\mathbf{R}^{3}
is the closure of a braid.
Namely, one constructs for each
n\geq 1
a certain function on the Iwahori–Hecke algebra of
S_{n}
, now known as a Markov trace
[
J
]
.
If
\mathit{Br}_{n}
denotes the braid group of
S_{n}
,
i.e.
, the group of topological braids on
n
strands, and
\lambda
is the closure of
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{n}
, then the HOMFLY series of
\lambda
is the Markov trace of
\beta
, multiplied by a factor depending only on
|\beta|
and
n
.

Y. Gomi generalized Ocneanu’s Markov traces in a uniform way beyond type
A
, extending work of Geck–Lambropoulou in type
BC

[
G
]
.
We follow Gomi’s conventions here.

Let
(W,S)
be any finite Coxeter system.
Recall from §
A.4
that if
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
and
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
is the
S
-parabolic subgroup generated by
S^{\prime}
, then
H_{W^{\prime}}\subseteq H_{W}
.
A
Markov trace
on
H_{W}
is a
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-linear function

(229)

\displaystyle\mathsf{tr}:H_{W}\to\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}](a)

such that:

(1)

\mathsf{tr}(1)=1
.

(2)

For all
\beta,\gamma\in H_{W}
, we have
\mathsf{tr}(\beta\gamma)=\mathsf{tr}(\gamma\beta)
.

(3)

For all
s\in S
and
\gamma\in H_{W^{\prime}}
, where
W^{\prime}
is the
S
-parabolic subgroup of
W
generated by
S\setminus s
, we have

(230)

\displaystyle\mathsf{tr}(\sigma_{s}^{\pm 1}\gamma)=-a^{\mp 1}\left(\frac{\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{a-a^{-1}}\right)\mathsf{tr}(\gamma).

Axioms (2) and (3) are respectively known as the first and second Markov moves.

Theorem 7.4.1

(Ocneanu)
.

The axioms above uniquely define a Markov trace on the Iwahori–Hecke algebra of
S_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
.
For all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{n}
, we have

(231)

\displaystyle[\hat{\beta}]_{a,\bm{q}}=(-a)^{|\beta|}\left(\frac{a-a^{-1}}{\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\right)^{n-1}\mathsf{tr}(\beta),

where
\hat{\beta}
is the link closure of
\beta
.

Suppose that a Markov trace on
H_{W}
exists.
After base change from
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
to
\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
, we can view it as a trace on
H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
(see §
A.7
).
Then

(232)

\displaystyle\mathsf{tr}=\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}\mathsf{tr}_{\phi}{\phi_{\bm{q}}}

for some weights
\mathsf{tr}_{\phi}\in\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}},a)
.

Axioms (1)-(3) show that
\mathsf{tr}|_{a^{-2}=0}
is the symmetrizing trace
\tau_{\bm{q}}:H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})\to\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
.
Proposition
A.9.3
then suggests that the weights
\mathsf{tr}_{\phi}
should involve bivariate Molien series.
These observations motivate the form of Gomi’s Markov weights.
Below, let
\mathbf{V}
be a realization of
W
such that
\mathbf{V}^{W}=0
, and for all
\psi\in\hat{W}
, let

(233)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bm{m}_{\psi}(\bm{q},x)&=\sum_{i,j}{(-x)^{i}}\bm{q}^{j}(\psi,\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V})\otimes\Sym^{j}(\mathbf{V}))_{W}.\end{split}

By construction,
\bm{m}_{\psi}(\bm{q},0)=\bm{m}_{\psi}(\bm{q})
, the usual Molien series of
\psi
.

Theorem 7.4.2

(Gomi)
.

The weights

(234)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{tr}_{\phi}&=\left(\frac{1-\bm{q}}{1-a^{-2}}\right)^{r}\sum_{\psi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}\bm{m}_{\psi}(\bm{q},a^{-2})\end{split}

define a Markov trace on
H_{W}
for any finite Coxeter group
W
of rank
r
.

This is a bivariate refinement of Proposition
A.9.3
.

Henceforth,
\mathsf{tr}
denotes Gomi’s Markov trace.
To relate it to the
R(W)
trace, we introduce the normalization

(235)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}={(-\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|}}\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}\cdot\varepsilon[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}},

where
\varepsilon
denotes the sign character of
W
(see §
A.5
).
We emphasize that whenever we use
\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
,
we always assume that
\mathbf{V}^{W}=0
.
For Weyl groups, the result below follows from Theorems
6.2.1
and
5
.

Proposition 7.4.3
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
7.4.2
, we have

(236)

\displaystyle\mathsf{tr}(\beta)=(-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|}\left(\frac{1-\bm{q}}{1-a^{2}}\right)^{r}\sum_{i}{(-a^{2})^{i}}(\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V}),\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0})_{W}

for all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
.

Lemma 7.4.4
.

We have

(237)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-x^{-1})^{r}}\sum_{i}{(-x^{-1})^{i}}\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V})=\frac{\varepsilon}{(1-x)^{r}}\sum_{i}{(-x)^{i}}\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V})

in
R(W)(x)
.

Proof.

Use the identity
\Alt^{r-i}(\mathbf{V})=\varepsilon\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V})
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.4.3
.

By Lemma
7.4.4
and the self-duality of
\Sym(\mathbf{V})
,

(238)

\displaystyle\mathsf{tr}_{\phi}=\left(\frac{1-\bm{q}}{1-a^{2}}\right)^{r}\sum_{\psi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}\sum_{i,j}{(-a^{2})^{i}}\bm{q}^{j}(\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V}),\varepsilon\psi\cdot\Sym^{j}(\mathbf{V}))_{W}.

Comparing with the terms that make up
\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
, we get the result.
∎

Corollary 7.4.5
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
7.4.2
, we have

(239)

\displaystyle\tau_{\bm{q}}=(-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|}(1-\bm{q})^{r}(\varepsilon,\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0})_{W}

as
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-linear traces on
H_{W}
.

Corollary 7.4.6
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
7.4.1
, we have

(240)

\displaystyle{[\hat{\beta}]_{a,\bm{q}}}=(a\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|-n+1}\sum_{0\leq i\leq n-1}{(-a^{2})^{i}}(\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V}),\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0})_{W}

for all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{n}
.

7.5.

To conclude this section, we prove:

(1)

Proposition
7.5.1
, a rationality result that will simplify the proof of Theorem
9
in Section
8
.

(2)

Proposition
7.5.5
, a symmetry result.
Together with (1) and Theorem
9
, it implies Theorem
6
, as we will also explain.

These results about the
R(W)
trace respectively generalize, and are inspired by, the following facts about the HOMFLY series:

(1
′
)

HOMFLY has integer coefficients.
Moreover, it is a
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}
-monomial shift of an element of
\mathbf{Z}[\![\bm{q}]\!][a^{\pm 1}]
, not just of
\mathbf{Z}[\![\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}]\!][a^{\pm 1}]
.

(2
′
)

HOMFLY is invariant under
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

Note that the second claim of (1
′
) can be proved via the Markov trace, while (2
′
) is a consequence of the HOMFLY skein relation (see
[
DGR
, §2.4]
, noting that their
q
is our
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}
).
By contrast, the proofs below are representation-theoretic.
Throughout, we assume that the realization
\mathbf{V}
satisfies
\mathbf{V}^{W}=0
.

7.5.1.

Let
d_{1},\ldots,d_{r}
be the invariant degrees of the
W
-action on
\mathbf{V}
.
Let:

\displaystyle\bm{q}_{0}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}&\text{$W$ has a factor of type $E_{7}$ or $E_{8}$}\\
\bm{q}&\text{else}\end{array}\right.

The reason we make an exception for
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
is the existence of so-called exceptional characters in these types.
See
[
GP
, §9.5]
and the references therein.

Proposition 7.5.1
.

For all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
, we have

(243)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}

\displaystyle\in(\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|}R(W)[\bm{q}_{0}],

(244)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}

\displaystyle\in\frac{1}{(1-\bm{q}^{d_{1}})\cdots(1-\bm{q}^{d_{r}})}\,R(W)[\bm{q}_{0}],

(245)

\displaystyle(1,\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0})_{W}

\displaystyle\in\frac{1}{(1-\bm{q}^{d_{1}})\cdots(1-\bm{q}^{d_{r}})}\,(1+\bm{q}_{0}\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}_{0}]).

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\beta}=\bm{q}^{\frac{|\beta|}{2}}\beta
.
To prove the first statement, we must show that

(246)

\displaystyle(\psi,\langle{\tilde{\beta}}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}=\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}\phi_{\bm{q}}(\tilde{\beta})\in\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})

actually belongs to
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}_{0}]
for all
\psi\in\hat{W}
.
We make a series of reductions:

By Lemma-Postulate
A.9.1
(1), we can assume that
W
is irreducible.

We can expand
\tilde{\beta}
as a
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}]
-linear combination of the elements
\tilde{\sigma}_{w}=\bm{q}^{\frac{|\sigma_{w}|}{2}}\sigma_{w}
for
w\in W
.
So we can assume that
\tilde{\beta}=\tilde{\sigma}_{w}
for some
w
.

We can now check the non-crystallographic types case by case, using
[
GP
, §8.3]
and
[
L94
]
for the dihedral types,
[
GP
, Table 9.1]
and
[
BM93
, §7]
for type
H_{3}
(but see Remark
7.5.4
, below), and
[
AL
,
M
]
for type
H_{4}
.
So we can assume that
W
is crystallographic.

Theorem
5
implies that
(\psi,\langle{\tilde{\beta}}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}\in\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
.
So it remains to show that
(\psi,\langle{\tilde{\beta}}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}\in\mathbf{Q}[\bm{q}_{0}]
.
Since
\{-,-\}
takes values in
\mathbf{Q}_{W}=\mathbf{Q}
, it is enough to get
\phi_{\bm{q}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{w})\in\mathbf{Q}[\bm{q}_{0}]
for all
\phi
.
For crystallographic irreducible
W
, this is a theorem of Benson–Curtis
[
BC
]
(joint with Springer in the case of
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
).

The remaining two statements follow from the first statement, once we establish the following two lemmas.
∎

Lemma 7.5.2
.

We have

(247)

\displaystyle{[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}}\in\frac{1}{(1-\bm{q}^{d_{1}})\cdots(1-\bm{q}^{d_{r}})}\,R(W)[\bm{q}].

Proof.

In the notation of §
A.6
and §
A.8
, we have

(248)

\displaystyle{[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}}=\bm{m}_{1}(\bm{q})\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}{\mathrm{Feg}_{\phi}(\bm{q})\phi},

where
\bm{m}_{1}(\bm{q})=\prod_{i}{(1-\bm{q}^{d_{i}})^{-1}}
and
\mathrm{Feg}_{\phi}(\bm{q})\in\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}]
for each
\phi
.
∎

Lemma 7.5.3
.

For all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
, we have
(\varepsilon,\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}=\varepsilon_{\bm{q}}(\beta)=(-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|}
.

Proof.

By
[
GP
, 252]
, we have
\varepsilon_{\bm{q}}(\sigma_{s})=-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
for all
s\in S
.
(Note that
T_{s}=\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma_{s}
in the notation of
ibid.
.)
But
\varepsilon_{\bm{q}}
is the character of a
1
-dimensional
\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
-vector space, so it is multiplicative on
\mathit{Br}_{W}
.
We deduce that
\varepsilon_{\bm{q}}(\beta)=(-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|}
for all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
.

As for the other equality:
By
[
L84-1
, Ch. 4]
, we have
\{\phi,\varepsilon\}=0
for all
\phi\neq\varepsilon
.
This implies
(\varepsilon,\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}=\varepsilon_{\bm{q}}(\beta)
.
∎

Remark 7.5.4
.

The proof of Proposition
7.5.1
uses the explicit description of the exotic Fourier transform in type
H_{3}
.
For this group, there are three nontrivial families (see §
A.10
), one of which is exceptional.
The
4\times 4
matrix in
[
BM93
, §7]
represents the block of the Fourier matrix for the non-exceptional families.
I learned from George Lusztig that by contrast, the data for the exceptional family matches that of the exceptional family in type
E_{7}
.
The assertion in
[
GM
, 189]
that all three families have the same Fourier data is incorrect.

7.5.2.

Turning to the symmetry result:

Proposition 7.5.5
.

For all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
, we have

(249)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}

\displaystyle\in R(W)[\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\varepsilon\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}],

(250)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}

\displaystyle\in(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|-r}R(W)[(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{\pm 1}].

By Proposition
7.5.1
,
\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}
takes values in
R(W)[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
, so the proof amounts to the following lemmas:

Lemma 7.5.6
.

For all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
, we have
\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}\mapsto\varepsilon\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}
under
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

Proof.

Recall that for all
\beta\in H_{W}
, we have
(\psi,\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}=\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}\phi_{\bm{q}}(\beta)
.
Using Lemma-Postulate
A.9.1
(3), we compute

(251)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\psi,\varepsilon\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}&=(\varepsilon\psi,\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}\\
&=\sum_{\phi}{\{\phi,\varepsilon\psi\}}\phi_{\bm{q}}(\beta)\\
&=\sum_{\phi}{\{\varepsilon\phi,\varepsilon\psi\}}(\varepsilon\phi)_{\bm{q}}(\beta)\\
&=\sum_{\phi}{\{\phi,\psi\}}(\varepsilon\phi)_{\bm{q}}(\beta).\end{split}

It remains to show that if
\beta
is in the image of
\mathit{Br}_{W}
, then

(252)

\displaystyle\phi_{\bm{q}}(\beta)\mapsto(\varepsilon\phi)_{\bm{q}}(\beta)\quad\text{under}\quad\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

This follows implicitly from the proof of
[
GP
, Prop. 9.4.1(a)]
.
∎

Lemma 7.5.7
.

We have
\bm{q}^{\frac{r}{2}}[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}\mapsto\varepsilon\bm{q}^{\frac{r}{2}}[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}
under
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

Proof.

Observe that
\sum_{i}{(-\bm{q})^{i}}\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V})
is the multiplicative inverse of
[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}
.
So the claim follows from Lemma
7.4.4
with
x=\bm{q}
.
∎

7.5.3.

We show that Proposition
7.5.1
, Proposition
7.5.5
, and Theorem
9
together imply Theorem
6
.

Proof of Theorem
6
.

Proposition
7.5.1
shows that if
W
has no factors of type
E_{7}
or
E_{8}
, then
\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
and
(1,\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0})_{W}
are indeed rational functions in
\bm{q}
, not just power series in
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Theorem
9
extends this result to all
W
.
These results, combined with Proposition
7.5.5
, establish

(253)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}

\displaystyle\in\bm{q}^{-\frac{|\beta|}{2}}R(W)[\bm{q}]\cap R(W)[\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\varepsilon\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}],

(254)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}

\displaystyle\in R(W)[\![\bm{q}]\!]\cap R(W)(\bm{q})\cap(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|-r}R(W)[(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{\pm 1}].

In particular, the
\bm{q}
-degrees of
\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
and
(1,\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0})_{W}
are well-defined.
The following observations show that their degrees must be
|\beta|-r
:

•

Proposition
7.5.1
shows that
\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
, and hence
(1,\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0})_{W}
, belong to
\mathbf{Z}[\![\bm{q}]\!]
,
i.e.
, their lowest-order term in
\bm{q}
is the constant term.

•

By Proposition
7.5.5
, we have

(255)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}\mapsto(-\bm{q})^{r-|\beta|}\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}\quad\text{under}\quad\bm{q}_{0}\mapsto\bm{q}_{0}^{-1}.

•

Proposition
7.5.1
also shows that
(1,\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0})_{W}
, and hence
\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
, have nonzero constant term.∎

8.
Deligne–Lusztig Theory

8.1.

The goal of this section is to prove Theorems
7
,
8
, and
9
.
Throughout, we assume that
W
is a Weyl group and use the notation and hypotheses of Sections
1
-
2
.
In particular, we continue to assume that
\mathbf{F}
is of large characteristic.
We will write
\mathrm{H}_{!}^{\ast}(-)=\mathrm{H}_{!}^{\ast}(-,\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})
to denote cohomology with compact support.

8.2.

The proofs of Theorems
7
and
8
rely on properties of the virtual characters of
G^{F}
introduced by Deligne–Lusztig
[
DL
]
, which we now review.

For all
w\in W
, the
Deligne–Lusztig variety

X_{w}\subseteq\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}
is the transverse intersection of
O_{w}
with the graph of the Frobenius map
F:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
.
The
G
-action on
\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}
restricts to a
G^{F}
-action on
X_{w}
, so the formal sum

(256)

\displaystyle R_{w}=\sum_{n}{(-1)^{n}}\mathrm{H}_{!}^{n}(X_{w})

defines a virtual representation of
G^{F}
.
In general, a virtual representation of
G^{F}
is
unipotent
iff it occurs with nonzero multiplicity in
R_{w}
for some
w
.

We have
X_{1}=\mathcal{B}^{F}
, a scheme of finitely many points, and
R_{1}=\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[\mathcal{B}^{F}]
, the
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-vector space of functions on
\mathcal{B}^{F}
.
For all
B\in\mathcal{B}^{F}
, let
\mathbf{1}_{B}
be the indicator function on
\{B\}
.
Recall,
cf.
Remark
2.5.1
, that Iwahori constructed an isomorphism

(257)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\otimes H_{W}|_{\bm{q}^{1/2}=q^{1/2}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\End_{G^{F}}(R_{1}).

In terms of the functions
\mathbf{1}_{B}
, the action of
\sigma_{w}\in H_{W}
on
R_{1}
is given by

(258)

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{w}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{B}=\sum_{(B,B^{\prime})\in O_{w}^{F}}\mathbf{1}_{B^{\prime}},

where
\tilde{\sigma}_{w}=q^{\frac{|w|}{2}}\sigma_{w}
.

Irreducible representations of
W
give rise to unipotent virtual representations of
G^{F}
in two ways:

8.2.1.

For any
\phi\in\hat{W}
, the formal
\mathbf{Q}
-linear combination

(259)

\displaystyle R_{\phi}=\frac{1}{|W|}\sum_{w\in W}\phi(w)R_{w}

has nonzero multiplicity in
R_{1}
(where we mean
1\in W
, not
1\in\hat{W}
), so it is unipotent.
The characters of the virtual representations
R_{\phi}
are called
almost-characters
, being very close to irreducible characters:
See Lemma-Postulate
8.3.1
, below.

8.2.2.

For any
\phi\in\hat{W}
, the trace
\phi_{\bm{q}}:\mathbf{Q}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})\otimes H_{W}\to\mathbf{Q}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
(see §
A.7
) restricts to a function
H_{W}\to\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]

[
GP
, Thm. 9.3.5]
.
So we can set

(260)

\displaystyle\phi_{q}=\phi_{\bm{q}}|_{\bm{q}^{1/2}=q^{1/2}}:H_{W}\to\mathbf{Z}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}].

Then the double centralizer theorem gives an isomorphism of
(H_{W}\times G^{F})
-modules

(261)

\displaystyle R_{1}\simeq\bigoplus_{\phi\in\hat{W}}V_{\phi,q}\otimes\rho_{\phi},

where
V_{\phi,q}
,
resp.

\rho_{\phi}
, is a simple
H_{W}
-module of character
\phi_{q}
,
resp.
a unipotent irreducible representation of
G^{F}
.
The representations
\rho_{\phi}
are called the
unipotent principal series
.

If
\rho
is a representation of
G^{F}
, then
\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},\rho)
forms an
\End_{G^{F}}(R_{1})
-module under precomposition.
In particular, applying
\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},-)
to both sides of (
261
) yields an isomorphism of
H_{W}
-bimodules:

(262)

\displaystyle\End_{G^{F}}(R_{1})\simeq\bigoplus_{\phi\in\hat{W}}V_{\phi,q}\otimes\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},\rho_{\phi}).

By Artin–Wedderburn, we deduce:

Lemma 8.2.1
.

For all
\phi\in\hat{W}
, we have
\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},\rho_{\phi})\simeq V_{\phi,q}
as
\End_{G^{F}}(R_{1})
-modules, hence as
H_{W}
-modules.

The map
\rho\mapsto\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},\rho)
extends uniquely to an additive map from virtual representations to virtual modules.
We emphasize that
\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},\rho)
may still be nonzero even when its virtual dimension
(R_{1},\rho)_{G^{F}}
is zero
.

8.3.

Lusztig showed that the unipotent irreducible representations of
G^{F}
can be indexed by a set that only depends on
W

[
L84-1
, Ch. 4]
.
This is the set denoted
\mathrm{Uch}(W)
in §
A.9
.
Abusing notation, we will conflate the elements of
\mathrm{Uch}(W)
with the unipotent irreducible representations themselves.
Then the embedding
\hat{W}\to\mathrm{Uch}(W)
is precisely the map
\phi\mapsto\rho_{\phi}
.
Cor. 4.24-4.25 of
[
L84-1
]
entail:

Lemma-Postulate 8.3.1
.

There is a function
\Delta:\mathrm{Uch}(W)\to\{\pm 1\}
such that for all
\phi\in\hat{W}
, we have:

(1)

\Delta(\rho_{\phi})=1
.

(2)

The transformation rule

(263)

\displaystyle R_{\phi}=\sum_{\rho\in\mathrm{Uch}(W)}{\{\rho_{\phi},\rho\}}\Delta(\rho)\rho.

Equivalently,
(\Delta(\rho)\rho,R_{\phi})=\{\rho_{\phi},\rho\}
for all
\rho\in\mathrm{Uch}(W)
.

Remark 8.3.2
.

We have
R_{\phi}(1)=\mathrm{Feg}_{\phi}(q)
and
\rho_{\phi}(1)=\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(q)
for all
\phi\in\hat{W}
.

8.4.

Springer conjectured
[
S76
]
, and Kazhdan proved
[
Kaz
]
, a remarkable identity relating the character values of the virtual representations
R_{w}
to the action of
W
on the fibers of the Grothendieck–Springer map.
To state it, let
\mathcal{B}_{g}
denote the fiber of
\tilde{G}\to G
above
g\in G^{F}
, and let

(264)

\displaystyle Q_{g}(w)=\sum_{n}{\tr(wF\mid\mathrm{H}^{2n}(\mathcal{B}_{g}))}.

(By
[
DLP
]
,
\mathcal{B}_{g}
has no odd cohomology.)
The main result of
[
Kaz
]
implies:

Theorem 8.4.1

(Kazhdan)
.

For all
g\in G^{F}
and
w\in W
, we have
Q_{g}(w)=R_{w}(g)
.

Remark 8.4.2
.

Lusztig generalized the functions
Q_{g}
to bad characteristic in
[
L81
]
, and similarly generalized Kazhdan’s theorem in
[
L90
]
.

8.5.

In this subsection, we state our arguments in such a way that
we do not need to assume
G
is semisimple
.
The following result recovers Theorem
7
in the semisimple case.

Theorem 8.5.1
.

For all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
, we have

(265)

\displaystyle(\langle{\tilde{\beta}}\rangle_{\bm{q}}\cdot\varepsilon[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}})|_{\bm{q}^{1/2}=q^{1/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{r}}{|G^{F}|}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}^{F}}|\mathcal{U}(\beta)_{u}^{F}|Q_{u}

in
\mathbf{Q}(q^{\frac{1}{2}})\otimes R(W)
, where
\tilde{\beta}=q^{\frac{|\beta|}{2}}\beta
.

Remark 8.5.2
.

An algebraic group
H
is
special
iff every principal
H
-bundle in the étale topology is Zariski-locally trivial
[
Gr
]
.
If
G
is special, then
[
HR
, Appendix]
and
[
Jo
, Thm. 4.10]
together imply that the right-hand side of the identity is the virtual weight series of
[\mathcal{Z}(\beta)/G]
.
In this case, the identity is essentially a corollary of Theorem
5
.
The sign character appears when we use Verdier duality to match the dual of Borel–Moore homology with compactly-supported cohomology,
cf.

[
AHJR
, Lem. 4.5]
.

However, not all reductive algebraic groups are special.
If
X_{0}
is a
G_{0}
-variety and
G
is not special, then the virtual weight series of
[X/G]
need not be the quotient of the virtual weight polynomial of
X
by that of
G
:
See
[
Jo
, Ex. 4.9]
.
The force of Theorem
7
is that for
X_{0}=\mathcal{Z}(\beta)_{0}
, that property holds anyway.

The following orthogonality result for the characters
Q_{u}
will reduce our work to computing the multiplicities
(Q_{u},\langle{\tilde{\beta}}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}
.
We are using the version quoted by Shoji in
[
S
, 510]
, in turn based on
[
S76
, Thm. 5.6]
.
The counterpart for the characters
R_{w}
is
[
DL
, Thm. 6.9]
, which holds in any characteristic.

Theorem 8.5.3

(Deligne–Lusztig, Springer)
.

For all
\phi\in R(W)
, we have

(266)

\displaystyle q^{N}\phi\cdot\varepsilon Q_{1}=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}^{F}}{(Q_{u},\phi)_{W}}Q_{u}

in
R(W)
.

Before we can use Theorem
8.5.3
, we must rewrite the left-hand side of the orthogonality identity:

Lemma 8.5.4
.

We have
[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}=\frac{(-1)^{r}}{|G^{F}|}\bm{q}^{N}Q_{1}
.

Proof.

We have
\mathcal{B}_{1}=\mathcal{B}
, so the Borel isomorphism states that
Q_{1}
is the algebra of
W
-coinvariants of
\mathbf{V}
, graded by
\bm{q}
.
That is,

(267)

\displaystyle Q_{1}=[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}\cdot\prod_{1\leq i\leq r}{(1-\bm{q}^{d_{i}})},

where
d_{1},\ldots,d_{r}
are the invariant degrees of the
W
-action on
\mathbf{V}

[
GP
, 149]
.
(If
G
is not semisimple, then we might have
d_{i}=1
for one or more
i
.)
At the same time, if
B_{0}\subseteq G_{0}
is a fixed Borel, then

(268)

\displaystyle|G^{F}|=|B^{F}||\mathcal{B}^{F}|=\bm{q}^{N}(\bm{q}-1)^{r}\sum_{w\in W}\bm{q}^{|w|}.

By the Bott–Solomon formula
[
Sol
]
,
\sum_{w\in W}\bm{q}^{|w|}=\prod_{i:d_{i}\neq 1}\frac{\bm{q}^{d_{i}}-1}{\bm{q}-1}=\prod_{i}\frac{\bm{q}^{d_{i}}-1}{\bm{q}-1}
.
∎

Combining Theorem
8.5.3
and Lemma
8.5.4
, it remains to prove the following multiplicity formula:

Proposition 8.5.5
.

For any
G
-orbit
C\subseteq\mathcal{U}
and
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
, we have

(269)

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in C^{F}}{(Q_{u},\langle{\tilde{\beta}}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}|_{\bm{q}^{1/2}=q^{1/2}}}=\sum_{u\in C^{F}}|\mathcal{U}(\beta)_{u}^{F}|,

where
\tilde{\beta}=q^{\frac{|\beta|}{2}}\beta
.

Lemma 8.5.6
.

For all
\beta\in H_{W}
and
w\in W
, we have

(270)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}(w)|_{\bm{q}^{1/2}=q^{1/2}}=\tr(\beta\mid\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},R_{w})).

Proof.

By Lemma
8.2.1
and Lemma-Postulate
8.3.1
, the left-hand side equals

(271)

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi,\psi\in\hat{W}}(\rho_{\phi},R_{\psi})_{G^{F}}\tr(\beta\mid\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},\rho_{\phi}))\psi(w).

If
\rho\in\mathrm{Uch}(W)
does not take the form
\rho_{\phi}
for some
\phi\in\hat{W}
, then
\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},\rho)=0
.
So above, the sum over
\phi\in\hat{W}
can be extended to a sum over
\rho\in\mathrm{Uch}(W)
, giving

(272)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{\psi\in\hat{W}}\sum_{\rho\in\mathrm{Uch}(W)}(\rho,R_{\psi})_{G^{F}}\tr(\beta\mid\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},\rho))\psi(w)\\
&=\sum_{\psi\in\hat{W}}\tr(\beta\mid\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},R_{\psi}))\psi(w).\end{split}

By (
259
) and Schur orthogonality, the last expression is
\tr(\beta\mid\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},R_{w}))
.
∎

Lemma 8.5.7
.

For all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
and
g\in G^{F}
, we have

(273)

\displaystyle|G(\beta)_{g}^{F}|=\tr(\tilde{\beta}\times g\mid R_{1}),

where
\tilde{\beta}=q^{\frac{|\beta|}{2}}\beta
.

Proof.

Fix
B_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{F}
.
Suppose that
\beta=\sigma_{s_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{s_{\ell}}
, where
s_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell}\in S
.
Then

\displaystyle G(\beta)_{g,B_{0}}^{F}\simeq\left\{(B_{1},\ldots,B_{\ell})\in(\mathcal{B}^{F})^{\ell}:\begin{array}[]{ll}B_{0}=g^{-1}B_{\ell}g,\\
(B_{i-1},B_{i})\in O_{s_{i}}&\text{for $1\leq i\leq\ell$}\end{array}\right\}.

By (
258
), the cardinality of the right-hand side is precisely the coefficient of
\mathbf{1}_{B_{0}}
in the expansion of
(\tilde{\beta},g)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{B_{0}}
with respect to the basis
\{\mathbf{1}_{B}\}_{B\in\mathcal{B}^{F}}
of
R_{1}
.
Summing over
B_{0}
gives the result.
∎

Proof of Proposition
8.5.5
.

By Schur orthogonality, Lemma
8.5.6
, and Theorem
8.4.1
,

(276)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(Q_{u},\langle{\tilde{\beta}}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}&=\frac{1}{|W|}\sum_{w\in W}{\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}(w)}Q_{u}(w)\\
&=\frac{1}{|W|}\sum_{w\in W}{\tr(\beta\mid\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},R_{w}))}R_{w}(u).\end{split}

On the other hand, by Lemma
8.5.7
, (
261
), and Lemma
8.2.1
,

(277)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\mathcal{U}(\beta)_{u}^{F}|&=\tr(\tilde{\beta}\times u\mid R_{1})\\
&=\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}\phi_{\bm{q}}(\tilde{\beta})\rho_{\phi}(u)\\
&=\sum_{\rho\in\mathrm{Uch}(W)}\tr(\beta\mid\Hom_{G^{F}}(R_{1},\rho))\rho(u).\end{split}

So the following result concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 8.5.8

(Geck–Lusztig)
.

We have

(278)

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in C^{F}}\rho(u)=\sum_{u\in C^{F}}\frac{1}{|W|}\sum_{w\in W}{(\rho,R_{w})_{G^{F}}}R_{w}(u)

for any
G
-orbit
C\subseteq\mathcal{U}
and
\rho\in\mathrm{Uch}(W)
.

Proof.

This is shown on pages 497-498 of
[
L11
]
, relying on
[
G96
, Prop. 1.3]
.
∎

Remark 8.5.9
.

If
G
is of type
A
, then the identity holds without summing over
C^{F}
on both sides,
i.e.
, at the level of individual
u\in C^{F}
.

8.6.

Recall from Section
7
that for any topological braid
\beta
, we write
\hat{\beta}
for its link closure and
[\hat{\beta}]_{\bm{q}}\in\mathbf{Z}(\!(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})\!)[a^{\pm 1}]
to denote the HOMFLY series of the link; for any finite Coxeter group
W
, we write
\mathsf{tr}:H_{W}\to\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}](a)
to denote Gomi’s Markov trace.
In
[
Kál
]
, Kálmán proved the following result.

Theorem 8.6.1

(Kálmán)
.

For all
n
and
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{n}
, we have an equality of
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}
-series:

(279)

\displaystyle\text{coefficient of $a^{|\beta|+n-1}$ in $[\widehat{\beta\pi}]$}=\text{coefficient of $a^{|\beta|-n+1}$ in $[\hat{\beta}]$}.

More generally, if
W
is any finite Coxeter group of rank
r
, then we have an equality

(280)

\displaystyle\text{coefficient of $a^{2r}$ in $\overline{\mathsf{tr}}(\beta\pi)$}=\text{coefficient of $a^{0}$ in $\overline{\mathsf{tr}}(\beta)$},

where
\overline{\mathsf{tr}}=\left(\frac{1-a^{2}}{1-\bm{q}}\right)^{r}\mathsf{tr}
.

More precisely,
[
Kál
]
includes two proofs of the statement about HOMFLY.
The second proof only uses the structure of
H_{W}
, so it generalizes to a proof of the statement about
\mathsf{tr}
.
By Proposition
7.4.3
, we deduce:

Corollary 8.6.2
.

For any finite Coxeter group
W
and
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
, we have

(281)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon,\langle{\beta\pi}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0})_{W}=\bm{q}^{\frac{|\pi|}{2}}(1,\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0})_{W}.

Corollary 8.6.3
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
8.5.1
, we have

(282)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{X}(\beta\pi)^{F}|=|\mathcal{U}(\beta)^{F}|.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Corollary
4
in Section
6
.
Namely, by Springer theory, we have:

(1)

(1,Q_{u})_{W}=1
for all
u\in\mathcal{U}^{F}
.

(2)

(\varepsilon,Q_{1})_{W}=\bm{q}^{\dim\mathcal{B}}
and
(\varepsilon,Q_{u})_{W}=0
for all
u\neq 1
.

Now, Theorem
8.5.1
and the preceding corollary together imply
q^{\dim\mathcal{B}}|\mathcal{X}(\beta\pi)^{F}|=q^{\frac{|\pi|}{2}}|\mathcal{U}(\beta)^{F}|
.
But we have
\dim\mathcal{B}=N=|w_{0}|=\frac{1}{2}|\pi|
.
∎

Remark 8.6.4
.

We claim that, in the case where
\beta=\sigma_{w}
, Corollary
8.6.3
recovers Proposition 2.2 of
[
L21
]
.
In our notation, the latter states that

(283)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{U}(\sigma_{w})^{F}|=q^{N}|\mathcal{B}^{F}|R_{1,w}(q),

where
R_{1,w}(\bm{q})\in\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}]
is the so-called
R
-polynomial defined in
[
KL
]
.
If
B_{+},B_{-}\in\mathcal{B}^{F}
are fixed Borels in opposite position, then

(284)

\displaystyle R_{1,w}=|\{B\in\mathcal{B}^{F}:(B_{+},B)\in O_{w},\,(B,B_{-})\in O_{w_{0}}\}|

by
[
KL
, Lem. A.4]
.
So, by the transitivity of the
G^{F}
-action on
O_{w_{0}}^{F}
, we have

(285)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{X}(\sigma_{w}\pi)^{F}|=|O_{w_{0}}^{F}|R_{1,w}(q)=q^{\dim\mathcal{B}}|\mathcal{B}^{F}|R_{1,w}(q).

Now Corollary
8.6.3
implies (
283
).

8.7.

In the next subsection, we will prove Theorem
8
.
Before doing so, we set notation for parabolic subgroups and review how they interact with the virtual representations
R_{w}
.

Fix a split torus
T_{0}\subseteq G_{0}
.
We identify the character lattice
\mathbf{X}
in the root datum of
G
with the character lattice of
T
.
We identify the set
S\subseteq W
with the set of reflections in
\mathbf{V}^{\vee}
corresponding to a system of simple roots in
\mathbf{X}
.
This system of simple roots determines a Borel
B_{0}=T_{0}\ltimes U_{0}
.

If
P_{0}
is a parabolic subgroup of
G_{0}
containing
B_{0}
, then the Weyl group of the Levi quotient of
P_{0}
is an
S
-parabolic subgroup of
W
(see §
A.4
).
(Conversely, every
S
-parabolic subgroup of
W
arises in this way.)
We fix such a parabolic
P_{0}
.
We write
U_{P,0}
and
L_{0}
for its unipotent radical and Levi quotient, respectively.
In general, we will distinguish the data attached to
L_{0}
with a subscript.
Thus,

(1)

W_{L}
is the Weyl group of
L_{0}
.

(2)

\mathcal{B}_{L,0}
is the flag variety of
L_{0}
.

(3)

\mathcal{U}_{L,0}
is the unipotent locus of
L_{0}
.

(4)

If
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W_{L}}^{+}
, then we write
O_{L}(\beta)_{0}
for the analogue of
O(\beta)
with
L_{0}
in place of
G_{0}
.

(5)

If
w\in W_{L}
, then
R_{L,w}
is the Deligne–Lusztig virtual character with
L^{F}
in place of
G^{F}
.

The following formula for the parabolic restriction of the
R_{w}
is
[
C
, Prop. 7.4.4]
, though it originates in
[
DL
, §8]
.

Proposition 8.7.1

(Deligne–Lusztig)
.

For all
w\in W
, we have

(286)

\displaystyle(R_{w})^{U_{P}^{F}}=\frac{1}{|W_{L}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in W\\
xwx^{-1}\in W_{L}\end{subarray}}R_{L,xwx^{-1}}

as virtual representations of
L^{F}
, where
(-)^{U_{P}^{F}}
means we take
U_{P}^{F}
-invariants.

8.8.

We now use Theorem
7
to prove Theorem
8
.
In what follows, let
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
be a parabolic subgroup, and let
d\geq 0
be the difference in their ranks.

8.8.1.

First, we establish the commutative diagram:

(287)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{Br}_{W^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(W^{\prime})[\![\bm{q}]\!]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{Br}_{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(W)[\![\bm{q}]\!]}
\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
\frac{1}{(1-\bm{q})^{d}}\Ind_{W^{\prime}}^{W}
\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}

We can and will assume that
W^{\prime}=W_{L}
in the setup of the previous subsection.

To disambiguate notation, we will write
\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
,
resp.

\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0^{\prime}}
, for the function on
\mathit{Br}_{W}
,
resp.

\mathit{Br}_{W^{\prime}}
.
For all
w\in W^{\prime}
, let the
G_{0}
-schemes
\mathcal{U}_{w,0}
,
\mathcal{U}_{L,w,0}
over
\mathbf{F}
be defined by the cartesian squares:

(288)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}}
\mathit{act}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{L,w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{L,w,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{L,0}\times\mathcal{B}_{L,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{L,0}\times\mathcal{B}_{L,0}}
\mathit{act}

Let
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}=\mathbf{V}/\mathbf{V}^{W^{\prime}}
, so that
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
is a realization of
W^{\prime}
satisfying
(\mathbf{V}^{\prime})^{W^{\prime}}=0
.
Then
d=\dim\mathbf{V}-\dim\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
.
So for all
x\in W^{\prime}
, Theorems
8.4.1
and
8.5.1
give

(289)

\displaystyle\langle{\sigma_{w}}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}(x)|_{\bm{q}=q}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{r-|\beta|}}{|G^{F}|}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}^{F}}|(\mathcal{U}_{w})_{u}^{F}|R_{x}(u),

(290)

\displaystyle\frac{(\Ind_{W^{\prime}}^{W}\langle{\sigma_{w}}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0^{\prime}})(x)|_{\bm{q}=q}}{(1-q)^{d}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{r-|\beta|}}{|L^{F}||W^{\prime}|}\sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{L}^{F}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in W\\
yxy^{-1}\in W^{\prime}\end{subarray}}|(\mathcal{U}_{L,w})_{u^{\prime}}^{F}|R_{L,yxy^{-1}}(u^{\prime}).

Since
\beta\mapsto{(-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|}}\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
is linear as a function on
H_{W}
, it remains to match the right-hand sides above for all
w
and
x
.
By Proposition
8.7.1
, we further reduce the problem to:

(291)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|G^{F}|}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}^{F}}|(\mathcal{U}_{w})_{u}^{F}|R_{x}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|L^{F}|}\sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{L}^{F}}|(\mathcal{U}_{L,w})_{u^{\prime}}^{F}|(\Res_{P^{F}}^{G^{F}}R_{x})^{U_{P}^{F}}(u^{\prime}).

We will rewrite the point counts on both sides.
Let
(\mathcal{U}_{w})_{B}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{w}
be the fiber of
\mathcal{U}_{w}\to\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
above our fixed Borel
B\in\mathcal{B}^{F}
, so that

(292)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{U}_{w})_{B}^{F}=\{u\in\mathcal{U}^{F}:(u^{-1}Bu,B)\in O_{w}\}.

Let
B^{F}
act on
G^{F}
,
resp.

(\mathcal{U}_{w})_{B}^{F}
, by right multiplication,
resp.
right conjugation.
(Thus its action on
(\mathcal{U}_{w})_{B}^{F}\times G^{F}
is free.)
Analogously to Lemma
4.5.2
, we have:

Lemma 8.8.1
.

We have a
G^{F}
-equivariant bijection

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}((\mathcal{U}_{w})_{B}^{F}\times G^{F})/B^{F}&\xrightarrow{\sim}&\mathcal{U}_{w}^{F}\\
{[u,g]}&\mapsto&gug^{-1}\end{array}

where
G^{F}
acts on
((\mathcal{U}_{w})_{B}^{F}\times G^{F})/B^{F}
by left multiplication on the factor of
G^{F}
.

Let
\{\mathcal{U}_{i}^{F}\}_{i}
be the set of
G^{F}
-orbits of
\mathcal{U}^{F}
.
For each
i
such that
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{F}
intersects
(\mathcal{U}_{w})_{B}^{F}
, we pick a point
u_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}^{F}
.
Using Lemma
8.8.1
, we expand:

(295)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{|G^{F}|}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}^{F}}|(\mathcal{U}_{w})_{u}^{F}|R_{x}(u)&=\frac{1}{|B^{F}|}\sum_{i}|\mathcal{U}_{i}^{F}\cap(\mathcal{U}_{w})_{B}^{F}|R_{x}(u_{i})\\
&=\frac{1}{|B^{F}|}\sum_{u\in(\mathcal{U}_{w})_{B}^{F}}R_{x}(u).\end{split}

Let
B_{L}=L\cap B
.
As above, let
\{\mathcal{U}_{L,j}^{F}\}_{j}
be the set of
L^{F}
-orbits of
\mathcal{U}_{L}^{F}
.
For each
j
such that
\mathcal{U}_{L,j}^{F}
intersects
(\mathcal{U}_{L,w})_{B_{L}}^{F}
, we pick a point
u^{\prime}_{j}\in(\mathcal{U}_{L,j})_{B_{L}}^{F}
.
Then we can also expand:

(296)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{|L^{F}|}\sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{L}^{F}}|(\mathcal{U}_{L,w})_{u^{\prime}}^{F}|(R_{x})^{U^{F}}(u^{\prime})&=\frac{1}{|B_{L}^{F}|}\sum_{j}|(\mathcal{U}_{L,j})_{B}^{F}|(R_{x})^{U^{F}}(u^{\prime}_{j})\\
&=\frac{1}{|B_{L}^{F}|}\sum_{u^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{U}_{L,j})_{B}^{F}}(R_{x})^{U^{F}}(u^{\prime})\\
&=\frac{1}{|B_{L}^{F}||U_{P}^{F}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{U}_{L,j})_{B}^{F}\\
u^{\prime\prime}\in U_{P}^{F}\end{subarray}}R_{x}(u^{\prime}u^{\prime\prime}).\end{split}

Above,
|B_{L}^{F}||U_{P}^{F}|=|B^{F}|
.
So the following lemma concludes the proof of (
291
).

Lemma 8.8.2
.

For all
w\in W_{L}
, we have
(\mathcal{U}_{w})_{B}=(\mathcal{U}_{L,w})_{B_{L}}U_{P}\simeq(\mathcal{U}_{L,w})_{B_{L}}\times U_{P}
.

Proof.

Lifting
w\in W_{L}\simeq N_{L}(T)/T
to a point
\dot{w}\in N_{L}(T)
, we observe that

(297)

\displaystyle B\dot{w}B=U_{P}B_{L}\dot{w}B_{L}U_{P}=(B_{L}\dot{w}B_{L})U_{P}\simeq B_{L}\dot{w}B_{L}\times U_{P}.

But
(\mathcal{U}_{w})_{B}=B\dot{w}B\cap\mathcal{U}
and
(\mathcal{U}_{L,w})_{B_{L}}=B_{L}\dot{w}B_{L}\cap\mathcal{U}_{L}
.
∎

8.8.2.

We claim that commutativity of (
287
) implies commutativity of:

(298)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{W^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(W^{\prime})[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(W)[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]}
\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}
\Ind_{W^{\prime}}^{W}
\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}

This follows from the projection formula for induced representations, as we now explain.
If
[\Alt(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}=\sum_{i}{(-\bm{q})^{i}}\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V})
, then
\varepsilon[\Alt(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}
is the multiplicative inverse of
\varepsilon[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}
in
R(W)[\![\bm{q}]\!]
.
By the projection formula, we have

(299)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(-\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|}\Ind_{W^{\prime}}^{W}\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}&=\Ind_{W^{\prime}}^{W}{(\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}\cdot\varepsilon_{W^{\prime}}[\Alt(\mathbf{V}^{\prime})]_{\bm{q}})}\\
&=\Ind_{W^{\prime}}^{W}{(\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}\cdot\Res_{W^{\prime}}^{W}(\varepsilon_{W}[\Alt(\mathbf{V}^{\prime})]_{\bm{q}}))}\\
&=(\Ind_{W^{\prime}}^{W}\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0})\cdot\varepsilon_{W}[\Alt(\mathbf{V}^{\prime})]_{\bm{q}}\end{split}

Since
d=\dim\mathbf{V}-\dim\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
, we have

(300)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(-\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|}\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}&=\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}\cdot\varepsilon_{W}[\Alt(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}\\
&=\frac{1}{(1-\bm{q})^{d}}\,\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}\cdot\varepsilon_{W}[\Alt(\mathbf{V}^{\prime})]_{\bm{q}}.\end{split}

So the commutativity of (
287
) implies
\Ind_{W^{\prime}}^{W}(\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}})=\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}
, as needed.

8.9.

Finally, we prove Theorem
9
, stating that

(301)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}\in\frac{1}{(1-\bm{q})^{r(w)}}\,R(W)[\bm{q}],

where
w
is the image of
\beta
in
W
and
r(w)=\dim\mathbf{V}^{w}
.

First, we introduce a function
\delta:W\to\mathbf{Z}
.
For all
w\in W
, we claim that

(302)

\displaystyle\delta(w)=\lim_{\bm{q}\to 1}\frac{\det(1-\bm{q}w\mid\mathbf{V})}{(1-\bm{q})^{r(w)}}

exists and takes values in
\mathbf{Z}
.
Indeed, since
w
has finite order, its action on
\mathbf{V}
is diagonalizable.
Thus
r(w)
equals the multiplicity of
1-\bm{q}
in the characteristic polynomial
\det(1-\bm{q}w\mid\mathbf{V})
.
This shows that
\delta(w)
exists.
It is an integer because
\mathbf{Q}_{W}=\mathbf{Q}
and character values are algebraic integers.
We can now state:

Theorem 8.9.1

(Lusztig)
.

If
G
is semisimple and
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
maps to
w\in W
, then the isotropy groups of the
G
-action on
G(\beta)
are diagonalizable groups
D
such that

(303)

\displaystyle|D^{F}|\quad\text{divides}\quad(q-1)^{r(w)}\delta(w)|Z(G)|,

where
Z(G)
is the center of
G
.
In particular, the isotropy groups of the
G^{F}
-action on
G(\beta)^{F}
satisfy the same divisibility condition.

Proof.

Fix a maximal split torus
T_{0}\subseteq G_{0}
and a lift
\dot{w}\in N_{G}(T)
of
w
.
In the case where
G
is adjoint and
w
is
elliptic
, meaning
T^{w}
is discrete, the proof is analogous to the proof of
[
L11
, Thm. 5.2]
.
However, the argument there actually shows more:
For general
G
and
w
, it shows that we can assume
D\subseteq T^{\dot{w}}
, independently of
\beta
.
Let
\bar{G}=G/Z(G)
, the adjoint group of
G
, and let
\bar{T},\bar{D}
be the images of
T,D
in
\bar{G}
.
Writing
\bar{T}^{\prime}
for the identity component of
\bar{T}^{\dot{w}}
, we see that
\bar{T}^{\prime}
is a split torus of rank
r(w)
such that
\bar{T}^{\dot{w}}/\bar{T}^{\prime}
is discrete.
So we get
|(\bar{T}^{\dot{w}})^{F}|=(q-1)^{r(w)}\delta(w)
from the elliptic case.
Finally,
|(T^{\dot{w}})^{F}|=|(\bar{T}^{\dot{w}})^{F}||Z(G)|
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9
.

Combining Theorems
7
and
8.9.1
, we see that

(304)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}|_{\bm{q}=q}\in\frac{1}{(1-q)^{r(w)}\delta(w)|Z(G)|}\,R(W)

for all
q\gg 0
.
At the same time, by Proposition
7.5.1
,

(305)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}\in\frac{1}{(1-\bm{q}^{d_{1}})\cdots(1-\bm{q}^{d_{r}})}\,R(W)[\bm{q}_{0}],

where
\bm{q}_{0}
is defined by (
7.5.1
) and
d_{i}\in\mathbf{Z}
for all
i
.
Therefore, (
304
) simplifies to

(306)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}|_{\bm{q}=q}\in\frac{1}{(1-q)^{r(w)}}\,R(W)

for all
q\gg 0
.
Therefore, (
305
) simplifies to

(307)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}\in\frac{1}{(1-\bm{q})^{r(w)}}\,R(W)[\bm{q}],

as needed.
∎

9.
Periodic Braids

9.1.

In this section and the next,
we no longer assume that
W
is crystallographic
.
Let
w_{0}
be the longest element of
W
with respect to the chosen Coxeter presentation (see §
A.1
).
The
full twist
of
\mathit{Br}_{W}
is the positive braid

(308)

\displaystyle\pi=\sigma_{w_{0}}^{2}\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}.

It is central in
\mathit{Br}_{W}

[
BM97
, §2]
.
We say that a braid
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
is
periodic
iff

(309)

\displaystyle\beta^{n}=\pi^{m}

for some
\frac{m}{n}\in\mathbf{Q}
, in which case we say that
\frac{m}{n}
is the
slope
of
\beta
.

Theorem 9.1.1

(Lee–Lee)
.

If
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
is periodic of slope
\frac{m}{n}
, then
\beta=\gamma^{m}
and
\pi=\gamma^{n}
for some
\gamma\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
.

Proof.

Let
d
be the minimal positive exponent such that
\sigma_{w_{0}}^{d}
is central in
\mathit{Br}_{W}
.
Either
d=1
or
d=2
.

In
[
LL
]
, E.-K. Lee and S.-J. Lee say that an element
\gamma\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
is
primitive
iff, whenever
\gamma=\delta^{\ell}
for some
\delta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
, we have
\ell=\pm 1
.
Theorem 3.14 of
ibid.
implies that if
\gamma
is primitive and periodic, then
\gamma
is a positive root of
\sigma_{w_{0}}^{d}
.

If
\beta
is periodic, then any root of
\beta
is also periodic.
So it remains to check that some root of
\beta
is primitive.
Indeed, this follows from the well-ordering principle, because the writhe of
\beta
is an integer.
∎

Remark 9.1.2
.

In type
A
, where the elements of
\mathit{Br}_{W}
represent topological braids, Theorem
9.1.1
was first shown in 1934 by Kerékjártó–Eilenberg
[
E
]
.

Remark 9.1.3
.

A result of Bessis implies that if an
n
th root of
\pi
exists, then the
n
th roots of
\pi
form a single conjugacy class in
\mathit{Br}_{W}

[
Bes
, Thm. 12.4(ii)]
.

9.2.

The following result, an incarnation of Schur’s lemma, is
[
GP
, Thm. 9.4.3]
.

Theorem 9.2.1

(Springer)
.

For all
\phi\in\hat{W}
, the full twist acts on the underlying
H_{W}
-module of
\phi_{\bm{q}}
by the scalar
\bm{q}^{\mathbf{c}(\phi)}
, where
\mathbf{c}(\phi)
is the content of
\phi
(see §
A.10
).
In particular,
\phi_{\bm{q}}(\pi)=\bm{q}^{\mathbf{c}(\phi)}\phi(1)
.

The following corollary is a mild generalization of
[
BM97
, Prop. 9.2.8]
, but we give the proof anyway.
The proof is based on
[
J
, Lem. 9.4]
, which Jones used to calculate the HOMFLY polynomials of torus knots.

Corollary 9.2.2
.

If
\phi\in\hat{W}
and
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
is periodic of slope
\nu\in\mathbf{Q}
, then

(310)

\displaystyle\phi_{\bm{q}}(\beta)=\bm{q}^{\nu\mathbf{c}(\phi)}\phi(w),

where
w\in W
is the image of
\beta
under the surjection
\mathit{Br}_{W}\twoheadrightarrow W
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the result when
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}
has been specialized to a generic complex number
q^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
We set

(311)

\displaystyle H_{W}[q^{\frac{1}{2}}]=H_{W}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm 1/2}]}\mathbf{C},

where
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]\to\mathbf{C}
sends
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto q^{\frac{1}{2}}
, and

(312)

\displaystyle\phi_{q}=\phi_{\bm{q}}|_{\bm{q}^{1/2}=q^{1/2}}.

Write
\nu=\frac{m}{n}
in lowest terms.
By the theorem,
q^{-m\mathbf{c}(\phi)}\pi^{m}
acts on the underlying
H_{W}[q^{\frac{1}{2}}]
-module of
\phi_{q}
by the identity operator
I
.
Since
\beta^{n}=\pi^{m}
, we deduce that
q^{-\nu\mathbf{c}(\phi)}\beta
acts by an
n
th root of
I
.

Recall that
H_{W}|_{q=1}=\mathbf{Z}[W]
.
Choose a generic path in the complex plane from
q
to
1
.
Since the (diagonalizable) roots of
I
do not deform, the deformation of
q
along this path shows that the following operators must have the same eigenvalue spectrum:

•

The action of
q^{-\nu\mathbf{c}(\phi)}\beta
on the underlying
H[q^{\frac{1}{2}}]
-module of
\phi_{q}
.

•

The action of
w
on the underlying
\mathbf{C}[W]
-module of
\phi
.

The result follows.
∎

9.3.

Let
\mathbf{V}
be a realization of
W
over
\mathbf{C}
such that
\mathbf{V}^{W}=0
.
A vector of
\mathbf{V}
is
regular
with respect to
W
iff its stabilizer in
W
is trivial, or equivalently, it avoids the reflecting hyperplanes that generate the
W
-action.
Suppose that an element
w\in W
admits a regular eigenvector in
\mathbf{V}
with eigenvalue
\zeta\in\mathbf{C}^{\times}
.
In this case,
\zeta
must be a root of unity.
We say that
\zeta
is
regular
with respect to
W
and that
w
is a
\zeta
-regular element
of
W
.
The following results are parts (iv) and (v) of
[
S74
, Thm. 4.2]
, respectively.

Theorem 9.3.1

(Springer)
.

For any
\zeta\in\mathbf{C}^{\times}
, the set of
\zeta
-regular elements of
W
is either empty or forms a single conjugacy class.

Theorem 9.3.2

(Springer)
.

If
w\in W
is a
\zeta
-regular element, then

(313)

\displaystyle\phi(w)=\mathrm{Feg}_{\phi}(\zeta^{-1})=\mathrm{Feg}_{\phi}(\zeta)

for all
\phi\in\hat{W}
, where
\mathrm{Feg}_{\phi}
is the fake degree of
\phi
(see §
A.8
).

Remark 9.3.3
.

In
[
S74
, 170]
, Springer claims that if
n
is a fixed integer, then the regular elements of
W
of order
n
form a single conjugacy class.
This claim, which is stronger than Theorem
9.3.1
, fails when
W
is not crystallographic.
For example, let
W
be the Coxeter group of type
I_{2}(5)
and let
w
be a Coxeter element.
Then
w
and
w^{2}
are both regular elements of order
5
, but they are not conjugate.

Regular elements of
W
are related to periodic elements of
\mathit{Br}_{W}
by the following results, which are respectively Proposition 3.11 and Théorème 3.12 in
[
BM97
]
.

Proposition 9.3.4

(Broué–Michel)
.

If
w\in W
is regular of order
n
, then
\sigma_{w}^{n}=\pi
.

Theorem 9.3.5

(Broué–Michel)
.

If
\gamma\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
satisfies
\gamma^{n}=\pi
for some
n
, then its image in
W
is a
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{n}}
-regular element.

Corollary 9.3.6
.

If
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
is periodic of slope
\nu\in\mathbf{Q}
, then the image of
\beta
in
W
is a
e^{2\pi i\nu}
-regular element.

Proof.

This is immediate from Theorem
9.3.5
when
\beta
is a root of
\pi
.
Theorem
9.1.1
lets us bootstrap to the general case.
∎

Remark 9.3.7
.

We emphasize that Theorem
9.3.5
is deeper than Proposition
9.3.4
:
Its proof relies on results used by Charney to prove the biautomaticity of Artin groups
[
BM97
, §3.19]
.

9.4.

Theorem
10
states that if
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}
is periodic of slope
\nu\in\mathbf{Q}
, then its
R(W)
trace is given by

(314)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}=\sum_{\psi\in\hat{W}}{\bm{q}^{\nu\mathbf{c}(\psi)}}\mathrm{Deg}_{\psi}(\zeta)\psi,

where
\zeta=e^{2\pi i\nu}
and
\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}
is the unipotent degree of
\phi
(see §
A.8
).

Proof of Theorem
10
.

By Corollary
9.3.6
, the image of
\beta
in
W
is a
\zeta
-regular element.
Therefore, by Corollary
9.2.2
and Theorem
9.3.2
,

(315)

\displaystyle\phi_{\bm{q}}(\beta)=\bm{q}^{\nu\mathbf{c}(\phi)}\mathrm{Feg}_{\phi}(\zeta).

Therefore, by Lemma-Postulate
A.9.1
(2) and Corollary
A.10.4
,

(316)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}\phi_{\bm{q}}(\beta)&=\bm{q}^{\nu\mathbf{c}(\psi)}\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}\mathrm{Feg}_{\phi}(\zeta)\\
&=\bm{q}^{\nu\mathbf{c}(\psi)}\mathrm{Deg}_{\psi}(\zeta),\end{split}

as needed.
∎

10.
The Rational DAHA

10.1.

As in the previous section,
we do not assume that
W
is crystallographic
.
Let
\mathrm{Ref}(W)\subseteq W
be the subset of elements that act on
\mathbf{V}
by reflections,
cf.
§
A.10
.
We continue to assume that
\mathbf{V}^{W}=0
.

We write
\langle-,-\rangle:\mathbf{V}\times\mathbf{V}^{\vee}\to\mathbf{Q}_{W}
for the evaluation pairing.
For each reflection
t\in\mathrm{Ref}(W)
, we fix nonzero vectors
\alpha_{t}\in\mathbf{V}
and
\alpha_{t}^{\vee}\in\mathbf{V}^{\vee}
such that

(1)

\langle\alpha_{t},-\rangle=0
is the hyperplane of
\mathbf{V}^{\vee}
fixed by
t
.

(2)

\langle-,\alpha_{t}^{\vee}\rangle=0
is the hyperplane of
\mathbf{V}
fixed by
t
.

(3)

\langle\alpha_{t},\alpha_{t}^{\vee}\rangle=2
.

In the literature, the vectors
\alpha_{t}
and
\alpha_{t}^{\vee}
are called roots and coroots even when
W
is not crystallographic.
Condition (3) will ensure that in what follows, our constructions do not depend on the exact choices of
\alpha_{t},\alpha_{t}^{\vee}
.

For any
\nu\in\mathbf{C}
, the
rational Cherednik algebra
or
rational DAHA of slope
\nu
is the
\mathbf{C}
-algebra

(317)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}=\frac{\mathbf{C}[W]\ltimes(\Sym(\mathbf{V})\otimes\Sym(\mathbf{V}^{\vee}))}{\left\langle yx-xy-\langle x,y\rangle+\nu\displaystyle\sum_{t\in\mathrm{Ref}(W)}\langle x,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\langle\alpha,y\rangle t:x\in\mathbf{V},\,y\in\mathbf{V}^{\vee}\right\rangle}.

Note that
\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}
contains
\mathbf{A}_{W}=\mathbf{C}[W]\ltimes\Sym(\mathbf{V})
as a subalgebra, regardless of
\nu
.

Remark 10.1.1
.

If
\nu=0
, then the defining relations of
\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}
simplify to
[x,y]=\langle x,y\rangle
for all
x
and
y
.
This shows that
\mathbf{D}_{0}^{\mathit{rat}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{C}[W]\ltimes\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{V})
, the semidirect product of
\mathbf{C}[W]
with the Weyl algebra of
\mathbf{V}
.

10.2.

Like the universal enveloping algebra of a semisimple Lie algebra,
\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}
admits a triangular decomposition in the sense of Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt.
It therefore admits an analogue of the Bernstein–Gelfand–Gelfand category
\mathsf{O}
, which we will denote by
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}

[
GGOR
]
.

Any
\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}
-module
M\in\mathsf{O}_{\nu}
can be viewed as a
W
-equivariant quasicoherent sheaf on
\mathbf{V}^{\vee}=\Spec\Sym(\mathbf{V})
.
The
support
of
M
is the
W
-stable subvariety of
\mathbf{V}^{\vee}
that forms its support as a sheaf.
Let
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{tor}}\subseteq\mathsf{O}_{\nu}
be the Serre subcategory of modules that are torsion over
\Sym(\mathbf{V}^{\vee})\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}
.
By
[
GGOR
, Lem. 5.1]
,
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{tor}}
is also the full subcategory of
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}
of modules supported on the discriminant locus of
\mathbf{V}^{\vee}
,
i.e.
, the common complement of the reflection hyperplanes of the
W
-action.

The Verma modules in
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}
are indexed by the set
\hat{W}
.
For all
\phi\in\hat{W}
, we write
\Delta_{\nu}(\phi)
for the corresponding Verma module of
\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}
, and we write
L_{\nu}(\phi)
for its simple quotient.
Explicitly,
\Delta_{\nu}(\phi)
can be constructed by inflating
\phi
from
\mathbf{C}[W]
up to
\mathbf{A}_{W}
, then tensoring up to
\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}
.
In the literature,
\Delta_{\nu}(1)
is known as the polynomial module and
L_{\nu}(1)
as the simple spherical module.

Like the Weyl algebra of
\mathbf{V}
, the rational Cherednik algebra contains a canonical “Euler element”
\bm{h}
.
For the precise definition, see
[
E07
, Ch. 11]
.
It commutes with the subalgebra
\mathbf{C}[W]\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}
, and its action on any object of
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}
is locally finite.
Thus, each object
M\in\mathsf{O}
is endowed with a decomposition into finite-dimensional,
W
-stable
\bm{h}
-eigenspaces.
On all
\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}
-modules that we will need, the eigenvalues will be half-integers.
We define the
\bm{q}
-graded character
of such a module
M
to be

(318)

\displaystyle[M]_{\bm{q}}=\sum_{n}{\bm{q}^{\frac{n}{2}}}M_{\frac{n}{2}}\in R(W)(\!(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})\!)

where
M_{\frac{n}{2}}\subseteq M
is the
\bm{h}
-eigenspace of eigenvalue
\frac{n}{2}
.
By
[
E07
, Ch. 11]
,

(319)

\displaystyle[\Delta_{\nu}(\phi)]_{\bm{q}}=\bm{q}^{\frac{r}{2}-\nu\mathbf{c}(\phi)}\phi\cdot[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}

for all
\phi\in\hat{W}
, where we set
[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}=\sum_{i}{\bm{q}^{i}}\Sym^{i}(\mathbf{V})
as in Sections
7
-
8
and §
A.6
of Appendix
A
.

The basic problem in the representation theory of
\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}
is to understand how the graded characters
[\Delta_{\nu}(\phi)]_{\bm{q}}
, which are explicit, can be used to express the graded characters
[L_{\nu}(\phi)]_{\bm{q}}
, which are initially mysterious.
The problem is highly sensitive to the value of
\nu
.
When
\nu\notin\mathbf{Q}
, category
\mathsf{O}
is semisimple and
[L_{\nu}(\phi)]_{\bm{q}}=[\Delta_{\nu}(\phi)]_{\bm{q}}
.
When
\nu\in\mathbf{Q}
, category
\mathsf{O}
is no longer semisimple:
It grows more complex as the denominator of
\nu
grows smaller.

10.3.

Let
\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}}=e^{\pi i\nu}
.
We draw freely upon the notation in §
A.11
, so that

(320)

\displaystyle H_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})=H_{W}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]}\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})

and
\bm{d}_{\zeta}:R^{+}(H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}))\to R^{+}(H_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}}))
is the decomposition map that corresponds to the specialization
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Let
\mathsf{Mod}(H_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}}))
be the category of
H_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})
-modules, and for each
\phi\in\hat{W}
, let

(321)

\displaystyle\phi_{\zeta}=\bm{d}_{\zeta}(\phi_{\bm{q}})\in\mathsf{Mod}(H_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})).

In
[
GGOR
]
, Ginzburg, Guay, Opdam, and Rouquier introduced the
Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov (KZ) functor
, an exact monoidal functor

(322)

\displaystyle\mathsf{KZ}:\mathsf{O}_{\nu}\to\mathsf{Mod}(H_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})).

The following facts comprise
[
GGOR
, Thm. 5.14, Cor. 5.18, Thm. 6.8]
:

Theorem 10.3.1

(Ginzburg–Guay–Opdam–Rouquier)
.

For all
\nu\in\mathbf{C}
,

(1)

\mathsf{KZ}
induces an equivalence
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}/\mathsf{O}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{tor}}\simeq\mathsf{Mod}(H_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}}))
.

(2)

\mathsf{KZ}
induces an isomorphism between the (vertical) center of
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}
,
i.e.
, the algebra of endomorphisms of its unit object, and the center of
H_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

(3)

\mathsf{KZ}(\Delta_{\nu}(\phi))=\phi_{\zeta}
.

10.4.

As preparation for the proofs of Corollary
11
and Theorem
12
, we introduce some subsets of
\mathbf{Q}
depending on
\nu
.
It will be convenient to write
\{d_{i}\}_{i\in I(W)}
for the multiset of invariant degrees of
W
.
For any slope
\nu\in\mathbf{C}
, let

(323)

\displaystyle I_{\nu}(W)=\{i\in I(W):\nu\in\tfrac{1}{d_{i}}\mathbf{Z}\}.

Concretely, if
n
is the denominator of
\nu
in lowest terms, then
I_{\nu}(W)
is the set of indices
i
such that
n
divides the invariant degree
d_{i}
.

Definition 10.4.1
.

If
\nu\in\mathbf{Q}
and
\zeta=e^{2\pi i\nu}
, then we say that
\nu
is:

(1)

A
singular slope
iff
|I_{\nu}(W)|\geq 1
.

(2)

A
regular slope
iff
W
contains a
\zeta
-regular element.

(3)

A
regular elliptic slope
iff
W
contains a
\zeta
-regular element
w
such that
\mathbf{V}^{w}=0
.
(Recall that we assume
\mathbf{V}^{W}=0
.)

(4)

A
cuspidal slope
iff
|I_{\nu}(W)|=1
.

We say that an integer
n>0
is a
singular number
, etc., iff it is the denominator in lowest terms of a singular slope, etc.

Lemma 10.4.2
.

There is a chain of implications:

(324)

\displaystyle\text{cuspidal}\implies\text{regular elliptic}\implies\text{regular}\implies\text{singular}.

Proof.

It is tautological that regular elliptic implies regular.
That regular implies singular follows from
[
S74
, Thm. 4.2(iii)]
.
That cuspidal implies regular elliptic is
[
BE
, Rem. 3.28]
.
∎

Example 10.4.3
.

In type
A_{r}
, the invariant degrees are
2,3,4,\ldots,r+1
.
An integer is regular if and only if it is a divisor of either
r
or
r+1

[
S74
, §5.1]
.
The only regular elliptic number is the Coxeter number
r+1

[
VV
, Ex. 1.1.2]
.

Example 10.4.4
.

In type
BC_{r}
, the invariant degrees are
2,4,6,\ldots,2r
.
An integer is regular,
resp.
regular elliptic, if and only if it is a divisor of
2r

[
S74
, §5.2]
,
resp.
an even divisor of
2r

[
VV
, Ex. 1.1.2]
.
To demonstrate:
In type
BC_{5}
:

•

The singular numbers are
1,2,3,4,5,6,8,10
.

•

The regular numbers are
1,2,5,10
.

•

The regular elliptic numbers are
2,10
.

•

The only cuspidal number is
10
, the Coxeter number.

This is a minimal example where each implication in Lemma
10.4.2
is strict.

Throughout the rest of this section, we assume
\nu\in\mathbf{Q}
and set
\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}}=e^{\pi i\nu}
.
We form the virtual
\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}
-module

(325)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\nu}=\bigoplus_{\phi\in\hat{W}}\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(e^{2\pi i\nu})\Delta_{\nu}(\phi).

We can immediately prove part (1) of Theorem
12
, stating that
L_{\nu}(1)
occurs in
\Omega_{\nu}
with multiplicity one.
Indeed, we have
D_{1}(\bm{q})=1
, and for any
\nu
, the only Verma module that contains
L_{\nu}(1)
as a constituent is
\Delta_{\nu}(1)
.

10.5.
Singular Slopes

We prove part (2) of Theorem
12
, stating that if
\nu
is singular, then
\Omega_{\nu}\in\mathsf{O}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{tor}}
.

Lemma 10.5.1
.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}D_{\phi}(\bm{q})\phi_{\bm{q}}(\sigma_{w})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{s}(1_{\bm{q}})&w=1\\
0&w\neq 1\end{array}\right.

in
\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

Proof.

If
W\simeq W_{1}\times W_{2}
, then
D_{\phi_{1}\times\phi_{2}}(\bm{q})=D_{\phi_{1}}(\bm{q})\cdot D_{\phi_{2}}(\bm{q})
for all
\phi_{i}\in\hat{W}_{i}
.
So we can assume that
W
is irreducible.
After checking the non-crystallographic types by hand, we can further assume that
W
is crystallographic.
