Henceforth, we will use the notations and hypotheses of Section
8
.
Recall that by (
261
), we have

(328)

\displaystyle R_{1}\simeq\bigoplus_{\phi\in\hat{W}}V_{\phi,q}^{\oplus\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(q)}

as
H_{W}(q^{\frac{1}{2}})
-modules, where
V_{\phi,q}
is a free
H_{W}(q^{\frac{1}{2}})
-module that admits the character
\phi_{q}
.
From (
258
), we deduce that:

(1)

\sigma_{1}
acts on
R_{1}
by the identity operator.
Its trace is
|\mathcal{B}^{F}|
.

(2)

If
w\neq 1
, then
\sigma_{w}
acts on
R_{1}
by an operator with zeros along the diagonal.

Since
|\mathcal{B}^{F}|=\bm{s}(1_{\bm{q}})|_{\bm{q}=q}
, we are done.
∎

By Theorem
10.3.1
(1), it remains to show that the
H_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})
-module
\mathsf{KZ}(\Omega_{\nu})
is the zero module.
By Theorem
10.3.1
(3), we have

(329)

\displaystyle\mathsf{KZ}(\Omega_{\nu})=\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\zeta)\phi_{\zeta}.

This coincides with the image of the virtual
H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
-module
\sum_{\phi}\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\bm{q})\phi_{\bm{q}}
under the decomposition map from §
A.11
.
So by Lemma
10.5.1
, it remains to show

(330)

\displaystyle\bm{s}(1_{\bm{q}})|_{\bm{q}^{1/2}=\zeta^{1/2}}=0.

This follows from the Bott–Solomon formula
[
Sol
]

(331)

\displaystyle\bm{s}(1_{\bm{q}})=\prod_{i\in I(W)}\frac{1-\bm{q}^{d_{i}}}{1-\bm{q}}

together with the definition of singular slope.

10.6.
Regular Slopes

We prove Corollary
11
, stating that if
\beta
is periodic of slope
\nu
, then
[\Omega_{\nu}]=(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{r-|\beta|}\cdot\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
.
On the one hand,

(332)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{|\beta|}\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}&=\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}\cdot\varepsilon[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}\\
&=\varepsilon\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}{\bm{q}^{\nu\mathbf{c}(\phi)}}\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\zeta)\phi\cdot[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}\end{split}

by Theorems
5
and
10
.
On the other hand,

(333)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\zeta)[\Delta_{\nu}(\phi)]_{\bm{q}}&=\bm{q}^{\frac{r}{2}}\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}{\bm{q}^{-\nu\mathbf{c}(\phi)}}\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\zeta)\phi\cdot[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}\\
&=\bm{q}^{\frac{r}{2}}\varepsilon\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}{\bm{q}^{\nu\mathbf{c}(\phi)}}\mathrm{Deg}_{\varepsilon\phi}(\zeta)\phi\cdot[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}\end{split}

by (
319
) and the identity
\mathbf{c}(\varepsilon\phi)=-\mathbf{c}(\phi)
.
Since
|\beta|=\nu|\pi|
, it remains to check:

Lemma 10.6.1
.

If
\nu\in\mathbf{Q}
is a regular slope and
\zeta=e^{2\pi i\nu}
, then

(334)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Deg}_{\varepsilon\psi}(\zeta)=(-1)^{\nu|\pi|}\mathrm{Deg}_{\psi}(\zeta)

for all
\psi\in\hat{W}
.

Proof.

Pick a
\zeta
-regular element
w\in W
.
By the discussion in Section
9
and Lemma-Postulate
A.9.1
(3),

(335)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{Deg}_{\varepsilon\psi}(\zeta)&=\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\varepsilon\psi\}}\phi(w)\\
&=\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}(\varepsilon\phi)(w)\\
&=\varepsilon(w)\mathrm{Deg}_{\psi}(\zeta).\end{split}

Finally,
\varepsilon(w)=(-1)^{\nu|\pi|}
because
|w|=|\sigma_{w}|=(\nu-\lfloor\nu\rfloor)|\pi|
.
∎

10.7.
Regular Elliptic Slopes

We prove part (3) of Theorem
12
, stating that if
\nu
is regular elliptic,
\nu>0
, and
W
is a Weyl group, then
[\Omega_{\nu}]_{\bm{q}}\in R(W)[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
.

Write
\nu=\frac{m}{n}
, where
m,n>0
.
Since
\frac{1}{n}
is also a regular elliptic slope, we can pick a
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{n}}
-regular element
w\in W
such that
\mathbf{V}^{w}=0
.
By Proposition
9.3.4
,
\sigma_{w}^{m}
is periodic of slope
\nu
, so by Corollary
11
,

(336)

\displaystyle[\Omega_{\nu}]_{\bm{q}}\in\langle{\sigma_{w}}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}\cdot R(W)[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}].

But
W
is a Weyl group, so by Theorem
9
, the condition
\mathbf{V}^{w}=0
implies that
\langle{\sigma_{w}}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}\in R(W)[\bm{q}]
.

10.8.
Cuspidal Slopes

We prove part (4) of Theorem
12
, stating that if
\nu
is cuspidal and positive, then

(337)

\displaystyle{[\Omega_{\nu}]_{\bm{q}}}=\sum_{\psi\in\hat{W}_{1}}{[L_{\nu}(\psi)]_{\bm{q}}},

where
\hat{W}_{1}\subseteq\hat{W}
is the set of elements of greatest
\mathbf{a}
-value within their
\zeta
-blocks, among the
\zeta
-blocks of
H_{W}
of defect
1
in the sense of §
A.11
.

Lemma 10.8.1
.

If
\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}}=e^{\pi i\nu}
, then the
\zeta
-defect of the principal
\zeta
-block of
H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
equals
|I_{\nu}(W)|
.

Proof.

The Bott–Solomon formula (
331
) can be rewritten

(338)

\displaystyle\bm{s}(1_{\bm{q}})=\prod_{n\geq 1}\Phi_{n}(\bm{q})^{|\{i\in I(W)\,:\,\text{$n$ divides $d_{i}$}\}|},

where
\Phi_{n}
is the minimal polynomial of
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{n}}
over
\mathbf{Q}_{W}
.
∎

Lemma 10.8.2
.

The
\zeta
-defect of any
\zeta
-block of
H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
is bounded above by
I_{\nu}(W)
.
Equality holds if and only if
\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\zeta)\neq 0
.
Moreover, if
|I_{\nu}(W)|\leq 1
, then there are two kinds of blocks:

(1)

Any block of defect
0
is a singleton.
For
\phi
in such a block,
\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\zeta)=0
.

(2)

Any block of defect
1
is a line of characters ordered by
\mathbf{a}
-value.
If

(339)

\displaystyle\{\phi_{0}>\phi_{1}>\cdots>\phi_{n-1}\}

is such a block, then
\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi_{k}}(\zeta)=(-1)^{k}
for all
k
.

Proof.

The only claim that does not follow from Theorem
A.12.1
and Lemma
10.8.1
is the claim about generic degrees in case (2).
Recall that, by definition,

(340)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi_{k}}(\zeta)=\left.\frac{\bm{s}(1_{\bm{q}})}{\bm{s}(\phi_{k,\bm{q}})}\right|_{\bm{q}^{1/2}\to\zeta^{1/2}}.

By work of Broué, such ratios of Schur elements at roots of unity are Morita invariants of the block ideal
[
Br
, §3.7]
.
(Strictly speaking, Broué worked with group algebras, not Iwahori–Hecke algebras, but see the footnote on
[
CM
, 286]
.)
Thus, by Theorem
A.12.1
(2), these ratios match the corresponding ratios for the Brauer tree algebra of type
A_{n-1}
.
In this way, we reduce to the setting where
W=S_{n}
and
\nu=\frac{1}{n}
.
Here, we have
\phi_{k}=\Alt^{k}(\mathbf{V})
, so it remains to show that

(341)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Deg}_{\Alt^{k}(\mathbf{V})}(e^{\frac{2\pi i}{n}})=(-1)^{k}.

Let
w
be an
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{n}}
-regular element of
W
.
By
[
J
, Rem. 3.7]
, the right-hand side above equals
\tr(w\mid\Alt^{k}(V))
.
By Lemma-Postulate
A.9.1
(2) and Theorem
9.3.2
, the left-hand side also equals
\tr(w\mid\Alt^{k}(V))
.
∎

We return to the proof of Theorem
12
(5).
Since
\nu
is cuspidal, Theorem
12
(3) and Lemma
10.8.2
together imply:

(342)

\displaystyle[\Omega_{\nu}]_{\bm{q}}=\sum_{\Gamma=\{\phi_{k}^{\Gamma}\}_{k}}\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}[\Delta_{\nu}(\phi_{k}^{\Gamma})]_{\bm{q}},

where the outer sum runs over
\zeta
-blocks of
H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
of defect
1
.
To conclude, we claim that the inner sum on the right-hand side simplifies to
[L_{\nu}(\phi_{0}^{\Gamma})]_{\bm{q}}
, where
\phi_{0}^{\Gamma}
is the element of greatest
\mathbf{a}
-value in the block
\Gamma
.
Indeed, this follows from the theorem below, itself a corollary of Theorem
10.3.1
(2).

Theorem 10.8.3
.

Suppose that
\{\phi_{0}>\phi_{1}>\cdots>\phi_{n-1}\}
is a
\zeta
-block of
H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
, ordered by
\mathbf{a}
-value.
Then, in
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}
, there is a “Bernstein–Gelfand–Gelfand” resolution of
L_{\nu}(\phi_{0})
of the form

(343)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\nu}(\phi_{n-1})\to\cdots\to\Delta_{\nu}(\phi_{1})\to\Delta_{\nu}(\phi_{0})\to L_{\nu}(\phi_{0})\to 0.

In particular, we have

(344)

\displaystyle[L_{\nu}(\phi_{0})]_{\bm{q}}=\sum_{0\leq k\leq n-1}{(-1)^{k}}[\Delta_{\nu}(\phi_{k})]_{\bm{q}}

in
R(W)[\![\bm{q}]\!]
.

Remark 10.8.4
.

We believe that the first appearance of this theorem in the literature is
[
R08
, Thm. 5.15]
.
The type
A
case was used much earlier, in
[
BEG-2
]
.

10.9.
Cuspidal Slopes,
cont.

We prove Corollary
13
.

There are only two cases where
W
is an irreducible Coxeter group,
\nu
is a cuspidal slope, and there is a
\zeta
-block of
H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
of defect
1
besides the principal block.
Namely,
W
must be of type
E_{8}
or type
H_{4}
and the denominator of
\nu
in lowest terms must be
15
.

In these cases, there is one other
\zeta
-block of defect
1
and its element of greatest
\mathbf{a}
-value is the character of
\mathbf{V}
, assuming as before that
\mathbf{V}^{W}=0
.
We refer to these as the two
exceptional
cases for
(W,\nu)
.
Parts (3)-(4) of Theorem
12
now imply the following stronger form of Corollary
13
:

Corollary 10.9.1
.

If
W
is irreducible and
\nu
is cuspidal, then:

\displaystyle[\Omega_{\nu}]_{\bm{q}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{[L_{\nu}(1)]_{\bm{q}}+[L_{\nu}(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}}&\text{$(W,\nu)$ is exceptional}\\
{[L_{\nu}(1)]_{\bm{q}}}&\text{else}\end{array}\right.

in
R(W)[\bm{q}]
.
In particular, the right-hand side is finite-dimensional.

Remark 10.9.2
.

This statement also recovers the observation of Bezrukavnikov–Etingof
[
BE
, Rem. 3.31]
that for irreducible
W
and cuspidal
\nu
, the only finite-dimensional simple
\mathbf{D}_{\nu}^{\mathit{rat}}
-modules are those appearing on the right-hand side of (
10.9.1
).

Remark 10.9.3
.

If
W\simeq W_{1}\times W_{2}
and
\mathbf{V}\simeq\mathbf{V}_{1}\oplus\mathbf{V}_{2}
, such that
\mathbf{V}_{i}
is a realization of
W_{i}
for
i=1,2
, then:

•

|I_{\nu}(W)|=|I_{\nu}(W_{1})|+|I_{\nu}(W_{2})|
, where the right-hand side is defined in terms of
\mathbf{V}_{1}
and
\mathbf{V}_{2}
rather than
\mathbf{V}
.

•

Category
\mathsf{O}
for the rational Cherednik algebra of
W
is the tensor product of the corresponding categories for
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
.

So the above result can be generalized to any pair
(W,\nu)
such that
\nu
is cuspidal with respect to each irredcible factor of
W
.

10.10.

To conclude this section, we explain how to deduce
[
GORS
, Thm. 1.1]
from Corollaries
13
and
7.4.6
.

For
W=S_{n}
, we have
r=n-1
and
|\pi|=n(n-1)
.
Let
s_{i}\in S_{n}
be the transposition
(i\penalty\ i+1)
.
Then the (positive)
(m,n)
torus knot is the link closure of

(347)

\displaystyle\beta=(\sigma_{s_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{s_{n-1}})^{m}\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+},

a periodic braid of slope
\frac{m}{n}
.
Since
n
is the Coxeter number of
S_{n}
, it is a cuspidal number for
S_{n}
.
Corollary
13
gives

(348)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{[L_{\nu}(1)]_{\bm{q}}}={[\Omega_{\nu}]_{\bm{q}}}&=(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{r-|\beta|}\cdot\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}.\end{split}

Since
|\beta|-n+1=\frac{m}{n}|\pi|-(n-1)=(m-1)(n-1)
, Corollary
7.4.6
gives

(349)

\displaystyle{[\hat{\beta}]_{a,\bm{q}}}=a^{(m-1)(n-1)}\sum_{0\leq i\leq n-1}{(-a^{2})^{i}}(\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V}),{[L_{\nu}(1)]_{\bm{q}}})_{W}.

This is
[
GORS
, Thm. 1.1]
.

Appendix A
Coxeter Groups and Their Characters

A.1.

Our main reference for this appendix is the book
[
GP
]
.

A
Coxeter group
is a group
W
that admits a finite presentation of the form

(350)

\displaystyle W=\langle s\in S:(st)^{m_{s,t}}=1\rangle,

where
m_{s,s}=1
and
m_{s,t}\geq 1
for all
s,t\in S
.
We say that
W
is
irreducible
iff it is both nontrivial and not a product of smaller Coxeter groups.
The pair
(W,S)
is called a
Coxeter system
, and
S
is called its set of
simple reflections
.
We say that
|S|
is the
rank
of
W
.

If
w\in W
, then an
expression
for
w
is a sequence
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell})
of elements of
S
such that
w=s_{1}\cdots s_{\ell}
.
The
reduced
expressions for
w
are those of minimal length.
The
Bruhat order
on
W
is the partial order where
w^{\prime}\leq w
iff some reduced expression for
w^{\prime}
is a subsequence of a reduced expression for
w
, not necessarily a contiguous one.
The common length of the reduced expressions for
w
is called the
Bruhat length
of
w
and denoted
|w|
.

If
W
is finite, then there is a unique element
w_{0}\in W
of maximal Bruhat length, known as the
longest element
of
(W,S)

[
GP
, §1.5]
.

A.2.

The
Artin braid monoid
of
(W,S)
, which we denote
\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
, is the monoid freely generated by formal elements
\sigma_{s}
for each
s\in S
, modulo the relations

(351)

\displaystyle\overbrace{\sigma_{s}\sigma_{t}\sigma_{s}\cdots}^{\text{$m_{s,t}$ terms}}=\overbrace{\sigma_{t}\sigma_{s}\sigma_{t}\cdots}^{\text{$m_{s,t}$ terms}}

for
distinct

s,t\in S
.
The
Artin braid group
of
(W,S)
, denoted
\mathit{Br}_{W}
, is the
group
with the same presentation.
By
[
GP
, Prop. 4.1.10]
, the natural morphism
\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}\to\mathit{Br}_{W}
is injective.
The elements of
\mathit{Br}_{W}
are called
braids
.

There is a surjective morphism
\mathit{Br}_{W}\to W
that sends
\sigma_{s}\mapsto s
.
The kernel is generated by the elements of the form
\sigma_{s}^{2}
.

Example A.2.1
.

In the Coxeter system of type
A_{n-1}
, the group
W
is the symmetric group
S_{n}
.
We can take
S
to consist of the transpositions
(i\penalty\ i+1)
for
1\leq i<n
.
We have
\mathit{Br}_{W}\simeq\mathit{Br}_{n}
, the group of topological braids on
n
strands.

If
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
, then an
expression
for
\beta
is a sequence
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell})
of elements of
S
such that
\beta=\sigma_{s_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{s_{\ell}}
.
All expressions for
\beta
have the same length, which is called the
writhe
of
\beta
and denoted
|\beta|
.
Unlike Bruhat length, writhe is additive:
It defines a morphism of monoids
|{-}|:\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}\to\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
, which extends uniquely to a morphism of groups
|{-}|:\mathit{Br}_{W}\to\mathbf{Z}
.

If
w\in W
and
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell})
is a reduced expression for
w
, then
\sigma_{w}=\sigma_{s_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{s_{\ell}}
depends only on
w
, not on the chosen expression.
The map
w\mapsto\sigma_{w}
defines a set-theoretic right inverse of the morphism
\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}\to W
such that
|\sigma_{w}|=|w|
.

A.3.

The
Iwahori–Hecke algebra
of
(W,S)
is a variant of the group ring
\mathbf{Z}[W]
where we modify the relations
s^{2}=1
to depend on a variable
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Namely, it is

(352)

\displaystyle H_{W}=\frac{\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}][\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}]}{\left\langle(\sigma_{s}-\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})(\sigma_{s}+\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}):s\in S\right\rangle}.

The set
\{\sigma_{w}\}_{w\in W}
forms a free
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-basis for
H_{W}
.
By induction on the Bruhat order, one can show that the multiplication table for this basis is given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{w}\sigma_{s}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sigma_{ws}&ws>w\\
\sigma_{ws}+(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})\sigma_{w}&ws<w\end{array}\right.

for all
s\in S
and
w\in W
.
Thus,
\sigma_{w}\mapsto w
defines an explicit isomorphism
H_{W}|_{\bm{q}^{1/2}=1}\simeq\mathbf{Z}[W]
.

A.4.

We say that a subgroup
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
is
S
-parabolic
iff it is generated by a subset of
S
.
In this case, the inclusion
W^{\prime}\to W
induces a writhe-preserving inclusion of positive braid monoids
\mathit{Br}_{W^{\prime}}^{+}\to\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
by way of the map
w\mapsto\sigma_{w}
.
This, in turn, induces an inclusion of Iwahori–Hecke algebras
H_{W^{\prime}}\to H_{W}
.

A.5.

All representations of
W
in this paper are finite-dimensional linear representations over (a subfield of)
\mathbf{C}
or an isomorphic field.
We write
R(W)
for the Grothendieck ring of virtual representations of
W
, and we write
\hat{W}\subseteq R(W)
for the subset of irreducible representations.
Abusing notation,
we treat the elements of
R(W)
interchangeably with their characters
.
Let
\mathbf{Q}_{W}\subseteq\mathbf{C}
be the field obtained by adjoining to
\mathbf{Q}
the character values
\phi(w)
for all
w\in W
and
\phi\in\hat{W}
.

We write
1
and
\varepsilon
for the trivial and sign characters of
W
, respectively.
By definition,
\varepsilon(w)=(-1)^{|w|}
.

Let
(-,-)_{W}:R(W)\times R(W)\to\mathbf{Z}
be the pairing defined by

(355)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\phi,\psi)_{W}&=\dim\Hom(1,\phi\otimes\psi)\\
&=\frac{1}{|W|}\sum_{w\in W}\phi(w)\psi(w^{-1})\end{split}

for all
\phi,\psi\in R(W)
.
For any ring
A
, we extend
(-,-)_{W}
to a pairing on
R(W)\otimes A
by linearity.

A.6.

If
K
is a subfield of
\mathbf{C}
or a field isomorphic to
\mathbf{C}
, then a
realization
of
(W,S)
over
K
consists of a finite-dimensional
K
-vector space
\mathbf{V}
and a (faithful) representation
W\to\GL(\mathbf{V})
that identifies
S
with a set of reflections in
\mathbf{V}
.
It will be convenient to set

(356)

\displaystyle{[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}}=\sum_{i}{\bm{q}^{i}}\Sym^{i}(\mathbf{V})\in R(W)[\![\bm{q}]\!].

If
\mathbf{V}^{W}=0
, then
[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}}
does not depend on the choice of
\mathbf{V}
.
In this case,

(357)

\displaystyle\bm{m}_{\phi}(\bm{q})=(\phi,[\Sym(\mathbf{V})]_{\bm{q}})_{W}

is known as the
Molien series
of
\phi\in\hat{W}
.

Every finite Coxeter system admits a realization over
\mathbf{R}
.
We say that
(W,S)
is
crystallographic
iff it admits a realization
\mathbf{V}
over
\mathbf{Q}
, in which case
W
is the Weyl group of a root system
\Phi\subseteq\mathbf{V}
and
S
is the set of reflections corresponding to a system of simple roots in
\Phi
.
For crystallographic Coxeter systems,
\mathbf{Q}_{W}=\mathbf{Q}

[
S74
, Cor. 4.8]
.

A.7.

We will abbreviate

(358)

\displaystyle H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})=\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]}H_{W}.

By
[
GP
, Thm. 9.3.5]
, we have an isomorphism of
\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
-algebras
H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})\simeq\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})[W]
.
In particular,
H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
is semisimple.
Writing
R(H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}))
for the Grothendieck ring of
H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
-modules, we obtain a ring isomorphism:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}R(W)&\xrightarrow{\sim}&R(H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}))\\
\phi&\mapsto&\phi_{\bm{q}}\end{array}

Again, we will identify the representations
\phi_{\bm{q}}
with their characters.
Thus we will write
\phi_{\bm{q}}(\beta)=\tr(\beta\mid\phi_{\bm{q}})
for all
\beta\in H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
\phi\in\hat{W}
.
For example, we have
1_{\bm{q}}(\sigma_{w})=(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{|w|}
for all
w\in W
.

Let
\tau_{\bm{q}}:H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})\to\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
be the symmetrizing trace defined by

\displaystyle\tau_{\bm{q}}(\sigma_{w})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&w=1\\
0&w\neq 1\end{array}\right.

The corresponding symmetrizer is
\sum_{w\in W}\sigma_{w}\otimes\sigma_{w^{-1}}\in H_{W}\otimes H_{W}
.
By Schur orthogonality
[
GP
, Cor. 7.2.4]
, we can write

(363)

\displaystyle\tau_{\bm{q}}=\sum_{\phi\in\hat{W}}\frac{1}{\bm{s}(\phi_{\bm{q}})}\,\phi_{\bm{q}},

where the Schur element
\bm{s}(\phi_{\bm{q}})\in\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
is defined by

(364)

\displaystyle\bm{s}(\phi_{\bm{q}})=\frac{1}{\phi(1)}\sum_{w\in W}\phi_{\bm{q}}(\sigma_{w})\phi_{\bm{q}}(\sigma_{w^{-1}}).

In particular,
\bm{s}(1_{\bm{q}})=\sum_{w\in W}\bm{q}^{|w|}
, the so-called Poincaré polynomial of
W
.

A.8.

To each
\phi\in\hat{W}
, we attach two polynomial invariants.

A.8.1.

The
fake degree
of
\phi
is the ratio

(365)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Feg}_{\phi}(\bm{q})=\frac{\bm{m}_{\phi}(\bm{q})}{\bm{m}_{1}(\bm{q})}.

It follows from Chevalley’s restriction theorem that
\mathrm{Feg}_{\phi}(\bm{q})\in\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}]

[
S74
, §2.5-2.6]
.

A.8.2.

The
generic
or
unipotent degree
of
\phi
is the ratio

(366)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\bm{q})=\frac{\bm{s}(1_{\bm{q}})}{\bm{s}(\phi_{\bm{q}})}.

Benson–Curtis showed
[
GP
, Cor. 9.3.6, Rem. 9.3.7]
that
\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\bm{q})\in\mathbf{Q}_{W}[\bm{q}]
.

A.9.

In
[
L84-1
,
L93
,
L94
]
, Lusztig assigned to every finite Coxeter group
W
a finite set
\mathrm{Uch}(W)
, equipped with:

(1)

An embedding
\hat{W}\to\mathrm{Uch}(W)
that we denote
\phi\mapsto\rho_{\phi}
.

(2)

A function
\mathrm{Uch}(W)\to\mathbf{Q}_{W}[\bm{q}]
that we denote
\rho\mapsto\mathrm{Deg}_{\rho}(\bm{q})
.
It satisfies
\mathrm{Deg}_{\rho_{\phi}}(\bm{q})=\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\bm{q})
for all
\phi\in\hat{W}
.

(3)

A symmetric orthogonal pairing

(367)

\displaystyle\{-,-\}:\mathrm{Uch}(W)\times\mathrm{Uch}(W)\to\mathbf{Q}_{W}

known as the
exotic Fourier transform
.

In type
H_{4}
, his work was completed by Malle
[
M
]
.

For
W
the Weyl group of a finite group of Lie type, Lusztig used this data to relate almost-characters parametrized by
\hat{W}
with unipotent irreducible characters parametrized by
\mathrm{Uch}(W)

[
L84-1
]
,
cf.
Section
8
.
For general
W
, Lusztig gave a list of postulates that characterize this data uniquely
[
L93
, §2]
.

Outside of Section
8
, we only use the restriction of the exotic Fourier transform to a pairing on
\hat{W}
: that is, the pairing

(368)

\displaystyle\{-,-\}:\hat{W}\times\hat{W}\to\mathbf{Q}_{W}.

defined by
\{\phi,\psi\}=\{\rho_{\phi},\rho_{\psi}\}
for all
\phi,\psi\in\hat{W}
.

Lemma-Postulate A.9.1
.

Let
W
be any finite Coxeter group.

(1)

If
W=W_{1}\times W_{2}
, then

(369)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Uch}(W)=\mathrm{Uch}(W_{1})\times\mathrm{Uch}(W_{2})

and
\{-,-\}
is similarly multiplicative.

(2)

For all
\phi\in\hat{W}
, we have

(370)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\bm{q})=\sum_{\psi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}\mathrm{Feg}_{\psi}(\bm{q}).

(3)

For all
\phi,\psi\in\hat{W}
, we have
\{\varepsilon\phi,\varepsilon\psi\}=\{\phi,\psi\}
.

Proof.

Part (1) is
[
L93
, Postulate 2.3]
.
Part (2) is
[
C
, §13.6]
.
Part (3) does not seem to be stated in the literature directly, but for Weyl groups, it is a consequence of Alvis–Curtis–Kawanaka duality
[
C
, §8.2]
.
For general
W
, we can check it by using part (1) to reduce to the irreducible case.
∎

Remark A.9.2
.

In
[
Ma
, Thm. 4.3]
and the remark below it, Marberg claims that in types
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
, the identity in part (2) only holds up to precomposition with a certain involution described in
[
BL
]
.
Cross-referencing against the tables in
[
C
, §13.9]
and
[
L84-1
, Ch. 4]
shows that this claim is incorrect.

Recall the character decomposition (
363
) of the symmetrizing trace
\tau_{\bm{q}}
.
Using Lemma-Postulate
A.9.1
(2), we can re-express these weights in terms of Molien series and the exotic Fourier transform.

Proposition A.9.3
.

For all
\phi\in\hat{W}
, we have

(371)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\bm{s}(\phi_{\bm{q}})}=(1-\bm{q})^{r}\sum_{\psi\in\hat{W}}{\{\phi,\psi\}}\bm{m}_{\psi}(\bm{q})

in
\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

Proof.

Let
r=|S|
.
By Lemma-Postulate
A.9.1
(2), we must prove
\bm{s}(1_{\bm{q}})\bm{m}_{1}(\bm{q})=(1-\bm{q})^{-r}
.
Indeed, if
d_{1},\ldots,d_{r}
are the invariant degrees of the
W
-action on
\mathbf{V}

[
GP
, 149]
, then we have

(372)

\displaystyle\bm{s}(1_{\bm{q}})=\prod_{1\leq i\leq r}\frac{1-\bm{q}^{d_{i}}}{1-\bm{q}}\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{m}_{1}(\bm{q})=\prod_{1\leq i\leq r}{\frac{1}{1-\bm{q}^{d_{i}}}}.

The left-hand side is the Bott–Solomon formula for the Poincaré polynomial of
W

[
Sol
]
.
The right-hand side is true by definition.
∎

A.10.

In
[
L84-1
]
, Lusztig introduced functions
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{A}:\hat{W}\to\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
defined by

(373)

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\val_{\bm{q}}\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\bm{q}),

(374)

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\deg_{\bm{q}}\mathrm{Deg}_{\phi}(\bm{q}).

Using induction on parabolic subgroups of
W
, he also described a partition of
\hat{W}
into subsets called
families
.
The functions
\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{A}
are constant in families, and the matrix of the pairing
\{-,-\}
is block-diagonal with respect to them.

Example A.10.1
.

In type
A
, it turns out that
\hat{W}\to\mathrm{Uch}(W)
is bijective, the matrix of
\{-,-\}
is the identity matrix, and every family in
\hat{W}
is a singleton.

We use
\mathbf{a}
directly, whereas we only use
\mathbf{A}
by way of the auxiliary function that follows.
Let
\mathrm{Ref}(W)
be the set of elements of
W
that act on any realization by reflections.
(It is the union of the conjugacy classes that meet
S
.)
Motivated by the theory of partitions, we introduce:

Definition A.10.2
.

For all
\phi\in\hat{W}
, the
content
of
\phi
is the number

(375)

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}(\phi)=\frac{1}{\phi(1)}\sum_{t\in\mathrm{Ref}(W)}\phi(t).

Note that
\mathbf{c}(\mathbf{1})=|\mathrm{Ref}(W)|
.
If
W
is the Weyl group of a root system
\Phi
, then this number equals
\frac{1}{2}|\Phi|
.

If
W=S_{n}
, then there is a bijective correspondence between
\hat{W}
and the set of integer partitions of
n
.
Under this bijection,
\mathbf{c}(\phi)
is precisely the content of the partition attached to
\phi
.
For general
W
, the following result about
\mathbf{c}
is
[
BM97
, §4.21]
, which relies on
[
L84-1
, §5.12]
:

Lemma A.10.3

(Broué–Michel)
.

We have
\mathbf{c}(\phi)=N-\mathbf{a}(\phi)-\mathbf{A}(\phi)
for all
\phi\in\hat{W}
.

Corollary A.10.4
.

The function
\mathbf{c}
is constant in families.

A.11.

To conclude this appendix, we review the block theory of Iwahori–Hecke algebras at roots of unity, relying on
[
GP
, Ch. 7]
.

Given any ring
H
, we write
R(H)
for the Grothendieck group of finite-dimensional
H
-modules.
The relations on
R(H)
are
[M]=[M^{\prime}]+[M^{\prime\prime}]
for each short exact sequence
0\to M^{\prime}\to M\to M^{\prime\prime}\to 0
.
We write
R^{+}(H)\subseteq R(H)
for the semiring of actual, not virtual, modules.

A.11.1.

Let
E
be a field of characteristic zero, and let
A\subseteq E
be a subring.
Let
H
be a free
A
-algebra of finite rank such that the
E
-algebra

(376)

\displaystyle H_{E}=H\otimes_{A}E

is split semisimple.
Let
k
be another field and
\theta:A\to k
a ring morphism.
We set

(377)

\displaystyle H_{\theta}=H\otimes_{A}k.

We will review how Brauer’s theory of decomposition maps gives a direct relation between the representation theories of
H_{E}
and
H_{\theta}
, or more precisely, between
R(H_{E})
and
R(H_{\theta})
, as long as
A
is integrally closed in
E
.

Let
t
be an indeterminate over
K
.
We have a map:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}R^{+}(H_{E})&\xrightarrow{\bm{p}}&\prod_{H}{(1+tK[t])}\\[4.30554pt]
M&\mapsto&\{\det_{E}(1-t\alpha\mid M)\}_{\alpha\in H}\end{array}

The Brauer–Nesbitt theorem
[
GP
, Lem. 7.3.2]
implies that
\bm{p}
is injective.
Furthermore,
[
GP
, Prop. 7.3.8]
states that if
A
is integrally closed in
E
, then
\bm{p}
factors through
(1+tA[t])^{H}
.

Let
\bm{p}_{\theta}:R^{+}(H_{\theta})\to(1+k[t])^{H}
be defined by analogy to
\bm{p}
.
Brauer’s theorem
[
GP
, Thm. 7.4.3]
states that if
A
is integrally closed in
E
, then there is a unique additive map
\bm{d}_{\theta}:R^{+}(H_{E})\to R^{+}(H_{\theta})
making the following diagram commute:

(380)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{+}(H_{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1+tA[t])^{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{+}(H_{\theta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1+tk[t])^{H}}
\bm{p}
\bm{d}_{\theta}
\theta
\bm{p}_{\theta}

In particular, the map
\bm{d}_{\theta}
is compatible with the formation of characters from modules.
It is called the
decomposition map
of
\theta
.
Note that if
\phi\in R^{+}(H_{E})
is irreducible, then
\bm{d}_{\theta}(\phi)\in R^{+}(H_{\theta})
is usually no longer irreducible.

Let
\hat{H}_{E}\subseteq R^{+}(H_{E})
be the set of isomorphism classes of simple modules.
The
Brauer graph
of
H_{\theta}
is the (undirected) graph in which:

(1)

The vertex set is
\hat{H}_{E}
.

(2)

There is an edge between
M
and
M^{\prime}
iff there is a simple module shared in common by the decompositions of
\bm{d}_{\theta}(M)
and
\bm{d}_{\theta}(M^{\prime})
in
R^{+}(H_{\theta})
.

The connected components of this graph are called the
\theta
-blocks
of
H_{E}
.
The partition of
\hat{H}_{E}
into blocks corresponds to a direct-sum decomposition of
H_{\theta}
into idempotent ideals called
block ideals
, such that the simple modules over any block ideal are in bijection with the characters in the corresponding block.

A.11.2.

Fix a number
\nu\in\mathbf{Q}
.
Let
\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}}=e^{\pi i\nu}
, and let

(381)

\displaystyle H_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})=\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]}H_{W},

where the map
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]\to\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})
sends
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Let
\mathbf{Z}_{W}
be the ring of integers of
\mathbf{Q}_{W}
.
Then in the notation above, we can take

(382)

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}),

(383)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Z}_{W}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}],

(384)

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=H_{W}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{Z}_{W},

(385)

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})).

Let
\theta:A\to k
be the specialization map
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
These choices give
H_{E}=H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
H_{\theta}=H_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
So, Brauer’s theorem yields a decomposition map:

(386)

\displaystyle\bm{d}_{\zeta}\vcentcolon=\bm{d}_{\theta}:R^{+}(H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}))\to R^{+}(H_{W}(\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}})).

We will refer to the
\theta
-blocks of
H_{W}(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})
as
\zeta
-blocks
.
The
principal block
is the block that contains the trivial character
1
.

A.12.

By
[
GP
, Prop. 7.3.9]
, we have

(387)

\displaystyle\bm{s}(\phi_{\bm{q}})\in\mathbf{Z}_{W}[\bm{q}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]

for all
\phi\in\hat{W}
.
The
\zeta
-defect
of
\phi
is the multiplicity with which
\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}=\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}}
occurs as a root of the Laurent polynomial
\bm{s}(\phi_{\bm{q}})
(after we extend scalars).
This number measures the complexity of the
\zeta
-block containing
\phi
in the following sense:

Theorem A.12.1

(Geck)
.

The characters within a given
\zeta
-block have the same
\zeta
-defect, so we can speak of the defect of the block itself.
Moreover:

(1)

If the defect is
0
, then the block is a singleton.

(2)

If the defect is
1
, then the block is a line of characters ordered by
\mathbf{a}
-value.
Its block ideal is isomorphic to a Brauer tree algebra of type
A
(see below).

Proof.

This restates Prop. 7.4, Prop. 8.2, and Thm. 9.6 of
[
G92
]
.
∎

Remark A.12.2
.

By contrast, we do not know of a structure theorem for blocks of defect
\geq 2
.

Example A.12.3
.

Suppose that we are in type
A_{n-1}
, meaning
W\simeq S_{n}
, and that
\nu=\frac{m}{n}
with
m
coprime to
n
.
Here, the principal
\zeta
-block is a line.
If
\mathbf{V}
is the standard representation of
S_{n}
, then the principal block consists of the exterior powers
\Alt^{i}(\mathbf{V})
, ordered along the line from
i=0
to
i=n-1
.
We refer to the associated block ideal as the
Brauer tree algebra of type
A_{n-1}
.
All other blocks are singletons.

Appendix B
Braid Varieties in the Literature

B.1.

In this appendix, we explain how the varieties
\mathcal{U}(\beta),\mathcal{Z}(\beta),\mathcal{X}(\beta)
are related to other varieties that have appeared in the literature.
Throughout, we assume the notation and hypotheses of Sections
1
-
2
.
All varieties in this section can be constructed over
\mathbf{F}
, not just
\bar{\mathbf{F}}
, but we will omit the subscript
0
’s anyway to lighten notation.

B.2.

Let
w\in W
.
As in Section
2
, let
O_{w}\subseteq\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}
be the
G
-orbit indexed by
w
, and let
\mathit{act}:G\times\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}
be the map

(388)

\displaystyle\mathit{act}(g,B)=(g^{-1}Bg,B).

In the following diagram, all squares are cartesian:

(389)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{U}}\times\mathcal{B}}
\mathit{act}

To the best of my knowledge:

(1)

For fixed
g\in G
and
w
a Coxeter element of minimal length, the fiber
(G_{w})_{g}\subseteq G_{w}
first appears
[
St65
, §4]
.
For general
w
, it first appears in Lem. 3.6 of Kawanaka’s paper
[
Kaw
]
, where it is denoted
F_{g,w}
, and independently in Lusztig’s note
[
L80
]
, where it is denoted
Y_{s,w}
in the case where
g
is a regular semisimple element
s
.
More recently, it has appeared in Lusztig’s paper
[
L11
]
, where it is denoted
\mathcal{B}_{g}^{w}
, and in D. Kim’s papers
[
K18-1
,
K18-2
]
, where it is denoted
\mathcal{Y}_{w,g}
.

(2)

The variety
G_{w}
first appears in Lusztig’s paper
[
L85-I
]
, where it is denoted
Y_{w}
.
It is used throughout his series of works on character sheaves.
It also appears in
[
L11
]
and its sequels, where it is denoted
\mathfrak{B}_{w}
.

(3)

The varieties
\mathcal{U}_{w}^{\mathrm{red}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{w}^{\mathrm{red}}
first appear in Lusztig’s note
[
L21
]
, where they are respectively denoted
\mathfrak{B}_{w}^{(1)}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{B}}_{w}^{1}
.

More generally:
Let
\mathcal{T}
be the universal maximal torus of
G
, so that there are maps
G\to\mathcal{T}\sslash W
and
\tilde{G}\to\mathcal{T}
.
For any
\delta\in\mathcal{T}
with image
(\delta)\in\mathcal{T}\sslash W
, Lusztig defines varieties
\mathfrak{B}_{w}^{(\delta)}\subseteq G_{w}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{B}}_{w}^{\delta}\subseteq\tilde{G}\times_{G}G_{w}
as the corresponding fibers.

B.3.

Let
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
be a positive braid of writhe
\ell
.
Thus we have
\beta=\sigma_{s_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{s_{\ell}}
for some
s_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell}\in S
.
By definition,

(390)

\displaystyle O(\beta)=\{(B_{0},B_{1},\ldots,B_{\ell}):\text{$(B_{i-1},B_{i})\in O_{s_{i}}$ for $1\leq i\leq\ell$}\}.

In the following diagram, all squares are cartesian:

(391)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{U}}\times\mathcal{B}}
\mathit{pr}_{0}\times\mathit{pr}_{\ell}
\mathit{act}

In addition, we will abbreviate

(392)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(\beta)\vcentcolon=G(\beta)_{1}=\mathcal{U}(\beta)_{1}.

To the best of my knowledge:

(1)

For general
\beta
, the variety
O(\beta)
first appears in Broué–Michel’s paper
[
BM97
]
, where it is denoted
\mathcal{B}(\beta)
.
See also
[
D97
, Application 2]
.
However, it is arguably implicit in the works of Bott–Samelson
[
BS
]
and Hansen
[
H
]
.
In §6 of the paper
[
STZ
]
by Shende–Treumann–Zaslow,
O(\beta)
is called the open Bott–Samelson variety of
\beta
and denoted
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\beta)
.

(2)

The variety
G(\beta)
first appears in
[
L85-I
, §2.5]
, where it is denoted
Y_{\mathbf{w}}
in the case where
\mathbf{w}
is an expression for
\beta
(see §
A.2
).
Later, it implicitly appears in
[
STZ
, §6]
along with
\mathcal{X}(\beta)
:
In the notation of
loc. cit.
,

(393)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\beta^{\circ})

\displaystyle=[G(\beta)/G],

(394)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\beta^{\succ})

\displaystyle=[\mathcal{X}(\beta\pi)/G].

(The authors assume
G=\PGL_{n}
, but this hypothesis is extraneous.)
From the viewpoint of cluster geometry, the stack
\mathcal{M}(\beta^{\succ})
has been studied by L. Shen and D. Weng in
[
SW
]
:
It is the special case of their stack
\mathrm{Conf}_{d}^{b}(\mathcal{B})
where
b=\beta
and
d=\mathbf{1}
.

(3)

For fixed
B\in\mathcal{B}
, the variety
\mathcal{X}(\beta)_{B}^{\mathrm{red}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}(\beta)^{\mathrm{red}}
first appears in §5.3 of Mellit’s paper
[
Me
]
in an isomorphic form denoted
X_{0}(\beta)
, in the case where
G=\GL_{n}
.
In the next subsection, we generalize Mellit’s definition to arbitrary reductive
G
and explain the isomorphism in detail.

More recently,
X_{0}(\beta)
has been studied by Casals, Gorsky, Gorsky, and Simental in the paper
[
CGGS
]
:
It is the special case of their variety
X_{0}(\gamma;\pi)
where
\gamma=\beta
and
\pi=\mathbf{1}
.
(Note that in their notation,
\pi
is an element of
S_{n}
, not the full twist.)

(4)

The schemes
\mathcal{U}(\beta)
and
\mathcal{Z}(\beta)
are constructed similarly to the representation varieties
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{sing}}^{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{1}
in
[
Me
, §8]
.
However, they are not a special case of those varieties, or vice versa.

B.4.

Below, we relate the scheme
\mathcal{X}(\beta)
to the variety
X_{0}(\beta)
in
[
Me
]
, hence to the varieties in
[
CGGS
]
.

Fix a maximal torus
T\subseteq G
and a Borel
B\supseteq T
.
For each
s\in S
, we have a corresponding positive root subgroup

(395)

\displaystyle u_{\alpha_{s}}:\mathbf{A}^{1}\to U_{\alpha_{s}}\subseteq B.

Recall that
N_{G}(T)/T\simeq W
.
Fix a section
W\to N_{G}(T)
, to be denoted
w\mapsto w_{T}
.
For all
s\in S
, let
f_{\alpha_{s}}:\mathbf{A}^{1}\to G
be the map

(396)

\displaystyle f_{\alpha_{s}}(z)=s_{T}\cdot u_{\alpha_{s}}(z).

Let
f_{\beta,B}:\mathbf{A}^{\ell}\to G
be the map

(397)

\displaystyle f_{\beta,B}(\vec{z})=f_{\alpha_{s_{1}}}(z_{1})\cdots f_{\alpha_{s_{\ell}}}(z_{\ell}).

With these notations, let

(398)

X_{0}(\beta,B)=f_{\beta,B}^{-1}(B)\subseteq\mathbf{A}^{\ell}.

For
G=\GL_{n}
and
B
fixed, the underlying reduced scheme of
X_{0}(\beta,B)
is the variety denoted
X_{0}(\beta)
in
[
Me
, §5.3]
.

Example B.4.1
.

Let
G=\GL_{2}
and
B
the upper-triangular subgroup.
In this case,

(399)

\displaystyle f_{\alpha_{s}}(z)=\left(\begin{matrix}&1\\
1&z\end{matrix}\right).

Let
\sigma
be the positive generator of
\mathit{Br}_{W}=\mathit{Br}_{2}
, and let
\mathbf{G}
be the punctured affine line.
Then we can check that:

(400)

\displaystyle X_{0}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\emptyset,

(401)

\displaystyle X_{0}(\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle=\{\vec{z}\in\mathbf{A}^{2}:z_{1}=0\}

\displaystyle=\mathit{pt}\times\mathbf{A}^{1},

(402)

\displaystyle X_{0}(\sigma^{3})

\displaystyle=\{\vec{z}\in\mathbf{A}^{3}:1+z_{1}z_{2}=0\}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{G}\times\mathit{pt}\times\mathbf{A}^{1},

(403)

\displaystyle X_{0}(\sigma^{4})

\displaystyle=\{\vec{z}\in\mathbf{A}^{4}:z_{1}+z_{3}+z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}=0\}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{A}^{1}\times\mathit{pt}\times\mathbf{A}^{1})\sqcup(\mathbf{G}\times\mathbf{A}^{1}\times\mathbf{G}).

The argument in
[
Me
, §5.4]
shows that for general
G
and
\beta
, it is always possible to stratify
X_{0}(\beta)
into cells of the form
\mathbf{A}^{a}\times\mathbf{G}^{b}
with
a,b\geq 0
.

It would be desirable to organize the varieties
X_{0}(\beta,B)
into an algebraic family over the flag variety
\mathcal{B}
.
I do not know how to do this directly, given the dependence of
f_{\beta,B}
on the section
w\mapsto w_{T}
.
Nonetheless:

Proposition B.4.2
.

For all
\beta\in\mathit{Br}_{W}^{+}
, there is a commutative diagram

(404)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times\mathbf{A}^{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}(\beta,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1\}}
(f_{\beta,B}(\vec{z}),\vec{z})
f_{\beta,B}
\id\times\{B\}

where the middle vertical map sends
(b,\vec{z})\mapsto b^{-1}f_{\beta,B}(\vec{z})
and both squares are cartesian.
In particular,
X_{0}(\beta,B)
is isomorphic to the fiber
G(\beta)_{1,B}=\mathcal{X}(\beta)_{B}
.

Proof.

It is enough to construct the map
B\times\mathbf{A}^{\ell}\to G(\beta)
.
To this end, let
B^{\ell-1}
act on
Bs_{1,T}B\times\cdots\times Bs_{\ell,T}B
on the right by:

(405)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{i},\ldots,x_{\ell})\cdot(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell-1})\\
&=(x_{1}b_{1},b_{1}^{-1}x_{2}b_{2},\ldots,b_{i-1}^{-1}x_{i}b_{i},\ldots,b_{\ell-1}^{-1}x_{\ell}).\end{split}

We denote the quotient by

(406)

\displaystyle M(\beta,B)=(Bs_{1,T}B\times\cdots\times Bs_{\ell,T}B)/B^{\ell-1}.

(When
\beta=\sigma_{w}
, this variety is a dense open stratum in the variety denoted
M_{w}
in
[
H
]
.)
We have an isomorphism:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}B\times\mathbf{A}^{\ell}&\xrightarrow{\sim}&M(\beta,B)\\
(b,\vec{z})&\mapsto&[bf_{\alpha_{s_{1}}}(z_{1}),f_{\alpha_{s_{2}}}(z_{2}),\ldots,f_{\alpha_{s_{\ell}}}(z_{\ell})]\end{array}

Let
B
act on
M(\beta,B)
by outer right conjugation and on
G
by right multiplication.
Writing
x^{g}=g^{-1}xg
and
B^{g}=g^{-1}Bg
, we have a further isomorphism:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}(M(\beta,B)\times G)/B&\xrightarrow{\sim}&G(\beta)\\
{[x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell},g]}&\mapsto&((x_{1}\cdots x_{\ell})^{g},B^{x_{2}\cdots x_{\ell}g},\ldots,B^{g})\end{array}

We now have a composition

(411)

\displaystyle\begin{split}M(\beta,B)&\xrightarrow{\sim}(M(\beta,B)\times B)/B\to(M(\beta,B)\times G)/B\xrightarrow{\sim}G(\beta).\end{split}

It forms the top arrow of a cartesian diagram

(412)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\beta,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
m_{\beta,B}
\id\times\{B\}

in which
m_{\beta,B}([x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}])=x_{1}\cdots x_{\ell}
.
This is the left-hand square of the diagram in the proposition, once we identify
M(\beta,B)
with
B\times\mathbf{A}^{\ell}
.
∎

Remark B.4.3
.

The isomorphism (
B.4
) can be pulled back to an isomorphism

(413)

\displaystyle(X_{0}(\beta,B)\times G)/B\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{X}(\beta).

Thus, we have an isomorphism of stacks:

(414)

\displaystyle[X_{0}(\beta,B)/B]\simeq[\mathcal{X}(\beta)/G].

Note that
[X_{0}(\beta,B)/B]
is homotopy equivalent to
[X_{0}(\beta,B)/T]
.
The
T
-action on
X_{0}(\beta,B)
is important in
[
Me
]
and
[
CGGS
]
.

Appendix C
Examples of the Decategorified Trace

In this appendix, we list some values of
\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}
and
\langle{-}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
.
Throughout, we rely on Table 8.1 in
[
GP
]
and the descriptions of
\{-,-\}
in
[
L84-1
, Ch. 4]
.

Type
A_{1}

Here,
W=S_{2}
and
\hat{W}=\{1,\varepsilon\}
.
Writing
\sigma
for the positive generator of
\mathit{Br}_{W}=\mathit{Br}_{2}
, we have:

(415)

\displaystyle\langle{\sigma^{m}}\rangle_{\bm{q}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{m}+(-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{m}\varepsilon,

(416)

\displaystyle\langle{\sigma^{m}}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\bm{q}^{2}}((1-(-\bm{q})^{m+1})+(\bm{q}+(-\bm{q})^{m})\varepsilon).

For instance, we have
\langle{\sigma^{2k+1}}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}=(1+\bm{q}^{2}+\cdots+\bm{q}^{2k})+(\bm{q}+\bm{q}^{3}+\cdots+\bm{q}^{2k-1})\varepsilon
for all
k\geq 1
.

Type
A_{2}

Here,
W=S_{3}
.
We write
S=\{s,t\}
and
\hat{W}=\{1,\phi,\varepsilon\}
, where
\phi(1)=2
.
The following table lists
(\psi,\langle{\sigma_{w}}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}
for all
w\in W
and
\psi\in\hat{W}
:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r@{\quad}|@{\quad}l@{}ll@{}ll@{}ll@{}l}&&\mathbf{1}&&\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t}&&\sigma_{st},\sigma_{ts}&&\sigma_{sts}\\[4.30554pt]
1&&1&&\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}&&\bm{q}&&\bm{q}^{\frac{3}{2}}\\
\phi&&2&&\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&-&1&&0\\
\varepsilon&&1&-&\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&&\bm{q}^{-1}&-&\bm{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}}\end{array}

We deduce that:

\displaystyle\langle{\sigma_{w}}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(1-\bm{q})^{-2}(1+2\phi+\varepsilon)&w=1\\
(1-\bm{q})^{-1}(1+\phi)&w\in\{s,t\}\\
1&w\in\{st,ts\}\\
(1-\bm{q})^{-1}(1-\bm{q}+\bm{q}^{2}+\bm{q}\phi)&w=w_{0}\end{array}\right.

Type
BC_{2}

Here,
W
is the dihedral group of the square.
We write
S=\{s,t\}
and
\hat{W}=\{1,\delta,\phi,\varepsilon\delta,\epsilon\}
, where
\delta(1)=2
and
\phi(1)=2
and
\delta(s)=1
.
The following table lists
(\psi,\langle{\sigma_{w}}\rangle_{\bm{q}})_{W}
for all
w
and
\psi
:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r@{\quad}|@{\quad}l@{}ll@{}ll@{}ll@{}ll@{}ll@{}ll@{}l}&&\mathbf{1}&&\sigma_{s}&&\sigma_{t}&&\sigma_{st},\sigma_{ts}&&\sigma_{sts}&&\sigma_{tst}&&\sigma_{w_{0}}\\[4.30554pt]
1&&1&&\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}&&\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}&&\bm{q}&&\bm{q}^{\frac{3}{2}}&&\bm{q}^{\frac{3}{2}}&&\bm{q}^{2}\\
\delta&&1&&\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-&\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&&0&-&\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&&\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-&1\\
\phi&&2&&\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&&\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&-&1&&0&&0&&0\\
\varepsilon\delta&&1&-&\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&&\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}&&0&&\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-&\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&-&1\\
\varepsilon&&1&-&\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&-&\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&&\bm{q}^{-1}&-&\bm{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}}&-&\bm{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}}&&\bm{q}^{-2}\end{array}

We deduce that:

\displaystyle\langle{\sigma_{w}}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(1-\bm{q})^{-2}(1+\delta+2\phi+\varepsilon\delta+\varepsilon)&w=1\\
(1-\bm{q})^{-1}(1+\delta+\phi)&w=s\\
(1-\bm{q})^{-1}(1+\varepsilon\delta+\phi)&w=t\\
1&w\in\{st,ts\}\\
(1-\bm{q})^{-1}(1-\bm{q}+\bm{q}^{2}+\bm{q}\varepsilon\delta+\bm{q}\phi)&w=sts\\
(1-\bm{q})^{-1}(1-\bm{q}+\bm{q}^{2}+\bm{q}\delta+\bm{q}\phi)&w=tst\\
1+\bm{q}^{2}+\bm{q}\phi&w=w_{0}\end{array}\right.

Note that this is the smallest
W
for which
\{-,-\}
is nontrivial.

An Iterated Torus Braid

Let
W=S_{4}
.
We write
S=\{s,t,u\}
and
\hat{W}=\{1,\phi,\psi,\varepsilon\phi,\varepsilon\}
, where
\phi(1)=3
and
\psi(1)=2
and
\phi(s)=1
.
Let

(438)

\displaystyle\beta=(\sigma_{s}\sigma_{t}\sigma_{u})^{6}\sigma_{s}\in\mathit{Br}_{W}=\mathit{Br}_{4}.

Using SageMath, we calculated:

(439)

\displaystyle\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}=\bm{q}^{\frac{19}{2}}-\bm{q}^{\frac{7}{2}}\phi+(\bm{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bm{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}})\psi+\bm{q}^{-\frac{7}{2}}\varepsilon\phi-\bm{q}^{-\frac{19}{2}}\varepsilon.

The nonzero coefficients of
\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
are listed below:

\displaystyle\small\begin{array}[]{r@{\quad}|@{\quad}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}p{0.75em}}\hskip 9.24994pt\vrule\hskip 9.24994pt&$\bm{q}^{0}$&$\bm{q}^{1}$&$\bm{q}^{2}$&$\bm{q}^{3}$&$\bm{q}^{4}$&$\bm{q}^{5}$&$\bm{q}^{6}$&$\bm{q}^{7}$&$\bm{q}^{8}$&$\bm{q}^{9}$&$\bm{q}^{10}$&$\bm{q}^{11}$&$\bm{q}^{12}$&$\bm{q}^{13}$&$\bm{q}^{14}$&$\bm{q}^{15}$&$\bm{q}^{16}$\\[3.87498pt]
1\hskip 9.24994pt\vrule\hskip 9.24994pt&1&&1&1&2&1&&1&&1&&1&2&1&1&&1\\
\phi\hskip 9.24994pt\vrule\hskip 9.24994pt&&1&1&2&2&&&&&&&&2&2&1&1&\\
\psi\hskip 9.24994pt\vrule\hskip 9.24994pt&&&1&&2&1&&1&&1&&1&2&&1&&\\
\varepsilon\phi\hskip 9.24994pt\vrule\hskip 9.24994pt&&&&1&1&2&2&&2&&2&2&1&1&&&\\
\varepsilon\hskip 9.24994pt\vrule\hskip 9.24994pt&&&&&&&1&&1&&1&&&&&&\end{array}

This table offers a vivid illustration of Theorem
6
.
By Proposition
7.4.3
, the rows of the table for
1,\phi,\varepsilon\phi
, and
\varepsilon
list the coefficients of the HOMFLY polynomial of the link closure
\hat{\beta}
, up to shifting by
a^{2i}\bm{q}^{-8}
for various
i
.

Remark C.0.1
.

As it happens,
\hat{\beta}
is the link of the complex plane curve singularity given by
y=x^{\frac{3}{2}}+x^{\frac{7}{4}}
.
In particular, it is an iterated torus knot.

Remark C.0.2
.

Let
G=\SL_{4}
.
By Theorem
7
, it should be possible to decompose
\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}
into a
\mathbf{Z}[\bm{q}]
-linear combination of total Springer representations
Q_{u}
, as we run over representatives
u
of
G^{F}
-orbits in
\mathcal{U}^{F}
.

The
G^{F}
-orbits are indexed by the partitions of the integer
4
.
Let the orbit of the identity correspond to the partition
(1,1,1,1)\vdash 4
.
Then the
Q_{u}
are:

(446)

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q_{4}&=1,\\
Q_{3,1}&=1+\bm{q}\phi,\\
Q_{2,2}&=1+\bm{q}\phi+\bm{q}^{2}\psi,\\
Q_{2,1,1}&=1+(\bm{q}+\bm{q}^{2})\phi+\bm{q}^{2}\psi+\bm{q}^{3}\varepsilon\phi,\\
Q_{1,1,1,1}&=1+(\bm{q}+\bm{q}^{2}+\bm{q}^{3})\phi+(\bm{q}^{2}+\bm{q}^{4})\psi+(\bm{q}^{3}+\bm{q}^{4}+\bm{q}^{5})\varepsilon\phi+\bm{q}^{6}\varepsilon.\end{split}

Using (
C
), we find that:

(447)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle{\beta}\rangle_{\bm{q}}^{0}&=\bm{q}^{16}Q_{1}+(\bm{q}^{10}+\bm{q}^{11}+\bm{q}^{12}+\bm{q}^{13}+\bm{q}^{14})Q_{3,1}\\
&\qquad+(\bm{q}^{6}-\bm{q}^{7}+\bm{q}^{8}+\bm{q}^{10}+\bm{q}^{12})Q_{2,2}\\
&\qquad+(\bm{q}^{3}+\bm{q}^{4}+\bm{q}^{5}+2\bm{q}^{6}+2\bm{q}^{7}+2\bm{q}^{8}+\bm{q}^{9}+\bm{q}^{10})Q_{2,1,1}\\
&\qquad+(1+\bm{q}^{2}+\bm{q}^{4})Q_{1,1,1,1}.\end{split}

It is curious that the entries in (
C
) are all positive, whereas the decomposition into
Q_{u}
’s contains a (single) negative term.
